Chapter . Introduction

This whole book idea started as a bet I had with my editor that we couldn’t pick two random techie topics and tie them together in a book. Two darts flew through the air and the topics “Ajax” and “live CDs” ended up being the result. This book could’ve just as easily been about Blu-ray on Rails or iPod design patterns. So it’s just dumb luck that you’re picking up a book on Ajax and live CDs.

And if you believe that, maybe I can interest you in helping fund this startup company I’ve been thinking about launching! The truth is, Ajax and live CDs are surprisingly good bedfellows in that live CDs provide the perfect environment for building and testing Ajax applications. The whole idea of a “live CD” is that it includes a complete operating system on a single CD. Along with a complete operating system, you get to include any applications you want, such as a Web server that can serve up Ajax applications.

One thing that is unique about Ajax applications as compared to purely client-side applications developed solely in JavaScript is that they require something to happen on the server. In other words, the very premise of Ajax is communication between a client and a server. This means you not only need a server, but you need a server that you have a bit of control over. At the very least, you need the ability to quickly install and test server-side scripts that will handle the server-side processing for your Ajax applications. Live CDs fit the bill nicely in terms of giving you an unbelievably customizable platform for building, testing, and hosting Ajax applications.

As cool as live CDs can be, I have to be honest and say that this book is 95% about Ajax. The live CD facet of this book is primarily a convenience due to the fact that Ajax applications are more demanding of their environment than pure client applications.

OK, so the book is primarily about Ajax, but what does that really mean? It means that in a relatively few number of pages you learn how to do the following things with Ajax:

  • Dynamically load e-book chapters for a Web-based book reader (Chapter 3)

  • Instantly select and view live stock quotes (Chapter 4)

  • Turn any Web page into a news aggregator that understands RSS news feeds (Chapter 5)

  • Add auto-complete functionality to your Web site (Chapter 6)

  • Validate form data and look up location data in real time (Chapter 7)

  • Create a “Web 2.0” image viewer to modernize the way your pages handle images (Chapter 8)

  • Use informational popup windows to dynamically display small nuggets of content (Chapter 9)

  • Add a live weather condition popup window to any link (Chapter 10)

  • Calculate shipping charges on the fly with a U.S. Postal Service Ajax request (Chapter 11)

  • Incorporate an animated five-star rating system into your Web pages (Chapter 12)

These are the kinds of dynamic applications that Ajax makes possible. More importantly, this book uses a construction kit approach to guiding you through adding these features to Ajax applications. Although you will ultimately learn a lot about Ajax as you explore the many examples presented throughout this book, the focus of the book is not on teaching you Ajax. The focus is on showing you how to do cool things with Ajax. Sometimes this requires you to learn something entirely new; other times it simply requires cutting and pasting code. The point is that this book is about doing things.

In my opinion, doing is learning, so you’ll ultimately leave this book with a much more thorough understanding of Ajax than when you started. But more importantly, you’ll leave it with your own set of Ajax building blocks that you can take with you and apply to your own projects.

The Audience for This Book

I won’t lie to you—this book by its very nature has to delve into JavaScript and PHP on a regular basis as the various Ajax examples are presented. However, the original goal of the book was for it to be approachable by anyone with knowledge of HTML and CSS (Cascading Style Sheets), and not necessarily a background in scripting, either client (JavaScript) or server (PHP). I’m not entirely sure that was a realistic goal, but it served me well in trying extremely hard to present enough Ajax coverage for the examples to make sense but not so much to scare away scripting newcomers.

All that being said, you absolutely must know a thing or two about HTML to tackle this book. CSS isn’t quite as critical, but it does come up from time to time, so you might as well consider a basic knowledge of CSS a prerequisite. From there, it would serve you well to know something about JavaScript because it is the basis of Ajax. However, I believe you can duck and weave your way through the JavaScript code in this book with a limited knowledge of Web scripting, and still leave with a toolbox full of Ajax goodies that can be readily reused. The PHP code that pops up throughout the book is very simple, and I explain exactly what’s going on, so you can definitely get by without knowing anything about PHP.

The Organization of This Book

Although this book follows the charter of the Negus Live Linux Series to provide hands-on experience quickly, the organization of the book is slightly unconventional. The book is divided into three parts:

  • Part I, “Ajax Boot Camp”—. This part of the book wastes no time laying just enough groundwork so that you aren’t overwhelmed by the intensity of the sample Ajax applications to come. You explore several commercial real-world Ajax applications and get acquainted with the Ajax Toolkit that ships on the accompanying CD-ROM. You’ll leave this part of the book revved up to see Ajax in action.

  • Part II, “Building Real-World Ajax Applications”—. This part of the book, which constitutes the remainder of the book, is focused entirely on using Ajax to solve real-world problems. From dynamically resolving a ZIP code into a city/state for a data entry form, to injecting an interactive five-star rating system into a page, this part of the book wastes no time and takes no prisoners. Don’t get me wrong, it’s still a fun read, but don’t expect a bunch of tutorial talk or an essay on the history of Ajax. This part of the book in particular is all about doing!

  • Part III, “Appendices”—. This part provides additional information you may find helpful. Appendix A, “The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax,” paints a picture of where Ajax came from and where it’s headed. This is in no way critical to understanding how to add Ajax-powered features to your Web pages, but it does add some perspective to the buzz swirling around Ajax these days. Appendix B, “A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference” is exactly that—a reference for the JavaScript object that serves as the core of Ajax. This object is actually hidden for the most part throughout the book thanks to the Ajax Toolkit, but if you ever want to dig deeper into my code and modify the Toolkit itself, you’ll need to get to know the XMLHttpRequest object.

    Finally, Appendix C, “Using the Live Linux CD,” is arguably the handiest of all the appendixes because it explains exactly how to use the live Linux CD that comes with the book. Don’t worry a bit if you’ve never used or even heard of a live CD, and don’t think you need to be a Linux expert or even have Linux installed. Appendix C addresses the role of the live CD and guides you through how to use it for your particular hardware setup.

About the CD-ROM for This Book

The CD-ROM that comes with this book is where live CDs enter into the Ajax picture. The CD includes a bootable Ubuntu (Linux variant) operating system that includes the Apache Web server and all of the sample Ajax applications covered in the book. Perhaps more interesting is the fact that you can run the applications directly from the CD once it boots up, which is pretty powerful considering that you would otherwise be required to have your own Web server up and running.

There are actually a variety of different ways that you can access and use the Ajax sample applications on the live Linux CD. You don’t need to have Linux running on your computer—all you need is a computer with a Pentium III or better processor. The live CD can be run from Windows, Mac OS X, or another Linux variant. To find out more details about how to use the CD to explore the example applications, check out Appendix C, “Using the Live Linux CD.”

What You Need to Use This Book

Live Linux CDs are available to run on a range of computer hardware, such as standard PC architecture (i386), PowerPC (PPC), and 64-bit AMD (AMD64). I recommend a minimum of a Pentium III-class PC and at least 128MB of RAM for running the CD that comes with the book. As for hard-disk requirements, you don’t even need to have a hard disk in the computer to run the live CD. You might consider getting a USB flash drive (also sometimes called a pen drive, thumb drive, or memory stick). Because live CDs are typically run from read-only media, USB flash drives are good to keep handy in the event that an application needs to write data, as is the case in Chapter 12, “Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site.” And again, please refer to Appendix C for detailed instructions on how to use the live CD.

Part I. Ajax Boot Camp

Chapter 1. Ajax and a Brave New World Wide Web

Ajax in Cartoons

Ajax Corporation is a fictional corporation used by Metro-Goldwyn-Mayer (MGM) as a parody of Warner Brothers’ Acme Corporation, which is featured prominently in Road Runner cartoons. The Ajax Corporation is primarily referenced in Tom and Jerry cartoons.

So I’m waiting for my order at a fast food restaurant the other day, and I started to question why the word “fast” had any business in the description of this particular place. I was eager to have a seat and flip through a magazine, not loiter in a crowded lobby waiting for my “fast” food. This situation got me thinking about Ajax and how it improves the overall Web experience. Yes, in an arguably twisted way, it’s possible to look at Ajax through the greasy lens of a hamburger wrapper. And in just a moment you’ll see how.

Although it isn’t strictly considered an acronym, the name Ajax ultimately derives from Asynchronous JavaScript and XML. The key benefits to Ajax are that it allows you to receive small chunks of data over a Web connection asynchronously, meaning you don’t have to wait around for it, and in most cases you can avoid a full Web page reload. The real trick to using Ajax effectively is to get your brain wrapped around what it can do, which is far more important than what it is or why people seem so enamored by it. Ajax is one of those truly rare Web innovations whose benefits are immediately understood once you see it used in the proper context. And sort of like the popular 3M commercials you sometimes see on television, you don’t use Ajax to make things, you use it to make things better. This chapter starts you off with a healthy dose of Ajax realism, with a clear focus on what types of problems it aims to solve and why that matters to you. Yes, I also get around to explaining what it is and why people are enamored by it, but that’s just icing on the cake.

Dining with Ajax

Getting back to the fast food analogy in the opener, the scenario is a crowded fast food restaurant where you have to stand around waiting for your food before you can sit down and get comfortable. Contrast this fast food dining experience with a full-service restaurant where your meal is delivered to your table after you’ve already sat down and ordered. There is a considerable luxury in being seated at a table going about your business while someone is busy working away in the kitchen preparing the meal. In the fast food alternative, you must stand around waiting for your food before you can take a seat and get comfortable, assuming you consider fast food seating comforting. The point I’m getting at is that with the fast food approach, the time it takes to prepare your food is time out of your pocket because your dining experience is on hold until the food is ready. With a sit-down dinner, you’re able to enjoy a drink, munch on an appetizer, and read a magazine, or talk it up with your dining companion while you wait. These are very different experiences, as illustrated in Figure 1.1.

Figure 1.1. The time it takes to prepare food goes relatively unnoticed in a full-service dinner because you are free to enjoy the dining experience.

Other than the fact that I love eating, especially eating out, the point of this discussion is that Ajax is a Web development technique that offers to upgrade your Web pages to a sit-down dining experience. Traditional Web applications force you to wait while data is searched or otherwise processed on the server. Not so with Ajax. An Ajax Web application goes off and performs its data processing in the background, leaving you to continue to interact with the Web page in the meantime. This may seem like a subtle distinction, but it is not. Just ask yourself, would you rather stand in line at a not-so-fast-food restaurant or relax at a cozy table in a sit-down restaurant?

Note

One small problem with my restaurant analogy for Ajax is that it implies that Ajax Web applications are somehow slower than traditional Web applications. This is not the case. Ajax applications are no faster or slower in terms of raw data processing. In fact, they often give the impression of being faster because processing is typically going on in the background when you don’t even realize it.

Ajax, Sting, and Asynchronicity

In 1983 the Police released the album Synchronicity, and it went on to sell millions of copies and launch major hits such as “Every Breath You Take.” I bet Sting never realized that a few decades later his album title would be the antagonist in numerous tech books and articles. As it turns out, synchronicity is the bane of Web user interfaces, as it implies that one thing must finish before another can start. In a synchronous Web page, you cannot start Task B until Task A has completely finished, even if Task A takes a while. For example, a synchronous online music service wouldn’t allow you to continue browsing for music while it’s busy serving up a song for download.

Note

One reason Sting has probably never made the connection between the album Synchronicity and the tech world is because the album actually refers to a psychological principle developed by Swiss psychologist Carl Jung. In this context, Synchronicity refers to a “meaningful coincidence,” as in when two seemingly unrelated things take place that have more of a connection than mere coincidence. I apologize if I’m better at explaining techie computer topics than I am psychology principles.

Another example more to the point with Ajax is the shipping cost in an online shopping cart, which is often a critical part of a shopping transaction and in some cases can determine whether an item is worth purchasing. Take this from someone who has bought an automobile online—shipping charges can make or break an online purchase. The problem is that many shopping carts are designed to get all of your information, in many cases including your credit card number, before calculating and revealing the shipping charge. I don’t know about you, but if I haven’t yet committed to a purchase, there’s no way I’m going to enter my credit card number.

Even for shopping carts that are designed to calculate shipping early in the checkout process, you still have the issue of a server request/response, which can take time and will ultimately result in a page reload in order to reveal the shipping charge on the page. While this server request is taking place, the order-entry user interface is effectively frozen, which is not a good thing. It would make so much more sense if the shipping charge was seamlessly calculated and displayed as you enter the shipping address. Since the item being purchased is already in the cart, all the shopping cart application needs is a ZIP code to fire off a server request and get busy calculating the charges. This action can take place in the background while you’re filling out the rest of the order information. Similarly to the dining example, the wait involved in calculating the shipping charge is all but eliminated because you’re allowed to do something else useful while it is happening.

To Reload, or Not Reload

As I alluded to already, a frozen user interface is only part of the problem with traditional Web applications. The other side of the equation is the fact that a page must be reloaded for every server response. More specifically, when the server sends the shipping charge back from the server, the page must be reloaded to reflect the change because the server is responding with an entire Web page. Every change to a page that is based upon a server response requires a page refresh. This is a terribly annoying problem not only because page reloads/refreshes take time, but also because the page effectively flickers and blinks with each change.

Note

There is wide debate over the notion of applying version numbers to the Web as a whole. It’s very difficult to pigeonhole such a divergent beast into a numbering system, no more than you would label a highway system Autobahn 3.0. However, the general idea that there are discrete, evolutionary steps in the design of Web applications is absolutely valid. So maybe the highway analogy isn’t so far off after all—think of traditional Web applications as dirt roads, whereas Ajax-powered Web applications are asphalt interstate highways.

There is also a significant efficiency issue in that the entire contents of the page have to be resent with each page reload, even if only a tiny piece of data (a shipping charge, for example) is being updated. This is a waste of bandwidth and a waste of the user’s time. This type of Web application is what has generally become known as a Web 1.0 application. Ajax is playing a major role in ushering in a new generation of applications that fall under the Web 2.0 moniker, which “feel” more like true desktop applications.

Ajax solves the page-reload problem by reducing server requests and responses to only the exact data you need, as opposed to an entire page. In the case of the shipping charge request, the server just sends back a dollar amount, in which case the page updates only the shipping charge area of the page with server data, and then calculates a new total for the purchase. The entire page is not resent, no reload is necessary, and no flicker occurs. Figure 1.2 illustrates how Ajax improves the shipping charge calculation process.

Figure 1.2. Ajax makes it possible to calculate a shipping charge without interrupting the user experience.

What Exactly Is Ajax?

I’ve resisted the urge thus far to get overly specific about what Ajax is because it is much more important for you to understand what it does. To say that Ajax is a programming technique that involves the combination of JavaScript and XML is pointless if you don’t understand the kinds of problems it solves. Hopefully enough groundwork has been laid that you now have a basic grasp of the problems Ajax addresses.

So then, what is Ajax? Ajax is a specific Web development approach that involves the usage of JavaScript and quite often XML. That’s it. It’s not a fancy browser plug-in or hip new programming language. It’s just a programming technique involving technologies that have already been around a while.

The real muscle behind Ajax is a single JavaScript object that makes it possible to communicate with a Web server asynchronously. In traditional Web applications, the browser itself typically presents the only means of communicating with a server, and more importantly, it does so a Web page at a time. Ajax introduces the notion of using JavaScript to communicate with a server. Behind the scenes it’s still the browser doing the work, but the net effect is that your JavaScript code is able to carry on a conversation with a Web server to send and receive discrete pieces of data. And this communication takes place asynchronously, which means it occurs in the background without interrupting anything else.

For now, it’s not terribly important for you to know any of the specifics regarding the JavaScript object that makes Ajax possible. The next chapter gets into more detail concerning the nuts and bolts of Ajax and how to assemble Ajax code.

Note

Keep in mind that the whole point of this book is to provide you with a plug-and-play Ajax framework that minimizes code development. You’ll be able to reuse the code in this book with a minimal knowledge of the Ajax code and how it works. Not only that, but you can use the enclosed live Linux CD to experiment with many of the examples directly from your PC.

Real-World Ajax

This book is inherently noninteractive, and I can prove it. Click here with your finger and you’ll see what I mean. See, no new page appeared. Knowing that the printed page is rather static, I encourage you to take a few moments to tinker with some real Ajax applications online to get a feel for how they compare with traditional Web applications. The next few sections present some of the more popular and compelling Ajax applications, and help direct you toward their specific features that benefit the most from Ajax:

  • Google Suggest

  • Netflix

  • Flickr

  • Google Maps

Since you probably already use some of these Web applications, it will be even more insightful as you find out how Ajax makes specific features possible. After this little real-world tour, you’ll revisit the shipping charge calculator example in the context of a real Ajax application that is provided on the enclosed live Linux CD.

Google Suggest

Google Suggest (http://labs.google.com/suggest/) is a great example of a relatively simple Ajax application that is sneakily powerful. It turns out that “sneakily powerful” is something you may be thinking to yourself a lot as you explore Ajax and its many possibilities. Google Suggest is the regular Google search engine but with a suggestive text-entry feature that attempts to help you narrow down your search based upon actual Google search data.

As you type in a search phrase in Google Suggest, a dropdown list immediately appears with possible matches for the phrase. To accept one of the suggested phrases, just click it with your mouse, or cursor down and select it. What makes this Ajax application so cool is how each possible match has the number of matches listed out to the side of it. In other words, you are provided with an immediate clue as to the popularity of a particular search phrase.

As you experiment with Google Suggest, the key thing to note from a development perspective is how it is seamlessly making a round-trip to the server with each character you enter. That’s pretty amazing when you think about it. Talk about a responsive user interface! This is exactly the kind of “feel” that Ajax is intended to impart on modern Web applications. Chapter 6, “Reading the User’s Mind with Auto-Complete,” revisits Google Suggest as it shows you how to employ your own “auto-complete” feature using Ajax. But don’t wait until Chapter 6, go ahead and try out Google Suggest right now.

Netflix

Chances are you’ve already been basking in the comfortable glow of Ajax without even knowing it. If you use Netflix (http://www.netflix.com/) for movie rentals, then you’re already somewhat of an Ajax veteran, at least from the user side of things. Netflix uses Ajax to carry out a popular trick where a popup window automatically appears when you hover over a piece of information (a movie icon in this case) with the mouse pointer.

If you simply hover over the small icon of a movie, a popup window appears with a complete movie description and related movie information. All this movie data is retrieved from the server via Ajax in the background as the hover begins. From the user’s perspective, this is all gravy because you don’t even realize a server communication is taking place until it is over (the popup window appears). Again, Ajax adds a significant usability feature in an entirely seamless manner.

One interesting thing to note about using Ajax for popup windows is how the Netflix popup disappears automatically once you move the mouse pointer off of the movie in question. This helps to improve the perception that the popup is purely informative and not an annoying advertising popup that you have to click to get rid of—in the case of Netflix, the popups truly are a welcome addition to the user interface. You can experience Ajax on Netflix even if you don’t have a Netflix account—just go to http://www.netflix.com/ and click Browse Selection to find some movies to hover around. Incidentally, you learn how to inject informational popup windows into your own pages in Chapter 9, “Presenting Information with Slick Popups.”

Note

For another interesting usage of Ajax for displaying popup windows, check out the Invisible Ad advertisement service at http://www.theinvisiblead.com/. It is a very unconventional advertising concept involving a huge grid of tiny squares that you must hover over with your mouse in order to view ads. This type of advertising is known as pixel ads because you literally pay for each pixel of ad space on the screen. Groundbreaking? Silly? You be the judge!

Flickr

Without a doubt my favorite commercial Ajax application thus far, Flickr (http://www.flickr.com/) is Ajax-powered to the max. If you aren’t familiar with it, Flickr is an online photo-sharing community. What makes Flickr so unusual is how thoroughly it uses Ajax to give it the feel of a desktop application. There are very few tasks within Flickr that require a page reload or that make you sit around twiddling your thumbs during a server request/response. Figure 1.3 shows how intuitive the editing of a photo caption is in Flickr thanks to a heavy dose of Ajax.

Figure 1.3. Editing a Flickr photo caption occurs “in place” and without any page refreshes, even when saving the modified caption.

The figure shows a picture in my photo collection with its caption being edited. It’s important to understand that the edit field and its associated Save and Cancel buttons appeared automatically when I clicked the photo’s caption. After changing the caption, you simply click the Save button and the changes are committed to the server immediately. No waiting around for a new page to open, no confirmation dialog box, nothing. The caption just changes from edit mode back to a static piece of text. Like I said earlier, “sneakily powerful” comes to mind when you think about how cleanly the caption-editing task is made thanks to Ajax.

Flickr doesn’t stop with photo caption editing when it comes to its use of Ajax. There are all kinds of clever ways that Ajax permeates the Flickr user interface. I could spend a few more pages spoon-feeding them to you, or I could just turn you loose to try them out on your own. You’ll need to create a free Flickr account to truly experience the user interface, but that only takes a few simple steps. You can start out at http://www.flickr.com/.

Google Maps

I have to admit that it pained me a little to crown Flickr my favorite commercial Ajax application because there is another that is dear to my heart. I’m talking about Google Maps (http://maps.google.com/), which in many ways is the godfather of Ajax applications. It was only a few short years ago that Web developers across the globe were scratching their heads over how Google Maps could possibly be doing what it was doing, and with such speed. As it turns out, the trick up the sleeve of Google Maps was and still is Ajax. In my mind, Google Maps is still the ultimate example of processing data in the background to improve the user experience.

The primary trick employed by Google Maps is constantly anticipating and loading map data before it is needed. In other words, Google Maps isn’t concerned so much about what part of the map you are currently viewing as it is what part of the map you’re going to be viewing next. Safe guesses include the areas around the currently viewed area, as well as zoomed-in and zoomed-out versions of the current area. It turns out that Google Maps generally outworks the user by loading data faster than the user is able to process it. So by the time you get around to exploring a different part of the map, Google Maps is already ahead of you and has preloaded the map data. The net effect is a shockingly responsive user interface that would be impossible without the assistance of Ajax in loading map data in the background.

Ajax also enters the picture with Google Maps when it comes to customizing a map with your own information. Google publishes a special API (Application Programming Interface) for interacting with Google Maps, and it is based entirely upon Ajax. For example, you can create your own custom markers (push pins) to place on map locations and even tie them to custom popup windows that display additional information about the locations.

Ajax Isn’t for Everything

I’m guessing the little tour of real-world Ajax applications has gotten you excited about injecting a little Ajax into your own Web pages. That’s a very good thing. However, I must caution you that Ajax is not a one-size-fits-all solution. Like most things in life, a little moderation can go a long way toward getting the most benefit out of Ajax.

Earlier in the chapter you found out about the main problems that Ajax solves. Working back from these problems, it’s possible to come up with a list of scenarios where Ajax makes a lot of sense. By analyzing this scenario list, it’s also possible to determine when Ajax isn’t an ideal solution. Use this list as a basis for trying to determine whether Ajax truly stands to help your Web pages. Understand that the list isn’t all encompassing, but is intended more as a general guide for assessing the practical usefulness of Ajax. Here goes:

  • First and foremost, Ajax always involves server communication, or more specifically, data flowing from the server to the client Web browser. If your Web page does not hit a Web server for data of some sort, there’s no point in using Ajax. You can certainly still use JavaScript to make the page more interactive, but Ajax isn’t strictly necessary.

  • One of the key points of Ajax is to update a page without requiring a refresh/reload. If your application inherently requires a new page to be opened, such as a link to a new page in a series of discrete pages, then Ajax is probably not the answer. On the other hand, maybe you can merge several pages into a single Ajax-powered page that doesn’t need reloading.

    Note

    Some people mistakenly refer to any highly responsive Web user interface as an “Ajax user interface.” Strictly speaking, such an interface must involve server communication in order to qualify as an Ajax solution, at least in my humble opinion. That’s not to say that responsive user interfaces aren’t closely tied to Ajax, because they certainly are. But the fact remains that Ajax is about getting data from a server asynchronously. I’m not trying to split hairs semantically in regard to what is and isn’t Ajax; I’m just trying to help you know when it makes sense to use Ajax.

  • Another key point of Ajax is asynchronous communications, which is how Ajax shuttles data in the background so that other stuff can take place in the foreground. If your application specifically needs one thing to happen before another, then Ajax probably isn’t a good idea. Maybe a certain block of user entry data must be validated before going any further, in which case you probably really do want to open a new page or make the user wait while the data is verified. In either case, Ajax doesn’t really help.

The point I’m making here is that Ajax is incredibly powerful when used properly, but knowing when to use it is important. The three preceding points should serve as guides for assessing whether or not it makes sense to inject Ajax into your application. Additionally, the examples you explore throughout the remainder of the book will shed additional light on when and how Ajax should find its way into your Web pages.

The Shipping Charge Calculator Revisited

Earlier in the chapter I made a big deal about how annoying it is in an online shopping cart to find out the shipping charge only by first divulging financial information or stepping through a sequence of Web pages. I made a case for how Ajax could dramatically improve the situation by obtaining the shipping charge from a server in the background once you enter the ZIP code for where the order is being sent. You would then be free to continue entering order information instead of waiting for the server or being interrupted with a page reload. Figure 1.4 shows what such a shopping cart might look like prior to entering the ZIP code for shipping.

Figure 1.4. Part of the check-out process for any online shopping cart involves gathering the shipping information and displaying a total.

In this example, the check-out process has already progressed to the point of the purchase item being selected; all that remains is getting the shipping information, calculating a total, and completing the payment. This particular example stops short of completing the payment, instead focusing on streamlining the retrieval of the shipping charge and calculating a check-out total based on those charges.

With Ajax now in the mix, it becomes possible to get the shipping charge directly from a shipping provider’s server (in this case the U.S. Postal Service) as soon as the ZIP code is entered. Figure 1.5 shows the order entry page with the ZIP code partially entered. Notice at this point that the shipping charge has yet to be retrieved because I’m still entering the ZIP code.

Figure 1.5. Nothing happens while the ZIP code is being entered because you need the entire ZIP code to request the shipping charge.

Note

The USPS Web Tools API, which is responsible for allowing you to retrieve a shipping charge via Ajax, also supports the verification of shipping information. For example, you could take order entry to another level entirely by using the USPS server to find out the city and state from a ZIP code, thereby eliminating two pieces of user-entered information. You learn more about the USPS Web Tools API in Chapter 11, “Building an Ajax Shipping Calculator,” when we dissect the shipping rate calculator example in a bit more detail within the context of a real Ajax application.

Once the ZIP code is completely entered and I move to the next field in the user interface (Phone Number), the shipping charge magically appears and the check-out total is calculated (see Figure 1.6). This shipping charge was obtained behind-the-scenes directly from a U.S. Postal Service server and then reflected on the page without any noticeable delay or page interruption.

Figure 1.6. The shipping charge is retrieved and the check-out total is calculated in the background without distraction.

Even if the USPS server had taken its sweet time returning the shipping charge, the user experience wouldn’t have suffered much because the user is free to continue entering information. Additionally, any changes to the ZIP code result in a new server request and a new shipping charge. Not only that, but the specific USPS shipping service (Parcel Post, Priority Mail, etc.) can be changed, which also results in a new shipping charge being retrieved.

That last little trick in this Ajax example is revealed when you enter a ZIP code that isn’t supported. Maybe the specific item can’t be shipped to a certain region—or maybe, as in this example, you limit the ZIP code for testing purposes. Regardless of the motivation, if the ZIP code doesn’t result in a valid shipping charge, an error notice is displayed next to the ZIP code field, as shown in Figure 1.7.

Figure 1.7. An error message appears next to the ZIP code field if you enter a ZIP code that isn’t accepted.

Chapter 11 explores the inner workings of this example in more detail. For now, just know that the example is limited to only working for the ZIP code 20008, meaning that any other ZIP codes will result in an error. This limitation has to do with using the USPS Web Tools API in “test mode,” not the example code itself.

Summary

The last thing I wanted to do in this chapter was give you a long-winded summary of how Ajax came to be and who made it possible. I realize your motivation in buying this book is to do some cool stuff with what is without question one of the coolest programming techniques to hit the Web in quite some time. The goal of this chapter was to get you acclimated with what Ajax can do and why it is relevant to both your current applications and the future of the Web.

Anyone developing applications for the Web should be thinking about how to make their applications better: more interactive, more intuitive, and less like the Web of old. We are standing at the edge of an exciting new Web that has the potential to erase the line between Web app and desktop app. When you think about it, why should that distinction matter anyway? An application is just a way to do something useful or fun with a computer, and Ajax is a tool that can make many online applications dramatically better. The rest of this book shows you exactly how to make your applications better with Ajax.

Chapter 2. Inside an Ajax Application

Ajax on the High Seas

Several ships in the United States Navy have taken the name Ajax over the years, with the first USS Ajax launched in 1864. The most recent USS Ajax was a repair ship that saw duty in both World War II and the Vietnam War, and was ultimately decommissioned in 1986.

If it’s one thing I hate, it’s computer books that spend the first five chapters laying the groundwork for everything to come. Too many books feel like they have to teach you a little bit about everything instead of focusing on the one topic at hand. For this reason, you start off this chapter with just a bit more setup and then dive right into the guts of Ajax. If you think you need to brush up a bit on HTML and CSS, or maybe you think a quick JavaScript or XML tutorial might be helpful, then by all means take a break and brush up on them. Just don’t hold your breath for me to waste your afternoon holding your hand through those topics when we’ve got Ajax business to take care of.

I’m not trying to scare you away; I’m just trying to get you fired up to jump into the cool stuff that you bought this book to learn how to do. By the end of this chapter, you’ll have completed your first fully functioning Ajax application. Not only that, but you’ll also have a pretty good sense of how to customize that application to do things it was probably never even intended to do. That’s a good thing. Improvise, invent; that’s the spirit here. Now let’s get started!

The following files are used by the Booker application in this chapter and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap02 example code folder:

  • booker.html—Main Web page

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • book_alicewonderland.xml—Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland XML e-book

  • book_frankenstein.xml—Frankenstein XML e-book

  • book_mobydick.xml—Moby Dick XML e-book

  • book_warandpeace.xml—War and Peace XML e-book

  • book_warofworlds—War of the Worlds XML e-book

The Nuts and Bolts of Ajax

You probably now have a decent feel for the fact that Ajax isn’t so much a single technology as it is a specific application of other technologies. Understanding how these other technologies fit together within the context of an Ajax application is important in understanding how to build and modify your own Ajax applications. So let’s explore the component technologies that make Ajax work. Figure 2.1 shows how the major Ajax technologies fit together in a typical Ajax application.

Figure 2.1. HTML, CSS, JavaScript, XML, and a server program such as PHP all work together to coordinate an Ajax request and response.

Notice in the figure that the Ajax request/response process unfolds in an orderly fashion, as is evident by the numbers 1 through 3. Keep this figure in mind as you learn more about how the different Ajax components fit together in the next few sections.

HTML and CSS

It would be difficult to create any Web page without a little HTML since it is the language of the Web. In Ajax applications, HTML still serves as the structural portion of content on the page, such as text, images, form fields, etc. In fact, most of the HTML code in Ajax applications is nothing special—the interesting stuff takes place primarily in JavaScript code, which I get to in a moment.

If you’ve done any Web page creation in the past few years, you likely already have an appreciation of Cascading Style Sheets (CSS) and how important they are to creating visually attractive Web pages. CSS allows you to cleanly separate the appearance of a Web page from its structure, with the end result being pages that are more easily maintained, not to mention prettier. CSS enters the Ajax picture in that it provides you with a means of applying styles to change the look and feel of HTML content dynamically. For example, you might want to highlight an invalid data entry field upon validating the data against a database through an Ajax request. The highlighting in this example would be carried out on an HTML form element via CSS.

JavaScript

JavaScript’s job in Ajax is to orchestrate everything on the client side of the equation, which includes any and all logic that must be carried out by the browser. Don’t forget that the very essence of Ajax is an exchange of data between a client (browser) and a server (Web server), so you’ll always be thinking in terms of client/server communication when designing and building Ajax applications. JavaScript code runs on the browser, and is used to initiate Ajax requests and handle the resulting data returned by the server.

You use JavaScript to package up user data for sending to the server, as well as receiving the server’s response and presenting it back to the user in a meaningful way. Of course, this means that HTML and CSS factor heavily into the JavaScript code for an Ajax application, with JavaScript often utilizing the Document Object Model (DOM) to dynamically alter HTML elements based upon Ajax responses. The dashed lines in Figure 2.1 indicate JavaScript’s ability to alter HTML and CSS code dynamically using the DOM.

Note

You aren’t expected to understand how to program in JavaScript to be able to reuse the examples in this book in your own Web pages. However, it is impossible to explore Ajax at any depth without digging into JavaScript code, so a cursory understanding of JavaScript is important if you plan on taking any of the sample code to another level.

XML and Ajax Data

OK, so you know that an Ajax Web page is still made up of HTML and CSS, and that it can be dynamically tinkered with using crafty JavaScript code that manages Ajax requests and responses. I still haven’t explained the nature of the data that is sent between the client and server in an Ajax request/response sequence. And part of the reason is because the format of data in an Ajax transaction is not set in stone. You have the freedom of using several data formats to suit the specific needs of each application. In other words, you can carefully tailor the representation of data to each Ajax application.

One of the most compelling reasons to use Ajax is to take advantage of the XML data standard, which provides a highly structured means of organizing information. XML is particularly adept at representing complex data that would otherwise be difficult to represent, such as a hierarchical database with several levels of nested data. Ajax fully supports XML on either side of the request/response equation, meaning you can send and/or receive data in XML.

While XML is a very good thing, it is not the magical solution to every Ajax problem. In fact, it is perfectly acceptable, and in many cases desirable, to use raw text data as the format for Ajax data exchange. For example, it is very concise to pass data along to the server as simple name/value pairs like you often see in URLs, as in

fname=Michael&lname=Morrison

Compare this to the same information structured in XML:

<fname>Michael</fname>
<lname>Morrison</lname>

The URL name/value version of the data is much more effective in this case because the data is so simple.

The bottom line is that the format of data shuttling around in an Ajax application is entirely dependent on the specific needs of the application. Throughout the book you’ll encounter applications that use several different data types, including raw text, XML, and another type known as JSON (JavaScript Object Notation).

PHP and the Server

One facet of Ajax that is often misunderstood by newcomers is the fact that it is entirely dependent on a server program accepting requests and responding in some way. The server program is the hidden ingredient in the familiar Asynchronous JavaScript + XML recipe that you typically see associated with the term Ajax. On a practical level, this means that you can’t just assemble a Web page with some JavaScript code and crank out an Ajax application—there has to be a server-side component that responds to requests from the client.

The role of the server program in an Ajax application is entirely specific to each Ajax application, but in general you will find that most Ajax server programs are responsible for looking up some piece of information and returning it to the client. For example, an Ajax employee directory application might pass an employee ID to the server, in which case the server program looks up the ID in a database and returns the employee’s name and contact information. Or, as in the example you pondered in the previous chapter, information about an item to be shipped might be passed to the server, after which the server looks up the shipping rate and returns it.

Unlike on the client side of an Ajax application, which is exclusively the domain of JavaScript, the server side is wide open in terms of what programming language is used to create the server program. Fortunately, there are some popular options that are widely supported and relatively easy to modify and use. One of these options is PHP, which is a very popular server scripting language that is handy in creating Ajax applications. Similar to JavaScript code that is run on a browser, PHP code is run on a server. Referring back to Figure 2.1 once more, you can see how a PHP program on the server receives an Ajax request from the browser (thanks to JavaScript), and then responds after processing the request.

The interesting thing about PHP code, and all server-side scripts for that matter, is that they ultimately end up passing data back to the client. When working with PHP, you have to always keep in mind that whatever you’re doing will culminate in data being shuttled back to the browser. In the case of an Ajax application, a PHP script is shuttling data back to an Ajax request.

The Ajax Request Lifecycle

Every Ajax request goes through a predictable lifecycle that is important to understand before you begin looking under the hood of Ajax applications. Each milestone in the Ajax lifecycle corresponds to a distinct state within the XMLHttpRequest object, which is the JavaScript object that serves as the heart of Ajax. The Ajax toolkit that you learn about in a moment shields you from the vast majority of the inner workings of the XMLHttpRequest object, but it’s still worth getting acquainted with some facets of how it works. For a full dissection of the object, check out Appendix B, “A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference.”

Following are the five states that an Ajax request cycles through as it goes from creation to submission to successful response:

  1. UNINITIALIZED—The request is uninitialized.

  2. LOADING—The request is initialized.

  3. LOADED—The request has been sent and is waiting for server acknowledgement.

  4. INTERACTIVE—The response data is being downloaded from the server.

  5. COMPLETE—The request/response cycle is complete.

The capitalized names for each state in the lifecycle correspond directly to internal states of the XMLHttpRequest object. The good news about this lifecycle is that you typically only concern yourself with the last state, COMPLETE, which makes sense given that you don’t need to take any action until the request is finished and its resulting data is available. However, it’s often helpful from a diagnostic perspective to peek into a transaction and make sure it is progressing as expected through the lifecycle. For this reason, the Ajax toolkit provided on the CD-ROM with this book includes a diagnostic feature that visually shows each state of an Ajax request as it takes place.

Note

Given the nature of the Booker sample application, you might think that this is a perfect opportunity for a synchronous application since there isn’t much to do but wait around for the book to load. But what if you change your mind while the book is being downloaded? It would be much better if the user interface didn’t lock you out of another selection while the loading is taking place. By using Ajax, you are perfectly free to choose another book while one book is loading. In this case, the current Ajax request is cancelled and the second book immediately begins loading.

Figure 2.2 shows an Ajax sample application called Booker as it is in the midst of carrying out a request. The Booker sample application is a simple e-book viewer.

Figure 2.2. A diagnostic bar along the top of the page shows the status of the Ajax request in the book viewer example.

In this example, the entire text of the book War of the Worlds, by H. G. Wells, is being loaded and displayed via an Ajax request. Since the book is several hundred kilobytes in size, it takes a moment for the request to complete, depending upon connection speed of course. Figure 2.2 shows the application in the INTERACTIVE (“Downloading response...”) state, which means the server response (the book text) is being downloaded. Notice that the area where the book text is to appear contains the message “Loading...” to indicate that there is a reason why nothing appears just yet. A few moments later, the request state changes to COMPLETE (“Request complete!”) and the book text appears on the main part of the page (see Figure 2.3).

Figure 2.3. The complete text of the e-book appears once the request completes.

Note

All of the books used in this example are public domain books that have been made available electronically by Project Gutenberg, a producer of free electronic books. To learn more, visit Project Gutenberg online at http://www.gutenberg.org/.

You might have noticed an e-book selection list near the top of the page just beneath the Ajax diagnostic bar. Not only does this allow you to access different books, it also serves as a good way to watch as Ajax requests of varying lengths take place. To test out what I’m talking about, open the booker.html page in the chap02 folder on the accompanying live Linux CD. The initial book loaded in the viewer is Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland, by Lewis Carroll. Click the selection list and select War and Peace. Widely regarded as one of the heftiest novels ever written, the raw text for War and Peace by Leo Tolstoy weighs in at around 3MB. Even with the relative quickness of performing an Ajax request directly from a CD-ROM, you will notice the state change in the Ajax diagnostic bar.

Note

As you progress through the book, you’ll have the option of turning the Ajax diagnostic bar on and off in each application based upon whether or not you care to monitor the request status.

Getting to Know the Ajax Toolkit

I have now made several references to the Ajax toolkit that is supplied on the CD-ROM with this book—now it’s time to learn exactly what it is and how it works. As you will eventually find out if you do much of your own Ajax development, there are literally dozens if not hundreds of Ajax libraries out there designed to make using Ajax easier and more efficient. And many of these libraries live up to their goals. Problem is, many of them have about as much learning curve as Ajax itself, which is not a good thing unless you’re building a monstrous Ajax application with lots of bells and whistles. And in that case, you may end up wanting to build your own Ajax library anyway.

To keep things as simple and straightforward as possible, I opted to build my own custom Ajax library for use in this book, and it should serve as your starting point for incorporating Ajax features into your Web pages. In many cases, you can reuse my code verbatim to get instant results. Beyond that, you may want to brush up on JavaScript and XML to take my code to another level. Either way, my Ajax toolkit serves as a handy jumpstart to Ajax Web development that has a very low learning curve.

My handy-dandy Ajax toolkit consists of several JavaScript files, each of which contains JavaScript functions and related helper code:

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax

  • domkit.js—DOM (Document Object Model)

  • modalimgkit.js—Modal images

  • validatekit.js—Data validation

  • infowinkit.js—Information windows

  • ratingkit.js—Five-star ratings

By “helper code,” I mean that these files offer pre-built code that is intended to make certain tasks dramatically easier. Not all of these files are included in every Ajax-powered Web page that you develop—you use them on an as-needed basis. As an example, following is the code used to import the first two files into a page, which must be placed in the <head> of the page:

<script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>

Once these lines of code have been added to your page, you are free to use any of the features supplied by the files within the Ajax toolkit.

The next few sections explore the different parts of the Ajax toolkit in more detail. Although I quickly explain all of the different JavaScript functions within each toolkit file, it isn’t critical that you commit them to memory or even understand fully what their role is. The goal for now is just to let you know generally what each part of the toolkit offers—the remainder of the book revisits the toolkit in more detail when each piece of the puzzle comes into play.

The ajaxkit.js File

The ajaxkit.js portion of the toolkit consists solely of two JavaScript functions that handle submitting an Ajax request and updating the Ajax diagnostic bar, respectively:

ajaxSendRequest(type, url, handler, postDataType, postData)
ajaxUpdateState()

If you aren’t familiar with JavaScript functions, just know that they are reusable chunks of code that carry out a specific task. In this case, the ajaxSendRequest() function sends an Ajax request to the server based upon information supplied in its five parameters. Again, it’s not critical that you understand what function parameters are just yet—you’ll get to know them as you see how the ajaxSendRequest() function is used in actual sample applications. For now, just know that the five parameters correspond to the type of Ajax request (GET or POST), the address of the server program that is processing the request, the custom function you provide that is handling the server response, and optional data type and data if the request is a POST.

Note

The type of Ajax request (GET or POST) flows directly from the fact that an Ajax request is really just an HTTP request. If you’ve ever worked with forms in HTML, you should have some familiarity with GET and POST requests. In general, the idea is that you use a GET request if you’re just getting information from the server, whereas POST is intended more for passing information to the server.

Just to show that the ajaxSendRequest() function isn’t too terribly difficult to use, here’s the exact Ajax request used in the Booker e-book viewer example:

ajaxSendRequest("GET", "book_" + title + ".txt", handleRequest);

Even without getting deep into JavaScript, it isn’t too terribly difficult to see that the first parameter indicates that this is a GET request, the second parameter is assembling a filename for the book, as in book_alicewonderland.txt, and the last parameter is the name of a custom handler function. You’ll come back to this code a bit later in the chapter.

The ajaxUpdateState() function is used to update the Ajax diagnostic bar that you saw in Figures 2.2 and 2.3. You’ll end up making a call to this function in the custom handler function that you create to respond to requests, like this:

ajaxUpdateState();

Making a call to a JavaScript function simply means that you’re telling the function to do whatever task it was designed to do. A handler function is simply a function that is called in response to something happening. For example, a mouse click event handler function gets called whenever a mouse click occurs. Similarly, every Ajax application has a custom request handler function that gets called whenever the state of the request changes. That’s why it was so important for you to learn about the request states a bit earlier in the chapter. You revisit request states and find out exactly how they fit into the Ajax toolkit equation in the section “Dismantling the E-Book Viewer.”

The domkit.js File

Getting back to the pieces and parts of the Ajax toolkit, the domkit.js file contains several functions that carry out different DOM tasks. For example, the replaceText() function is responsible for changing the text of an element on the page:

replaceText(elem, text)

If you’ve done any JavaScript programming before, you may be familiar with the innerHTML property of most HTML elements, which is used to change the value of an element on the page. The usage of this property is considered passé by modern Web standards, and you’re now encouraged to use newer features of the DOM to carry out a change in an element’s contents in most cases. However, this newer approach is not as simple as setting a single property. Rather than have you reproduce line after line of unnecessary code, I opted to create a function called replaceText() that does the content replacement for you. You hand it the element and the new contents for it, and the replaceText() function does the rest. As an example, the following line of code changes the contents of the element stored in firstName to "Michael":

replaceText(firstName, "Michael");

You’ll see this function used quite a lot throughout the examples in the book. As long as you import the domkit.js file into your pages, you are free to use the function as well. Don’t worry, you see exactly how to import the file and use the replaceText() function when the examples roll around, so it’s not critical to understand it fully at this stage.

Similar to replaceText() is the getText() function, which uses the DOM to extract the contents of an HTML element:

getText(elem);

You give this function an HTML element and it returns to you the contents of the element.

The domkit.js file also contains four functions for determining the size of the client area of the browser window, which can be very handy when carefully placing elements on the page:

getClientWidth()
getClientHeight()
getClientLeft()
getClientTop()

These functions return the width, height, left position, and top position of the browser’s client area, which is the area where a page actually resides. In other words, it doesn’t include the various toolbars, status bars, and other gadgets that surround a page.

The last function in the domkit.js file is used to obtain the data associated with a cookie that has been stored by the browser:

getCookieData(cookieName)

This function accepts the name of the cookie in question and then returns to you the cookie value. Cookies are a handy way to remember something the user has done on a page, and they come into play in this book in Chapter 12, “Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site,” when you build a five-star rating application.

The modalimgkit.js File

The modalimgkit.js part of the toolkit consists of a single JavaScript function that adds modal image support to a Web page:

initModal()

You learn a great deal more about modal images in Chapter 8, “A Killer Interface for Image Viewing,” but the general idea is to allow you to view a full-sized image from a thumbnail without having to load a new page into the browser. Similar to an About dialog box that might appear over an application window, a modal image pops up and appears over the current Web page.

The validatekit.js File

The validatekit.js portion of the toolkit offers several valuable functions for validating different types of user input:

validateNonEmpty(inputControl, helpControl)
validateInteger(inputControl, helpControl)
validateNumber(inputControl, helpControl)
validateZipCode(inputControl, helpControl)
validatePhone(inputControl, helpControl)
validateEmail(inputControl, helpControl)
validateDate(inputControl, helpControl)

These functions are all geared toward validating a specific data type, and most common data types you would encounter in a form are addressed. The two parameters to the functions are the input control to be validated and an output element where a help message is displayed when a validation problem is detected.

The infowinkit.js File

The infowinkit.js part of the toolkit contains functions used to add an Ajax-powered information popup window to a page:

initInfoWindow(width, height) {
showInfoWindow(infoURL, evt)

The first function is called initially to set the width and height of the information window, while the second function actually displays an information window with content from the specified URL.

The ratingkit.js File

The ratingkit.js portion of the toolkit implements a five-star rating system that can be added to any site, including adding rating support for multiple items:

initRatings()
setRating(id, rating)

The first function is used to initialize the rating system, while the second function sets the rating for a specific item. Each item within the rating system is assigned a unique ID, which is the first thing passed into the setRating() function. The second piece of information is the rating itself, which is in the range 1 to 5.

Dismantling the E-Book Viewer

It’s time to quit playing around in the hypothetical world of Ajax theory and get into something real. The remainder of this chapter explores the inner workings of the Booker e-book viewer that you saw back in Figures 2.2 and 2.3, and explains exactly how it works. You can think of this section as a mini version of what each upcoming chapter is—a dissection of a real-world Ajax application that can be readily reused for your own purposes. Although I can’t guarantee you’ll understand every line of code without some JavaScript experience, I can definitely point you to the relevant places where you can make educated changes to customize the code to suit your own needs. In other words, I won’t necessarily teach you how to be a mechanic, but I will tell you where to turn the wrench.

Earlier in this chapter I explained how every Ajax application ultimately boils down to two parts—a JavaScript program on the client (browser) side and a server-side program that is responsible for responding to Ajax requests. Although the server-side program can be written in any number of languages, this book focuses on PHP for the vast majority of examples. The good news is that PHP is a simple enough language that you don’t have to know PHP to be able to make out the sample code and make isolated changes for customization.

The Booker example is somewhat unique in that it doesn’t require any custom development on the server side. Since all the Ajax request is doing is loading an entire e-book text file from the Web server, the server itself is all that is needed. If the application were plucking a portion of a book, such as an individual chapter, then you might need to develop a custom program on the server.

Of course, the client still needs some custom programming that must respond to the user selecting an e-book from the selection list. This custom code must then load the e-book from the server asynchronously and display it on the page. In many ways, this is the most basic of all Ajax requests because you don’t need to pass any information to the server—you just specify the name of the e-book text file when making the Ajax request. More on this detail in a moment; let’s first revisit the e-books.

The E-Books

In the Booker example, the e-books are provided as raw text files with a familiar .txt file extension. You can open and edit these files just like any other text files using Vi, Emacs, or Pico on Linux systems, Notepad on Windows systems, or TextEdit on the Mac. The following books and their associated text files are included with the sample application:

  • Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland—book_alicewonderland.txt

  • Frankenstein—book_frankenstein.txt

  • Moby Dick—book_mobydick.txt

  • War and Peace—book_warandpeace.txt

  • The War of the Worlds—book_warofworlds.txt

You may notice a very clear pattern in the naming of the e-book text files in that they all follow the form book_AbbrevTitle.txt, where AbbrevTitle is the abbreviated title of the book. By following a standard format for the e-book filenames, it becomes much easier to programmatically load each book in response to the user making a selection from the selection list on the page. More specifically, you can build a filename programmatically using the following equation:

"book_" + AbbrevTitle + ".txt"

As you see in a moment, this is in fact exactly how the code works that handles loading an e-book in response to a change in selection.

The Booker Web Page

All of the heavy lifting in the Booker sample application takes place in the booker.html Web page, which contains HTML and JavaScript code, along with a few CSS styles here and there. Before digging into the code, however, let’s take one more look at the application in action in Figure 2.4, this time with the e-book Frankenstein loaded.

Figure 2.4. The complete text of the e-book appears once the request completes.

Although the JavaScript code occurs near the beginning of the actual HTML document, it makes more sense to take a look at the body HTML code first:

<body style="background:#EEEEEE"
  onload="document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex = 0;
  loadBook(document.getElementById('book').options[
  document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex].value)">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:block"></div>
  <br />
  <div style="margin-left:10%">Select book:
    <select id="book" onchange=
      "loadBook(this.options[this.selectedIndex].value)">
      <option value="alicewonderland">Alice's Adventure's in
        Wonderland by Lewis Carroll</option>
      <option value="frankenstein">Frankenstein by Mary
        Shelley</option>
      <option value="mobydick">Moby Dick by Herman
        Melville</option>
      <option value="warandpeace">War and Peace by Leo
        Tolstoy</option>
      <option value="warofworlds">The War of the Worlds by H.G.
        Wells</option>
    </select>
  </div>
  <br />
  <div id="booktext" style="margin-left:10%; margin-right:10%;
    padding:2%; background:#FFFFFF; border:thin solid #333333">
    </div>
</body>

The bulk of this code consists of the e-book selection list, which appears within the <select> tag. Notice that the abbreviated title of each book is specified as the value attribute of each e-book’s <option> tag. This is how the JavaScript code is able to assemble the name of a book’s text file (book_alicewonderland.txt) from its value in the selection list (alicewonderland).

Moving down the page a bit, you’ll see a div element with an ID of booktext. This is the div element that holds the text contents of an e-book after it is loaded via an Ajax request. In other words, this div is the main area of the page. The various CSS styles applied to the div are what align it in the center of the page, space the text in a little from the edges, and give it a visible border. The ID of this div element is used in the JavaScript code for the page to set the e-book text after it has been loaded through an Ajax request.

Looking back for a second at the selection list, perhaps the most critical piece of code in the page is the onchange event handler for the selection list, which contains the following line of JavaScript code:

loadBook(this.options[this.selectedIndex].value)

This is the code that loads an e-book based upon a selection change in the selection list. The code is passing the value of the currently selected e-book to the custom loadBook() function. Here’s the code for that function:

function loadBook(title) {
  replaceText(document.getElementById("booktext"), "Loading...");
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "book_" + title + ".txt", handleRequest);
}

Ah, two familiar functions! If you recall from the earlier discussion of the Ajax toolkit, the replaceText() and ajaxSendRequest() functions were both explained. As a little refresher, the replaceText() function is used to set the contents of any HTML element. In this case, it is being used to set the main div element (booktext) to the text “Loading...” so that the user knows that the e-book is being loaded. The actual Ajax request is initiated by the ajaxSendRequest() function, which uses the GET request method. The second parameter to the ajaxSendRequest() function is the filename of the e-book to load, which has the form book_AbbrevTitle.txt. And finally, the last parameter is the custom handler function that is called when the Ajax request is completed (i.e., the e-book finishes loading).

OK, if that last paragraph felt like a technical punch in the mouth, I readily apologize. I don’t entirely expect you to follow every nuance of this code as quickly as I’ve thrown it at you. As you see more Ajax applications, it will all start to feel much more natural and familiar. The good news is that in the meantime, you only need to understand a few critical points in the code to make customizations for your own purposes. We’ll get to that in the next section.

The next big stop in this whirlwind examination of the Booker application is the handleRequest() function, which is the custom function that is called when an Ajax request completes. Here’s the code for it:

function handleRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    replaceText(document.getElementById("booktext"),
      request.responseText);
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

Hopefully this code is a little simpler than you were expecting. The first line of code within the function checks to make sure that the request finished successfully. This involves looking at the values of the readyState and status properties of the request object. This is very standard Ajax code that you will see reproduced verbatim in every Ajax application. The specific values being checked are directly related to the standard XMLHttpRequest object, which is covered in great detail in Appendix B. Feel free to study that appendix to find out more about how the Ajax toolkit works under the hood. For now, however, just know that the if statement is simply checking to make sure the request finished up without a problem.

Within the if statement, the replaceText() function is called to set the main div element (booktext) on the page to the e-book text that was just loaded. The key to this code is the responseText property of the request object, which contains the response data from the request. You’ll always use the responseText property for raw text data or the responseXML property for XML data when handling the results of an Ajax request.

The last line of meaningful code in the handleRequest() function is a call to the ajaxUpdateStatus() function, which updates the Ajax diagnostic bar. It is very important that this function remain outside of the if statement so that it gets called every time the state of the XMLHttpRequest object changes. One thing I haven’t mentioned is that this function requires a special div element to be placed on the page. If you look back at the HTML code for the Booker application page, you’ll see a div element that is coded like this:

<div id="ajaxState" style="display:block"></div>

The ajaxUpdateStatus() function looks for a div element with an ID of ajaxState, and then sets the background color and content of the div accordingly. To hide the Ajax diagnostic bar, just change the style property of the div element to display:none. You can then show the diagnostic bar again later if necessary by changing the style back to display:block. When you are finished developing an Ajax application and are ready to completely eliminate the diagnostic bar, just remove the div entirely, as well as the call to ajaxUpdateStatus().

One bit of housekeeping that I’ve yet to explain is the onload event handler for the body element, which is very important because it establishes which e-book is initially selected, and also makes sure the book gets loaded properly. Here’s the code that is set in the onload event handler:

document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex = 0;
loadBook(document.getElementById('book').options[
  document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex].value);

The first line sets the selected item in the selection list to the first item in the list (item 0). You use 0 for the first item because the indexing of selection lists starts at 0 and counts up. If you wanted to select the second item, you would set the selectedIndex property to 1.

The second and third lines of code are really just one line of code carried across two lines because it’s so long. The code is made a bit messier because of the need to use the getElementById() function twice to get the selection list object. The code is much easier to understand if you replace the getElementById() function calls with a name. Here’s a simplification of the code with this selection list object renamed sel:

sel.selectedIndex = 0;
loadBook(sel.options[sel.selectedIndex].value);

Now, hopefully, you can see how the loadBook() function is passed the value of the currently selected e-book. Or more specifically, it is passed the abbreviated title of the currently selected e-book. And if you recall from earlier in this discussion, the loadBook() function uses this abbreviated title to build a filename for the e-book and pass it along as part of the Ajax request.

Customizing the E-Book Viewer

I know, there’s a good chance the last few pages have made you question all this Ajax stuff and whether it’s really worth the headache. Again, I have to reiterate that the goal here is to just get your feet wet, not to present you with a trial-by-fire tutorial on Ajax. Remember that the premise of this book is to present you with plug-and-play Ajax applications and components that you can reuse in your own Web pages with minimal effort. As it turns out, this minimal effort sometimes requires some minor JavaScript tweaking, which is why I wanted you to go ahead and see some code early.

Speaking of reusing code in your own Web pages, have you had time to think about how the Booker application might be useful for any of your own projects? Think about it this way: Even though the application in its current form is being used as a rudimentary e-book viewer, what’s really going on is that it allows the user to select and load large text documents dynamically. So any application that involves the selection of unwieldy text documents would be an ideal candidate for a similar Ajax solution. Maybe it’s an archival Web site containing lots of archived documents, or maybe your company stores proposals, processes, or contracts as raw text that would be more easily accessed through such an Ajax interface. If so, you might want to consider customizing the Booker sample application for your own needs.

Here is a list of ways you can modify the application with relatively little effort:

  • Add more content—. You can easily expand the content of the application dramatically by copying new e-book text files to the Web server and then adding a new selection list option for each new book. Just make sure that the value attribute of each <option> tag (warofworlds, for example) matches the abbreviated title of the book you use in the filename of the text file (book_warofworlds.txt).

  • Change the rendering of the e-book content—. If you want to make the e-books a bit more accessible to all readers, you may want to consider increasing the font size and maybe even choosing a different font that is more suitable for reading. Since all of the book content flows into the div element with the ID booktext, you can easily alter the appearance of an entire book by adding some CSS font styles to that div element.

  • Turn off the Ajax diagnostic bar—. Don’t forget to turn off the Ajax diagnostic bar before releasing your application to the public. To do so, just remove the div element with the ID of ajaxState from the HTML part of the page, and then remove the line of code in the JavaScript handleRequest() function that calls the ajaxUpdateState() function.

If you’re thinking another cool tweak to the Booker application might be to load and display each e-book one chapter at a time, then you’re certainly on to something because the next chapter tackles exactly that challenge.

Summary

Indeed, this chapter shifted things into high gear pretty quickly. You started off with a gingerly stroll through the pieces and parts of the Ajax machine, learned a few things about the lifecycle of an Ajax request, and then put the pedal to the metal in getting a rapid-fire introduction to the Ajax toolkit and a downright jarring introduction to your first complete Ajax application. All I can say is that things only get better from here. Sure, the applications build and get a little trickier here and there, but you’ll start to see some very common threads among all Ajax applications.

Part II. Building Real-World Ajax Applications

Chapter 3. Dynamically Loading Web Data: An XML E-Book Viewer

Ajax in the Arcade

The Web isn’t the first time the term Ajax has been associated with interactivity and computing. In 1987 Konami released a vertically scrolling space shoot-’em-up video game of the same name. In Ajax the video game you alternate between controlling a helicopter and a fighter jet, and fire various types of missiles, lasers, and bombs.

The previous chapter explored the basics of loading data dynamically with Ajax, but the e-book example was fairly inefficient since it required you to wait for an entire book to load before being able to read anything. The Booker application also only demonstrated how to load a raw text file, which doesn’t do much in the way of addressing the XML part of the Ajax equation. It actually makes more sense to deal with e-books in XML form, which gives you the ability to pick and choose what information you want to process. More specifically, it is relatively straightforward to access an XML e-book on a chapter-by-chapter basis, thereby shortening the transfer time of each Ajax request and improving the readability of the book by only displaying one chapter at a time.

This chapter builds upon the Booker example in the previous chapter by guiding you through a redesign of the application using XML as the e-book data format.
