In addition to seeing how XML fits into a practical Ajax application, you get a feel for how much more responsive an application becomes when you reduce the amount of data being exchanged per request.

The following files are used by the Booker application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap03 example code folder:

  • booker.html—Main Web page

  • booker.php—Server script for loading a chapter from an e-book

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • book_alicewonderland.xml—Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland XML e-book

  • book_frankenstein.xml—Frankenstein XML e-book

  • book_mobydick.xml—Moby Dick XML e-book

  • book_warandpeace.xml—War and Peace XML e-book

  • book_warofworlds—War of the Worlds XML e-book

The Challenge: Loading Data in Smaller Chunks

If you took a month hiatus from reading this book after the last chapter in an attempt to read the War and Peace e-book, then you quickly found out that the Booker sample application is a bit lacking in performance. The problem is that the application deals with an e-book as one huge chunk of raw text, which is quite cumbersome when you attempt to read a truly hefty book such as War and Peace, which weighs in at just over 3MB. I don’t know about your Internet connection, but even my broadband connection grunts and moans and takes its sweet time when loading a 3MB text file.

Not only is the transfer time a problem with the original Booker application, but there is a serious usability problem inherent in trying to display an entire book on a single page. Think about it this way—a physical printed book is first broken down into chapters and then further broken down into pages. Not only does this provide you with a great place to put a bookmark to remember your location within a book, but it serves as a helpful mental break to turn the page after reading a good-sized block of text. Not so with Booker. An entire 3MB novel is crammed into one big fat virtual page in your browser. Unless you’re some kind of superhuman speed reader, you will hate this approach to reading almost immediately.

So we’ve assessed that the first Booker application has some problems, and in the spirit of dynamic data loading, surely Ajax offers a solution. Indeed, with a little Ajax help it is possible to dynamically load only what you need, which in the case of an e-book is just a single chapter. You’re killing two birds with one stone with this change because the new approach of loading a chapter of book data at a time yields significant performance improvements due to the dramatically smaller amount of data being shuttled across in each Ajax request. The challenge then is to develop a new-and-improved version of Booker that loads one chapter at a time.

Note

It wouldn’t be out of the question to consider breaking down an e-book into individual pages, as opposed to just chapters. However, what would define the length of a page? Physical printed books certainly don’t adhere to the same number of pages when printed in different sizes and fonts. Web browsers are even more diverse in terms of how much information they can display. For this reason, it makes sense to focus on a chapter as the unit of measure for breaking down e-books into component parts. Later on if you want to paginate a chapter programmatically to fit a particular browser window for better readability, that’s perfectly fine. But for the purposes of organizing and loading an XML e-book, chapters are the way to go.

Before we move on to the design phase of the new e-book viewer application, it’s worth taking a brief step back to reassess the original Booker application and see how it works. By doing so, you’ll have a more accurate picture of the challenge you’re facing in the design of the new application.

First off, Figure 3.1 shows how the main Booker HTML Web page contains a selection list with the available e-books. Choosing a book from the selection list results in the entire contents of the book loading into the area (div) just below the list, which occupies most of the page.

Figure 3.1. The original Booker application loads the entire text of an e-book when the user chooses the book from a list.

Behind the scenes, the Booker Web page is using an Ajax request to dynamically load the book text in response to the user choosing it in the list. From an Ajax perspective, the original Booker application represents perhaps the simplest of all Ajax requests in that it is dealing with unformatted data (raw text) and the data is obtained directly from a file on the server (an e-book text file); no special server program is required to process the data before responding to the request.

The challenge going into the new version of Booker is to first reformat the e-books as highly structured XML documents and then rework the Web page to be able to load a single chapter as opposed to an entire book. This will also involve some user interface changes since the user will now need to be able to navigate between chapters in a book. It turns out that this seemingly subtle change in data format has a significant impact on the design and implementation of the Booker application.

The Design: Designing an XML E-Book Viewer

OK, so you’re revamping the Booker application to load e-books using an XML data format via Ajax requests. This means you’ll first need to come up with a suitable XML format for the books that allows them to be readily accessed one chapter at a time. With the XML format figured out, you’ll need to figure out what exactly is being transferred between the server and client during an Ajax request. It obviously stands to reason that chapter text will be sent along, but now that the e-books will be formatted more intelligently, you should be able to receive the chapter title as part of the request as well. Then you can display the name of the chapter as a heading above the chapter text, giving the page a little more of the feel of a real book.

Note

If you’re not experienced with XML, don’t worry because it is very easy to understand once you see it in action. In fact, you’re indirectly already using XML when creating Web pages in HTML. HTML, or actually its more formal cousin XHTML, can be thought of as a specific application of XML.

The Client Request

So a big part of the design of the new-and-improved Booker application is figuring out exactly what information needs to move between client and server, and then back from server to client. You probably recall that each e-book is stored in its own file. In the first Booker application, the abbreviated title of the book is sent along as part of the Ajax request—this is how the request knows which file to open, which in turn determines the book that is loaded. In the newer version, you’re no longer requesting an entire book but instead a single chapter of a book. For this reason, you’ll also need to pass along the chapter in addition to the abbreviated title. To keep things simple and efficient, the chapter can be specified as a number, where 0 is the first chapter, 1 is the second chapter, and so on.

Note

Since many books contain “chapters” such as an introduction, prologue, epilogue, etc., it’s better if you use a different terminology when referring to the “number” of a chapter with respect to loading a chapter from an XML document. It really makes more sense to call this number a chapter index instead. Therefore, if a book has an introduction and a prologue before Chapter 1, their chapter indexes would be 0, 1, and 2, respectively.

To summarize, the following two pieces of information must be sent from the client to the server to initiate an e-book request in the new version of the Booker application:

  • Abbreviated title (alicewonderland, for example)

  • Chapter index (0, for example)

Let’s now shift our attention to the server side of things and find out what it has to do in response to receiving the client request.

The Server Response

The server’s job is to take the abbreviated e-book title and chapter index, and respond with the chapter text and anything else that might be useful in the context of the client e-book viewer. You already know for certain that the chapter title would be a handy thing to know. However, some books go a step further by having both a chapter number and a chapter title. Knowing this, it’s probably a good idea to receive the chapter number from the server as well. This also helps to account for the situation where there is a “chapter” that doesn’t fall under the normal chapter designation, as in an introduction or epilogue. In this case, the “chapter number” might be “Introduction” instead of “Chapter 1.”

Although you could stop here and be well on your way to a working e-book viewer with support for discrete chapters, a critical piece of information is still missing. To understand what is missing, it’s important to assess how the user interface for the application will work. When you think about it, a physical book operates on the idea of turning pages. Even though the e-book viewer doesn’t break down books into pages, the concept of moving through chapters like the flipping of pages is a good idea. This can be accomplished with Previous and Next buttons, as illustrated in Figure 3.2.

Figure 3.2. Previous and Next buttons provide an intuitive means of navigating through the chapters in an e-book.

Notice in the figure how the chapters can be thought of as logically appearing one after the next. The Next and Previous buttons allow you to navigate from one chapter to the next in either direction. But how do you know when to stop? This seems like a trivial question, but it is very important because it reveals that you must know how many chapters there are in total to know when to disable the Next button. The Previous button is easier to handle because you already know which chapter is the first one. The last chapter is a trickier animal because you’d rather not have to read all of the chapters into the application and remember the name of the last one. The cleaner alternative is to return a total chapter count as part of the Ajax request.

With the total number of chapters in hand, you can simply compare the currently viewed chapter to the total number of chapters to see whether or not the Next button is usable. In other words, if the reader is already viewing the last chapter, you need to disable the Next button. The total number of chapters is the key piece of information that makes this user interface trick possible.

So recapping the server-to-client data transmission part of the Booker application, here’s what is getting sent:

  • Chapter number (Chapter I, for example)

  • Chapter title (Down the Rabbit-Hole, for example)

  • Chapter text (Alice was beginning to..., for example)

  • Total number of chapters (12, for example)

With the server’s response in mind, it’s now possible to assemble the entire client-server conversation and dig a little deeper into exactly how the data is represented within it.

Making Sense of the Client-Server Conversation

Putting together the entire data-transfer circuit for the application gives you Figure 3.3, which shows the flow of information from client to server and then back from server to client, all of which is part of a single Ajax request.

Figure 3.3. The data flow between the client and server in an Ajax request is a critical part of the design of the Booker application.

The figure reveals how multiple pieces of information are being transferred back and forth between the client and server in both directions. You already know from the original Booker application that it’s not difficult at all to send along information to the server as part of the URL for the Ajax request. For example, the abbreviated title of an e-book in the previous version of Booker was sent along using the following URL:

booker.php?title=alicewonderland

You can add the chapter index as a second parameter in the URL by separating it from the title with an ampersand (&), like this:

booker.php?title=alicewonderland&chapIndex=0

This covers the data transfer from client to server. The data coming back from server to client is a lot trickier since it contains the entire text for a chapter, along with other book information, including the chapter number, chapter title, and total number of chapters. This data requires fairly sophisticated formatting to ensure that it can be extracted cleanly back on the client. It turns out that XML offers a perfect solution for formatting this data. Even if you aren’t an XML guru, I don’t think you’ll have too much trouble deciphering the following code:

<bookData>
  <chapNum>Chapter I</chapNum>
  <chapTitle>Down the Rabbit-Hole</chapTitle>
  <chapText>Alice was beginning to get very tired of sitting by
  her sister on the bank, and of having nothing to do...
  </chapText>
  <totalChapters>12</totalChapters>
</bookData>

Note

There is nothing standard or official about any of this XML code. It was created purely for the purposes of the Booker application. One of the key benefits of XML is that it allows you to create any language you want for any purpose. Knowing this, you could easily rename the <bookData> tag to <eBookInfo> or <booker>, as long as you use the tag name consistently throughout all of the application code.

As you can see, the chapter information included in the server response is coded using XML tags that clearly describe each piece of information. The chapter text was deliberately pared down in this example to make the code easier to read.

So the server responds to the client’s Ajax request with XML data that includes the relevant information about the chapter. It then becomes the client’s job to parse the XML data and display it on the page. You find out exactly how this is done a little later in the chapter. Before that, however, it’s important to cover one additional design issue: the need for a server script.

A critical part of the design of the new Booker application is the coding of each e-book as an XML document. Since each e-book contains numerous chapters, along with other information such as the title and author of the book, it stands to reason that there must be some kind of program on the server that knows how to take a chapter request and respond with the XML chapter data you saw a moment ago. This server program (PHP script, in this case) is responsible for parsing through the XML data for a complete e-book, extracting the specified chapter number, chapter title, and chapter text, and also counting the total number of chapters in the book. The script must then package this information up into XML code of its own and return it to the browser. This is the same code you saw earlier that included the <bookData> tag.

You now have enough knowledge of the Ajax request/response for the new Booker application to go ahead and put the application together. If you’re a little apprehensive about jumping into XML so quickly, don’t worry about it. It takes surprisingly little code to load and make sense of XML data. Besides, the cool thing about having an XML data-driven application is that you don’t have to screw around with the application code much (if any) once it’s built—you can make significant changes by simply altering the XML for the e-books themselves.

Putting Together the E-Book Viewer

The best way to begin constructing the hopped up Booker application is to break it down into its pieces and parts. Following are the three major components that go into the application:

  • XML files (book_alicewonderland.xml, book_frankenstein.xml, etc.)

  • HTML Web page (booker.html)

  • PHP server script (booker.php)

The next few sections explore these pieces of the Booker puzzle in more detail, revealing the code that makes them work.

Note

Yikes! Another new technology to learn—PHP. Not so. Although knowing PHP is certainly a huge benefit, you absolutely don’t have to be a PHP programmer to understand and customize this application or any of the others in this book, for that matter. I have deliberately structured any and all PHP code in the sample applications to be extremely simple and minimal. Much of the PHP code you encounter is “boilerplate” code, meaning that you don’t need to touch it to make tweaks and customizations to an application. When an opportunity arises to fine-tune the PHP code, I show you exactly where and why.

The XML E-Books

In the original Booker example, the e-books are specified as raw text files with a familiar .txt file extension. The application opens and displays all of the data for each e-book as one huge chunk of text. The new Booker application expects e-books to be coded in an XML data format that clearly delineates chapters and other information such as the book title, book author, chapter title, etc. Similar to text files, XML files can be opened and edited in any text editor. You can think of an XML file as being just like an HTML Web page file, except that you are dealing with a customized vocabulary (set of tags), as opposed to <html>, <p>, <div>, etc. XML files all have the file extension .xml.

Note

You often see the terms file and document used interchangeably in discussions about XML. Generally speaking, they mean the same thing. Where they do differ at times is when a chunk of XML code isn’t necessarily related to a file on a hard disk. For example, it’s entirely possible (and useful!) to generate XML code dynamically, in which case there is no concept of a file. In this situation, it makes more sense to refer to the XML code using the more abstract term document. I tend to follow this same terminology throughout the book.

So what kind of XML vocabulary do you use to represent an e-book? Although there are some standard e-book vocabularies out there, they tend to be fairly complicated and overkill for this example, so I thought it would be better to create our own. Rather than give you a play-by-play reference to what each tag means, just take a look at an excerpt from an actual book:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<ebook>
  <title>ALICE'S ADVENTURES IN WONDERLAND</title>
  <author>Lewis Carroll</author>
  <chapters>
    <chapter num="Chapter I" title="Down the Rabbit-Hole">
    Alice was beginning to get very tired of sitting by her
    sister on the bank, and of having nothing to do...
    </chapter>
    <chapter num="Chapter II" title="The Pool of Tears">
    &#39;Curiouser and curiouser!&#39; cried Alice (she was so
    much surprised, that for the moment she quite forgot how to
    speak...
    </chapter>
    ...
  </chapters>
</ebook>

The first line is admittedly a bit cryptic, and indicates that the XML code adheres to the XML 1.0 standard and uses a standard character encoding scheme (UTF-8); this is standard code that appears at the top of just about every XML document. The remainder of the code consists of e-book-specific code that should be fairly easy to understand given the tag and attribute names.

It works like this: The code for every e-book is sandwiched within an ebook element, which consists of opening and closing tags, like this:

<ebook>
  ...
</ebook>

Within the ebook element you place another container element (chapters) for the collection of chapters. Here’s the e-book code after the chapters element is added:

<ebook>
  <chapters>
  ...
  </chapters>
</ebook>

Each chapter of an e-book is then coded using the <chapter> tag, which has two attributes for setting the chapter number and title: num and title. In addition to the two attributes, the <chapter> tag itself serves as a container for the chapter text. So, an individual chapter looks like this:

<chapter num="Chapter II" title="The Pool of Tears">
&#39;Curiouser and curiouser!&#39; cried Alice (she was so much
surprised, that for the moment she quite forgot how to speak...
</chapter>

The weird &#39; in the chapter text is an apostrophe (') that has been specially coded so as not to confuse the XML document processor. Another way to code such an element, also known as an entity in XML, is to use XML’s standard apostrophe entity, which is &apos;. A similar coding is necessary for quote characters in e-books, in which case you use &#34; instead of the actual quote (").

Now that you understand the general structure of an e-book XML document and how to code each chapter of an e-book in XML, you can combine the two to make better sense of the code for an entire book. Here’s the partial code for the book The War of the Worlds, by H. G. Wells:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<ebook>
  <title>The War of the Worlds</title>
  <author>H. G. Wells</author>
  <chapters>
  <chapter num="" title="INTRO">
  But who shall dwell in these worlds if they be
  inhabited? .  .  .  Are we or they Lords of the
  World? .  .  .  And how are all things made for man?
  — KEPLER (quoted in The Anatomy of Melancholy)
  </chapter>
  <chapter num="BOOK ONE, CHAPTER ONE"
  title="THE EVE OF THE WAR">
  THE COMING OF THE MARTIANS

  No one would have believed in the last years of the
  nineteenth century that this world was being watched keenly
  and closely by intelligences greater than man&#39;s and yet
  as mortal as his own...
  </chapter>
  <chapter num="BOOK ONE, CHAPTER TWO"
  title="THE FALLING STAR">
  Then came the night of the first falling star. It was seen
  early in the morning, rushing over Winchester eastward, a
  line of flame high in the atmosphere. Hundreds must have
  seen it, and taken it for an ordinary falling star...
  </chapter>
  ...
 </chapters>
</ebook>

Hopefully the XML code is making a bit more sense now that you’ve seen parts of two different books coded using the same format. It’s worth noting how The War of the Worlds is a bit unusual in that it is divided into two “books.” Rather than modify the entire data format for what amounts to a different way to specify chapters, the book number is incorporated into the chapter number. Unlike some of the other e-books, this book is also interesting in that it takes advantage of both the chapter number and chapter title, which helps demonstrate the need for both pieces of information.

Although I have to admit it was no fun at all, I manually converted the five sample e-books from the previous chapter to the XML format you just learned about. It was no sweat until I encountered the beastly (literally!) Moby Dick, which is divided into 102 chapters! And don’t even get me started on War and Peace, a novel of such epic proportions that it provides an excellent stress test for the Booker application. You’ll ultimately find that coding e-books as XML is the most time-consuming part of customizing the Booker application to suit your own needs.

Earlier in the chapter I mentioned that XML files have .xml file extensions even though they are actually text files. Following are the sample e-books used in the updated Booker application, along with their respective filenames:

  • Alice’s Adventures in Wonderland—book_alicewonderland.xml

  • Frankenstein—book_frankenstein.xml

  • Moby Dick—book_mobydick.xml

  • War and Peace—book_warandpeace.xml

  • The War of the Worlds—book_warofworlds.xml

The e-books are named using the same abbreviated titles as you saw before—the only change is that they have an .xml file extension instead of .txt. This means you can build an XML e-book filename programmatically using the following equation:

"book_" + AbbrevTitle + ".xml"

The next section reveals how the Booker application code uses this filename in conjunction with a chapter index to make Ajax requests to the server.

The Booker Web Page

Although there is a server-side component to the Booker application, the bulk of the work takes place on the client, which corresponds to the HTML Web page part of the application, booker.html. Figure 3.4 shows the newly revamped Booker application running in a Web browser, which reveals the updated user interface that provides buttons for chapter navigation.

Figure 3.4. Only a single e-book chapter is displayed now, and buttons are provided to navigate forward and back through other chapters.

Note

If you’re wondering where the Ajax diagnostic bar went in the Booker application, the answer is that I hid it so that it doesn’t distract from the figures. In fact, the diagnostic bar doesn’t appear in any of the remaining applications of the book. You can still easily enable the diagnostic bar in the examples by removing the style display:none from the Ajax state div (ID of ajaxState) in the HTML code for the Web page, or you can change the style to display:block if you want to leave the style so you can disable the bar later.

The figure reveals several new user interface facets to the application. First off, notice that the chapter title is displayed just below the e-book selection list. This area actually is capable of displaying both the chapter number and chapter title, but in the case of the introduction to The War of the Worlds only the chapter number (INTRO) appears.

Below the chapter heading (number + title) are two navigation buttons for moving to the first and previous chapters, respectively. Notice that these buttons are disabled in the figure, which is a subtle but handy user interface cue that you are viewing the first chapter of the book. The chapter text appears next, followed by additional navigation buttons for moving to the next and last chapters.

For testing purposes, I originally had all of the navigation buttons near each other above the chapter text, but it occurred to me that it wouldn’t make much sense to read to the bottom of a chapter and then have to scroll up to navigate to the next chapter. That’s why the Next and End (>>) buttons appear below the chapter text.

OK, I’ve beat around the bush long enough, so let’s go ahead and take a look at the code for the body of the page, which establishes the user interface and wires together the scripted events:

<body style="background:#EEEEEE"
  onload="document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex = 0;
  loadChapter(document.getElementById('book').options[
  document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex].value, 0)">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div style="margin-left:10%">Select book:
    <select id="book"
      onchange="loadChapter(this.options[
      this.selectedIndex].value, 0)">
      <option value="alicewonderland">Alice's Adventure's in
        Wonderland by Lewis Carroll</option>
      <option value="frankenstein">Frankenstein by Mary
        Shelley</option>
      <option value="mobydick">Moby Dick by Herman
        Melville</option>
      <option value="warandpeace">War and Peace by Leo
        Tolstoy</option>
      <option value="warofworlds">The War of the Worlds by
        H.G. Wells</option>
    </select>
  </div>
  <br />
  <div id="chapinfo" style="margin-left:10%; font-size:large">
  </div>
  <br />
  <div style="margin-left:10%">
    <input id="homeButton" type="button" value="<<"
      style="width:60px"
      onclick="curchapIndex = 1; prevChapter()" />
    <input id="prevButton" type="button"
      value="< Previous" style="width:120px"
      onclick="prevChapter()" />
  </div>
  <br />
  <div id="chaptext"
    style="margin-left:10%; margin-right:10%; padding:2%;
    background:#FFFFFF; border:thin solid #333333"></div>
  <br />
  <div style="margin-left:10%">
    <input id="nextButton" type="button" value="Next >"
      style="width:120px" onclick="nextChapter()"   />
    <input id="endButton" type="button" value=">>"
      style="width:60px"
      onclick="curchapIndex = (totalChapters - 2);
      nextChapter()" />
  </div>
</body>

Again, I realize this code is fairly JavaScript-laden for a book that claims you don’t need to know anything about JavaScript. But keep in mind that the point is for you to be able to reuse this code in your own applications. And that is entirely possible without you necessarily understanding how every line of code works. Even so, it’s worth painting a general picture of what is going on so that you have some insight into how you might make modifications. And don’t forget that all of this code (and the remaining code in this section) is located in the booker.html file on the live Linux CD that accompanies the book.

First off, the user interface is organized into several div elements that provide a good means of cleanly organizing the chapter information and navigation buttons. Similar to the previous version of the application, a selection list containing the e-books is coded in HTML using the <select> tag. Of more interest in this version are the navigation buttons, which are coded using the <input> tag.

The functionality of the Previous and Next navigation buttons comes in the way of calls to the prevChapter() and nextChapter() JavaScript functions, which you see in a moment. The Home (<<) and End (>>) buttons use these same functions, but with the help of the curChapIndex variable, which keeps track of the current chapter index. You see how this variable works in a moment when you get into the JavaScript code for the application.

The other really important piece of code in the body of the Booker Web page is the code that loads an e-book based upon a selection change in the selection list:

loadChapter(this.options[this.selectedIndex].value, 0)

This code is identical to the selection-change code for the previous version of the application, except this code passes along the chapter index (0) in addition to the currently selected e-book (this.options[this.selectedIndex].value). The function name has also changed to loadChapter() from loadBook() to indicate that only a chapter is being loaded. A similar call to the loadChapter() function occurs in the onload event handler for the <body> tag, which is responsible for loading the first chapter of the first e-book in the list when the page is first opened.

Here’s the code for the loadChapter() function, which loads a single chapter via an Ajax request given an abbreviated e-book title and chapter index:

function loadChapter(title, chapIndex) {
  // Could be a new book, so initialize the navigation buttons
  if (chapIndex == 0) {
    document.getElementById("homeButton").disabled = true;
    document.getElementById("prevButton").disabled = true;
    document.getElementById("nextButton").disabled = false;
    document.getElementById("endButton").disabled = false;
  }

  // Set the current chapter index
  curchapIndex = chapIndex;

  // Clear the previous chapter text
  replaceText(document.getElementById("chaptext"),
    "Loading...");

  // Send the Ajax request to load the new chapter
  var bookURL = "booker.php?title=" +
    encodeURIComponent(title) +
    "&chapIndex=" + encodeURIComponent(chapIndex);
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", bookURL, handleRequest);
}

The first several lines of the function initialize the navigation buttons so that they are enabled and disabled appropriately for the first chapter of the first book. The current chapter index is then stored away since you need to remember it in order to successfully navigate from one chapter to the next. The chapter text area serves double duty as a status notification for the loading of a chapter, and is set to "Loading..." while the chapter is being loaded. Once the chapter loads, this text is replaced with the actual chapter text. Finally, the actual Ajax request is sent via the GET request method by bundling the abbreviated title and chapter index into a URL. Notice that the familiar handleRequest() function is specified as the request handler, which means it gets called when the request completes.

The handleRequest() function does the lion’s share of the work in the Booker application, which in this case consists of receiving the XML book data, plucking out the important information, and updating the elements on the page accordingly. Before exploring the code for this function, let’s take a quick moment to recap the format of the XML data that is being sent back from the server in the Ajax response:

<bookData>
  <chapNum />
  <chapTitle>INTRO</chapTitle>
  <chapText>
  But who shall dwell in these worlds if they be
  inhabited? .  .  .  Are we or they Lords of the
  World? .  .  .  And how are all things made for man?
  — KEPLER (quoted in The Anatomy of Melancholy)
  </chapText>
  <totalChapters>28</totalChapters>
</bookData>

This is an example of the kind of XML data that must be processed by the handleRequest() function, whose code follows:

function handleRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Store the XML response data
    var xmlData = request.responseXML.getElementsByTagName(
      "bookData")[0];

    // Store the total number of chapters
    totalChapters =
getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("totalChapters")[0]);

    // Display the chapter info
    var chapNum = getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
      "chapNum")[0]);
    var chapTitle =
getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("chapTitle")[0]);
    var chapInfo;
    if (chapNum.length > 0 && chapTitle.length > 0)
      chapInfo = chapNum + " : " + chapTitle;
   else
     chapInfo = chapNum + chapTitle;
   replaceText(document.getElementById("chapinfo"), chapInfo);

   // Display the chapter text
   replaceText(document.getElementById("chaptext"),
     getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("chapText")[0]));
 }
 ajaxUpdateState();
}

The code first checks to make sure the Ajax request finished successfully, and then whittles down the XML data to the first bookData element, which in this case is the only bookData element. From there, JavaScript DOM functions are used to carefully extract each piece of data from the XML data. For example, the total number of chapters are grabbed and stored in the totalChapters variable with the following line of code:

totalChapters =
  getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("totalChapters")[0]);

If the total number of chapters happen to have been coded in XML with a different tag name (say, <NumberOfChapters>), then the previous line of code would simply be changed to this:

totalChapters =
  getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("NumberOfChapters")[0]);

This is an example of how you can modify the XML processing code of the application without necessarily digging into the nuts and bolts of the DOM functions that make it work.

Note

The getText() and replaceText() functions used in the Booker application are not standard JavaScript DOM functions but are instead part of the helper functions included with the book in the file domkit.js. The previous chapter explains the relevance of these functions and the other functions in the Ajax Toolkit.

The remainder of the code in the handleRequest() function involves plucking out pieces of information from the XML data and displaying them within each div element on the page. The code is smart enough to check for the presence of both a chapter number and chapter title, and combine them together if both exist. Otherwise, only the one that is specified is actually displayed. This is a clever way to account for the fact that some books have chapter numbers, some have chapter titles, and some have both.

The only part of the Web page you haven’t seen are the two functions that respond to navigation button clicks, prevChapter() and nextChapter(). Let’s start with the prevChapter() function, whose code follows:

function prevChapter() {
  if (curchapIndex > 0) {
    curchapIndex—;
    document.getElementById("nextButton").disabled = false;
    document.getElementById("endButton").disabled = false;
  }
  if (curchapIndex == 0) {
    document.getElementById("homeButton").disabled = true;
    document.getElementById("prevButton").disabled = true;
  }
  loadChapter(document.getElementById('book').options[
    document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex].value,
      curchapIndex);
}

A majority of the code in this function is devoted to intelligently enabling and disabling the navigation buttons based upon whether or not the current chapter is the first chapter. Once the navigation button logic is carried out, the loadChapter() function is called with the current chapter index used to specify which chapter to load.

The nextChapter() function is very similar to prevChapter(), with the role reversed to focus on the last chapter instead of the first. In other words, if the current chapter is the last chapter, then it is important for the nextChapter() function to disable the Next button. The following code shows how the totalChapters variable is used as the basis for determining if the current chapter is the last chapter:

function nextChapter() {
  if (curchapIndex  < (totalChapters - 1)) {
    curchapIndex++;
    document.getElementById("homeButton").disabled = false;
    document.getElementById("prevButton").disabled = false;
  }
  if (curchapIndex == (totalChapters - 1)) {
    document.getElementById("nextButton").disabled = true;
    document.getElementById("endButton").disabled = true;
  }
  loadChapter(document.getElementById('book').options[
    document.getElementById('book').selectedIndex].value,
      curchapIndex);
}

This wraps up the client side of the newly revamped Booker application. All that’s left is a quick look at the server script, which is a piece of cake compared to the client.

The Booker Server Script

The PHP script on the server side of the Booker application is responsible for reading an entire e-book XML file and then dynamically generating an XML document containing only the relevant chapter information needed by the Web page. This chapter information is relayed back to the client via an Ajax response. Following is the code for the server PHP script, booker.php:

<?php
header('Content-Type: text/xml');

// Parse the e-book XML data into a structure
$rawBook = file_get_contents("book_" . $_REQUEST['title'] .
  ".xml");
$xml = new SimpleXmlElement($rawBook);

$chap = $xml->chapters->chapter[intval($_REQUEST['chapIndex'])];
$xmlChapter = '<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>';
$xmlChapter .= '<bookData>';
$xmlChapter .= '<chapNum>' . $chap['num'] . '</chapNum>';
$xmlChapter .= '<chapTitle>' . $chap['title'] . '</chapTitle>';
$xmlChapter .= '<chapText>' . $chap . '</chapText>';
$count = 0;
foreach ($xml->chapters->chapter as $chapter) {
  $count++;
}
$xmlChapter .= '<totalChapters>' . $count . '</totalChapters>';
$xmlChapter .= '</bookData>';

// Generate a book info XML document
echo $xmlChapter;
?>

Granted, if you don’t know anything about PHP, then this code likely looks very foreign. Rather than trying to teach you a new language, which is certainly beyond the scope and realm of this book, I’ll serve as an interpreter. The beginning of the code establishes that the data being returned is in the XML data format; this is critical so that the server packages up the code properly when delivering the data back to the client in the Ajax response.

The first big chunk of code in the script is responsible for reading an entire e-book from an XML file into a data structure that can be further processed. The piece of code $_REQUEST['title'] is where the PHP script is gaining access to the title parameter that was sent along in the URL of the Ajax request. Remember this code:

booker.php?title=warofworlds&chapIndex=0

The code $_REQUEST['title'] is grabbing the title value sent along in this URL. Similarly, to grab the value of the chapIndex parameter, you use this piece of code:

$_REQUEST['chapIndex']

Getting back to the part of the script that parses in the XML e-book, the code builds a file name, such as book_warofworlds.xml, and reads it into an XML data structure. From there, the main big chunk of code kicks in and rips into the XML code looking for the specified chapter, as indicated by the chapIndex parameter to the script. This code is effectively reading and writing XML at the same time—it is plodding through the entire e-book looking for specific chapter information, and then writing a new XML document containing that information. The end result is the concise XML response data with the <bookData> tag you saw in the previous section.

To really get a sense for how the server script works, it’s best to open one of the e-book XML files in a text editor and study the tags as you go line by line through the last big chunk of code in the script. You’ll start to see a pattern where each tag and attribute is methodically drilled into and its relevant data extracted and packaged into a new XML tag.

When all is said and done with the script building a new XML document of chapter information, the data is returned to the client with a single line of code:

echo $xmlChapter;

I know that’s kind of anticlimactic for how much work the script is doing, but that’s how PHP can be. The real climax is yet to come in the next section when you take the application for a spin around the block. Before you get to that, however, let’s take a quick look at how you can go about testing a PHP script.

You know that the Ajax request for the Booker application consists of a URL that passes parameters to a PHP script. You also know that the script is responsible for sending back an XML document as the response to that request. It can be very helpful to study that response outside of the context of the Ajax request, meaning that you’re taking a peek at what the PHP script generates directly in a Web browser. You do this simply by entering the URL of the Ajax request as the address of a Web page in your browser, like this:

http://www.yourdomain.com/booker.php?title=warofworlds&chapIndex=0

Assuming the PHP script and XML e-books are stored on your server, change the domain to match yours in this line of code and then enter the URL into your Web browser. You can also try this trick directly on the live Linux CD that accompanies this book. Either way, Figure 3.5 shows the XML response data generated by the PHP script.

Figure 3.5. Opening a PHP script directly in a browser gives you a handy means of finding out exactly what kind of response it is generating.

Keep this little trick in mind as you work through other examples in the book, as well as projects of your own. I used it quite often while developing the sample applications, and several times it illuminated an error in the PHP script that wouldn’t have surfaced otherwise.

Testing Booker

With all the hard work behind you, the testing phase is the fun part of the process. Figure 3.6 shows the new Booker application with the book War and Peace open to its first chapter. To follow along in your own browser, open the booker.html page in the chap03 example code folder on the accompanying live Linux CD.

Figure 3.6. The new Booker application starts out by loading the first chapter of the selected book.

Note

At some point you want to consider exploring server logs as a means of finding out exactly what is taking place in regard to Ajax requests. You’ll need to check the documentation for your specific Web server, but all Web servers include logs so that you can find out what requests are made and if something went wrong. You can also view similar information specifically related to Ajax requests directly in your Web browser by using a JavaScript debugger such as Firebug.

Notice in the figure that the chapter number and title appear just below the selection list, which helps give the page a bit more of the feel of a real book. Below that are the Home and Previous navigation buttons, which are initially disabled because it’s impossible to move backward from the first chapter in the book.

If you scroll on down to the bottom of the chapter you’ll see the other pair of navigation buttons, shown in Figure 3.7, which allow you to either move to the next chapter or jump straight to the last chapter in the book.

Figure 3.7. The Next and End navigation buttons appear at the bottom of a chapter, and allow you to navigate forward in the e-book.

Clicking the Next button takes you to the next chapter, as shown in Figure 3.8.

Figure 3.8. Moving to a new chapter causes the chapter heading to update, as well as the chapter text.

This figure now shows the Home and Previous buttons enabled since you are no longer on the first chapter.

As with the earlier version of the application, you can select other books from the selection list and they will load via Ajax. The difference now is that every e-book is handled on a chapter-by-chapter basis, which is a significant improvement both in terms of readability and performance.

Giving Booker an Extreme Ajax Makeover

It’s fairly easy to see how the new Booker e-book viewer could be a handy tool for serving up e-books online. But assuming you aren’t in the business of opening a virtual Web library, what else could the application possibly be altered to accomplish. Keep in mind that the application excels at processing any text document that benefits from being broken into discrete parts. In the case of an e-book, those parts are chapters, but they could really be anything.

One potential twist on the Booker application is a data-driven blog tool. For example, you could alter the existing application so that each “book” corresponds to a blog topic, such as Business, Sports, or Politics. Then within each topic the “chapters” become individual blog entries. The cool thing about this repurposing of Booker is that you could pull it off with literally no changes to the JavaScript or PHP code. You’d need to make a few minor HTML changes to the selection list so that it displays blog topics instead of books, but other than that all of the changes take place in the XML files, which is a good thing.

Remember that the XML file for each e-book includes three primary pieces of information for a given chapter: number, title, and text. The only piece of information that doesn’t really fit the blog idea is the chapter number, which could easily be repurposed as the blog date/time. So it’s possible to code a blog document exactly like one of the e-books but with the blog date and time specified as the chapter number, the blog title as the chapter title, and the blog text as the chapter text. Here’s an example of what such a document might look like:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<ebook>
  <title>Michael's Sports Blog</title>
  <author>Michael Morrison</author>
  <chapters>
    <chapter num="01/16/2007 8:00pm"
    title="Predators Have Most Wins">
    After last night&#39;s win against conference rival Calgary,
    the Nashville Predators are now the first team to reach 32
    wins in the NHL this season, and are only one point behind
    Anaheim in the entire league. Go Preds!
    </chapter>
    <chapter num="12/31/2006 11:30pm"
    title="Titans Eliminate Themselves">
    In a loss that will haunt the franchise until next season,
    the Tennessee Titans&#39; miraculous season turn-around came
    to a screeching halt as they lost to the Patriots in Week 17
    and eliminated themselves from the playoffs.
    </chapter>
  </chapters>
</ebook>

Granted, you could argue that the XML vocabulary should be modified so that the blog XML code doesn’t have tags such as <ebook> and <chapter>, but that’s not really critical. If you feel comfortable enough to tinker with the XML vocabulary, by all means do so. But short of that, you can accomplish quite a lot by simply altering the content of the XML files.

Summary

You’re only three chapters in and you now have an Ajax-powered e-book viewer that is capable of dynamically loading individual chapters from e-books that are coded using a custom XML language. Sounds like a lot of complexity, but hopefully you realized that there wasn’t an enormous amount of code involved in an application that’s doing quite a lot. I’m not saying the code is easy to digest the first time around, but it is fairly concise. More importantly, you learned that the code is quite extensible, allowing you to transform the application into something else entirely with surprisingly few changes.

Chapter 4. Using Ajax to Dynamically Populate Lists: A Stock Picker

Covert Ajax

Who would’ve thought that Ajax has a connection to conflict in the Middle East? In 1953 the United Kingdom and United States collaborated in a covert operation to overthrow Iranian Prime Minister Mohammed Mossadegh. Operation Ajax was a short-term success but did little to stabilize relations between Iran and the West, as the 1979 Islamic Revolution and events thereafter have proved.

A fairly common user interface design in Web pages is to use multiple selection lists to allow users to pick hierarchical data. A good example is a city/state/country selection, where a list of countries in one list determines which states can be selected in another list, which in turn determines which cities can be selected in a third list. The idea is to constrain a user’s possible choices in one list based upon the selection in another. Although this feature can certainly be created using nothing more than client-side JavaScript code, Ajax is necessary in order to dynamically build the selection lists from data obtained from a server.

This chapter explores this problem in detail and shows you exactly how to use Ajax to dynamically build selection lists from server data. This is a handy Ajax design pattern you are likely to want to repeat in your own applications. What’s more, in the process of developing another practical sample application, you add a few more Ajax tricks to your rapidly expanding repertoire.

The following files are used by the Picker application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap04 example code folder:

  • picker.html—Main Web page

  • quoter.php—Server script for loading a stock quote

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • stocks.xml—XML list of stock picks

The Challenge: Displaying Selected Stock Information

The challenge is simple: Use Ajax to dynamically populate a selection list based upon a choice made in another selection list. Why bother? Because in plenty of scenarios such a two-pronged selection approach makes a lot of sense. In fact, a four-pronged approach is used thousands of times a day on one of the most popular Web sites known to man. I’m talking about eBay—specifically the category selection part of listing an item for auction. Figure 4.1 shows how eBay uses up to four selection lists to allow the user to drill down into subcategories when listing an item.

Figure 4.1. eBay is a perfect example of using multiple selection lists to allow the user to make selections between interdependent data.

Such an arrangement of selection lists provides the user an intuitive way to make a selection when dealing with data that is in some way hierarchical. If you can think of a set of data as a sequence of decisions or can somehow organize it into a tree-like structure that fans out (think of a family tree), then you may have a good opportunity to use two or more dynamically populated selection lists.

It’s worth clarifying that you don’t need Ajax to create selection lists that allow the user to navigate hierarchical data. You see the country/state/city example used all over the place without any Ajax help at all. It’s entirely possible to hard-code such data directly in the JavaScript code of a Web page. The problem is, you have to edit the Web page every time you want to update the selection options, and in some cases this simply isn’t possible. One key concept behind Ajax applications is to make the user interface data-driven, which in this case means building the selection list dynamically from data obtained from the server.

Note

If you do any other Ajax reading, you will eventually come across an Ajax selection list example that uses the familiar country/state/city data. Although the data itself is perfect for a hierarchical selection user interface, it’s not necessarily good from an Ajax perspective because countries, states, and cities rarely change. It’s not as if Alabama gets shuffled into another country every few years. For this reason, I don’t see the point in using a data-driven programming technique on static data unless the goal is to simply make the application more maintainable, which is an admirable goal. I’ve tried hard to make the examples in this book count, and the selection list example in this chapter is no different.

If you think about the data-driven nature of the multiple selection list technique, there are many applications where it could be used. A company directory that is navigable according to departments or security classifications is one possibility. Another is a product catalog organized by department, as in clothing, hardware, etc. One application that grabbed my attention is that of an investment service that offers advice in the form of a list of hot, cold, and safe stocks. It’s not that I necessarily am itching to use such a service, but it provides an opportunity to use Ajax on two fronts:

  • Dynamically populating a selection list

  • Retrieving a real-time stock quote

The first task is the real focus of this chapter, whereas the second task is a fringe benefit of creating an application that has a practical need that can be met by Ajax.

The application you develop in this chapter is called Picker, as in stock picker, and it presents two selection lists—one with stock types and the other with actual stocks. Stock types include hot, cold, and safe. The stock list is dynamically populated based upon selections made in the stock type list. An editor of such a site could make back-end changes to the stock picks on the server with the results automatically carrying over to the data-driven page. The stock quote enters the picture when a stock has been selected in the stock list. Detailed information, including the current stock price, is displayed below the two selection lists.

What you really have in this application is two trickle-down Ajax requests:

  • The first Ajax request grabs a list of stocks from the server based upon the selected stock type (hot, cold, or safe).

  • The second Ajax request grabs the latest information about a specific stock based upon the selected stock in the stock list.

If this sounds like a bold challenge, don’t sweat it too much because Ajax provides a clear path to accomplishing these tasks without too much trouble. The trick is coming up with a simple design so that the code doesn’t get out of hand.

The Design: Designing a Stock Picker

The design of any Ajax application in large part boils down to a careful analysis of what data is being shuttled between the client and server. In the case of the Picker application, two different Ajax requests are taking place, one after the next, so you have double the design work. Fortunately, neither request is particularly complex, so the design can still remain straightforward.

The first request is in response to the user selecting a stock type from the first selection list. When this type is selected, a request is made that hits the server for a list of stocks matching that particular type. At this point all you’re trying to do is get a list of stocks based on a selected type. This list of stocks is used to populate the second selection list, which just so happens to serve as the basis for the second Ajax request.

The second request is initiated when the user chooses a stock from the second selection list, the stock list. This request performs a live lookup of stock information from a Web service that provides financial data. Unless you happen to run a financial stock quote server, this is a request you’ll be making to a third-party service, as opposed to serving up your own data as in the first request. The up-to-the-minute stock information returned by the server is then displayed on the Web page. Figure 4.2 shows a crude representation of how the user interface of the application translates into Ajax requests.

Figure 4.2. The user interface for the Picker application consists of two selection lists and a text area containing information about the selected stock.

Now that you have an idea of the general design of the Picker application, let’s look a bit closer at the specific Ajax requests and responses, and what kind of data is flying around in them.

The Client Requests

The data sent from the client to the server in the Picker application is extremely minimal. In fact, for the initial stock list request, nothing at all needs to be sent because the XML data for the stock list is small enough that it’s not a problem just to retrieve the entire document and parse out what you need directly in client-side JavaScript code. So, you don’t have to worry about sending anything along with the first request.

The second request obviously has to send at least the name of a stock because it is performing a stock lookup on a financial server. It’s pretty surprising how much information you can get just from the ticker symbol of a stock. For this reason, all you have to send with the second request is the stock symbol, which is typically a three-or four-letter word such as SBUX (Starbucks), FDX (FedEx), AMZN (Amazon), etc.

That wraps up the rather simplistic data sent from the client to the server. Let’s now address what the server sends back.

The Server Responses

The first Ajax request made to the server is to retrieve the entire list of stocks, regardless of the stock type selected. You could argue that this request should include the stock type and therefore only return stocks of a single type in the response, but the role of the server is simplified if you just grab the entire list of stocks. In fact, you don’t need a server script at all to request an entire XML document.

The XML document returned by the server in the stock list response is a list of stocks for all of the possible stock types. The responsibility then falls on the Web page to process the list and pluck out stocks of a given type in order to fill the second selection list. You see an example of the specific XML data for the stocks in the next section.

The second Ajax request prompts the server to return information about a particular stock, including the latest stock price. Because this information is obtained from a third-party service (in this case, webserviceX.net), the stock information returned by the server is not something directly within your control. Not only that, but there is a problem inherent in trying to use Ajax to request data from a server other than your own—it isn’t allowed! For security purposes, you aren’t allowed to carry out an Ajax request with a server at a domain other than where the request originates. What to do?

Although there are other ways around the Ajax security restriction, we’ll use a PHP script as a proxy for snagging data from the third-party server. It works like this: Your Ajax client makes a request to a PHP script on your server, which in turn grabs the data from the third-party server. This is allowed because server-to-server communication isn’t restricted in the same way as client-to-server communication. And as you learn a bit later in the chapter, the PHP script required to pull this off is literally only a few lines of code.

So getting back to the overall server-response picture of the Picker application, the entire list of stocks is returned in the first Ajax response, which is triggered when the user selects a stock type from the first selection list. The second request, which is initiated by a stock selection from the second selection list, results in a response that contains detailed stock information, including the current price. The second response is indirectly received from a third-party Web service, webserviceX.net, that offers several handy data feeds in XML format.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

You now have enough of the data communication equation for the Picker application to put everything together into one cohesive design that factors in what is being sent and received in each of the two Ajax requests. Figure 4.3 shows the client-to-server conversation taking place with respect to these requests.

Figure 4.3. The Picker application involves data flowing back and forth in two different Ajax requests.

The figure reveals how the two Ajax requests generate responses that relay the stock list and stock information back to the browser (client), respectively. The first request sends no extra data along to the server, whereas the second request sends only the ticker symbol of the stock in question. This symbol is packaged into a URL as a single parameter. Here’s an example of a URL used to pass along a stock symbol to the PHP script for the Picker application:

quoter.php?symbol=EBAY

This code reveals that the PHP script responsible for handling the second Ajax request is in the file named quoter.php. You learn how this script works a bit later in the chapter. For now, let’s go back to the first Ajax request and the type of data it expects in return from the server.

The first Ajax request expects an XML document back from the server that contains the entire list of stocks used by the application, including all of the stock types. Following is an example of what this XML code looks like:

<stocks>
  <hot>
    <stock>AAPL</stock>
    <stock>LUV</stock>
    ...
  </hot>
  <cold>
    <stock>HSY</stock>
    <stock>WIN</stock>
    ...
  </cold>
  <safe>
    <stock>KO</stock>
    <stock>CAT</stock>
    ...
  </safe>
</stocks>

As you can see, the stocks are organized into groups that are defined by tags such as <hot>, <cold>, and <safe>. Within each of these groups of tags for each stock type you find the individual stocks, which are coded using the <stock> tag. It’s important to note that ticker symbols are used to identify the stocks, as opposed to actual company names. This is a critical design decision because the ticker symbol of a stock is what is used to look up any given stock, and can be thought of as a unique identifier for the stock.

In the actual data file for the stock list, I included five stocks for each of the different stock types, but you are free to include however many you want. As you see later in the chapter, the Picker application reads all of the stocks within a given type and adds them to the second selection list, so there isn’t really a hard limit on how many stocks you include in the stock list XML data.

Perhaps of more interest is the XML data returned by the server in response to the second Ajax request. This response data consists of detailed stock information, and is generated by the third-party Web service webserviceX.net. Following is an example of the kind of XML data generated by a stock quote request sent to webserviceX.net:

<string>
  <StockQuotes>
    <Stock>
      <Symbol>GOOG</Symbol>
      <Last>504.28</Last>
      <Date>1/16/2007</Date>
      <Time>4:00pm</Time>
      <Change>-0.72</Change>
      <Open>507.87</Open>
      <High>513.00</High>
      <Low>503.30</Low>
      <Volume>7579201</Volume>
      <MktCap>154.4B</MktCap>
      <PreviousClose>505.00</PreviousClose>
      <PercentageChange>-0.14%</PercentageChange>
      <AnnRange>331.55 - 513.00</AnnRange>
      <Earns>7.875</Earns>
      <P-E>64.13</P-E>
      <Name>GOOGLE</Name>
    </Stock>
  </StockQuotes>
</string>

The stock information is probably a bit more detailed than you were expecting, and it certainly contains more information than the Picker application really needs. However, because the data is there, you might as well use as much of it as possible. More specifically, the Picker application ends up using the following pieces of data from the stock information returned in the Ajax response:

  • Stock name (<Name>)

  • Latest price quote (<Last>)

  • Market capitalization (<MktCap>)

  • Trading volume (<Volume>)

  • Percent change in price (<PercentageChange>)

  • Price-to-earnings ratio (<P-E>)

  • Date and time of price quote (<Date> and <Time>)

This is all information a prospective investor might want to know about a particular stock being recommended. Now you just need to figure out how to incorporate all this data into the application itself.

Putting Together the Stock Picker

Like many of the sample Ajax applications throughout the book, the Picker application involves three primary components:

  • XML file (stocks.xml)

  • HTML Web page (picker.html)

  • PHP server script (quoter.php)

In addition to these three parts of the application, a third-party financial Web service is responsible for providing the live stock data. Although this service is an important part of the application, it doesn’t require a distinct component other than code within the HTML Web page that initiates an Ajax request and code within the PHP server script that handles relaying the response back to the page. This arrangement is necessary because Ajax requests aren’t allowed direct access to resources located on other domains.

Let’s break down each part of the Picker application in more detail so you can fully understand how the application works.

The Stocks XML File

The stocks XML file is entirely generated by you, and is responsible for controlling the stocks displayed in the application based upon the stock type selected. Following is the complete code for the sample stocks.xml file, which contains a list of more or less random stock picks:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<stocks>
  <hot>
    <stock>AAPL</stock>
    <stock>LUV</stock>
    <stock>GOOG</stock>
    <stock>FDX</stock>
    <stock>TM</stock>
  </hot>
  <cold>
    <stock>HSY</stock>
    <stock>WIN</stock>
    <stock>MRK</stock>
    <stock>AMGN</stock>
    <stock>BBY</stock>
  </cold>
  <safe>
    <stock>KO</stock>
    <stock>CAT</stock>
    <stock>DIS</stock>
    <stock>GE</stock>
    <stock>MSFT</stock>
  </safe>
</stocks>

Note

I strongly encourage you not to bet the farm on these stock picks! The designation of stocks as being hot, cold, or safe is random for the most part, so don’t hold me to it if you put your nest egg in Apple (AAPL) stock and the iPhone ends up being a flop.

You saw an excerpt of this code earlier in the chapter, so it shouldn’t be entirely foreign to you at this point. Keep in mind that this entire XML file is sent back to the client as the response to the first Ajax request, which is initiated by a stock type selection in the first selection list.

Tip

The stocks.xml file uses stock ticker symbols instead of company names to designate stocks. When assembling your own list of stocks, you may need to find their ticker symbols. You can use an online tool such as Yahoo!’s Symbol Lookup (http://finance.yahoo.com/lookup) to determine such ticker symbols. Also keep in mind that the Picker application will work fine for mutual funds, exchange traded funds (ETFs), and just about anything that has a ticker symbol associated with a major financial exchange; you aren’t limited solely to stocks.

Each individual stock is coded using the <stock> tag with no attributes, and the <stock> tags are carefully arranged within tags that indicate their type. For example, to designate a stock as being “hot,” you simply place its <stock> tag in between the <hot> and </hot> tags. You can include as many stocks as you want within each stock type—the client JavaScript code automatically fills the stock selection list with every stock listed for a given type.

The Picker Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Picker application (picker.html) is relatively simple in terms of its user interface, consisting primarily of two selection lists and a few div and span elements for holding the detailed stock information. Figure 4.4 shows the Picker application open in a Web browser with both selection lists and the stock information in view.

Figure 4.4. The Picker Web page consists of two selection lists and an area for displaying the stock information.

With this figure in mind, check out the HTML code for the body of the page:

<body onload="document.getElementById(
  'stocktype').selectedIndex = 0; loadStockList()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>Select type:
    <select id="stocktype" onchange="loadStockList()">
      <option value="hot">Hot Stocks</option>
      <option value="cold">Cold Stocks</option>
      <option value="safe">Safe Stocks</option>
    </select>
  </div>
  <br />
  <div>Select stock:
    <select id="stock"
onchange="loadStockInfo(this[this.selectedIndex].value);">
    </select>
  </div>
  <br />
  <div id="stockname" style="font-size:large; font-weight:bold">
  </div>
  Share price:  <span id="stockprice" style="font-weight:bold">
  </span><br />
  Market cap:  <span id="stockcap" style="font-weight:bold">
  </span><br />
  Trading volume:  <span id="stockvolume"
  style="font-weight:bold"></span><br />
  % change:  <span id="stockchange" style="font-weight:bold">
  </span><br />
  P/E ratio:  <span id="stockpe" style="font-weight:bold">
  </span><br />
  <div id="stockdatetime" style="font-style:italic"></div>
</body>

The first selection list (stocktype) is coded with fixed options for the different stock types. These stock types are used as the basis for dynamically filling the second selection list (stock), which starts out empty.

The last section of the page contains a couple div elements and several span elements used to cleanly format the stock information. A careful examination of the code reveals how each piece of information is preceded by a label, and in some cases a formatting character such as a dollar sign ($) to help show that the data is monetary. The stock name and date/time are the only elements without labels because the stock name element serves double duty as a status notification for the Ajax request. More specifically, the stock name element displays the text "Loading..." while a stock is being loaded. This lets the user know that a request for data has been made. The meaning of the date/time element on the page should be fairly obvious to the user, so no label is used there either.

The only JavaScript code in the body of the page is located in the onload event handler for the <body> tag and the onchange event handlers for the selection lists. The onload code takes care of initially setting the first stock type as the first selection, and then loading the stock list based upon that selection. This latter step is accomplished by calling the loadStockList() function, which you see in just a moment. For now, just know that this function initiates the Ajax request for loading the stock list from the server.

The loadStockList() function is also called any time the user chooses a different stock type in the first selection list. This action is what triggers the onchange event, resulting in the call to loadStockList(). A similar thing occurs when a change takes place in the second list, except this time the loadStockInfo() function is called to initiate the Ajax request for loading information about an individual stock. The currently selected stock is passed as the only argument into this function.

The loadStockList() function does nothing more than send an Ajax request to grab the contents of the stocks.xml file:

function loadStockList() {
  // Send the Ajax request to load the new stock list
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "stocks.xml", handleStockListRequest);
}

The key thing to notice in this code is the name of the request handler, which is handleStockListRequest(). This function is responsible for handling the Ajax request upon completion. Here’s the code for it:

function handleStockListRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Store the XML response data
    var xmlData = request.responseXML.getElementsByTagName(
      "stocks")[0];

    // Isolate the data for the current stock type
    var listData = xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
      document.getElementById('stocktype').options[
      document.getElementById('stocktype').selectedIndex].
      value);

    // Clear the stock list
    var stockSel = document.getElementById('stock');
    while (stockSel.length > 0)
      stockSel.remove(0);

    // Fill the stock list with stock symbols
    for (var i = 0; i  < listData[0].childNodes.length; i++) {
      if (listData[0].childNodes[i].nodeType == 1) {
        var symbol = getText(listData[0].childNodes[i]);
        stockSel.add(new Option(symbol, symbol, false, false),
          null);
      }
    }

    // Select the first stock in the list
    stockSel.selectedIndex = 0;

    // Load the info for the first stock in the list
    loadStockInfo(stockSel[0].value);
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

I realize this is a fairly hefty function, but it’s really not all that complicated in terms of what it’s actually doing. Refer back to the stocks.xml file to understand what kind of data this function is processing. The objective of the function is to drill down into the stocks.xml data and grab the list of stocks associated with a given stock type (hot, cold, or safe). More specifically, this list of stocks appears within a given stock type tag (<hot>, <cold>, or <safe>).

After initially retrieving the XML data from the server, the handleStockListRequest() function whittles down the XML data to a particular stock type. As an example, the subset of XML data for hot stocks looks like this:

<hot>
  <stock>AAPL</stock>
  <stock>LUV</stock>
  <stock>GOOG</stock>
  <stock>FDX</stock>
  <stock>TM</stock>
</hot>

Now the code is just about ready to cycle through the list of stocks within the <hot> tag to fill the selection list with hot stocks. Before it can do so, however, the list must be cleared to ensure that the previously displayed stocks are gone. After the stock list is cleared, it is then filled with stocks in the remaining XML data. For this example, this means that five stocks are added to the stock selection list. After the stock list is filled, the selection is set to the first stock in the new list.

This is where things get kind of interesting, because you’ve filled the stock list dynamically but you don’t yet have any stock information to display. You need to display information for the first stock in the new list. This is accomplished by the call to the loadStockInfo() function, which accepts the value of the first stock as its only argument. This function call results in the initiation of the second Ajax request, whose job is to get information for a specified stock.

Following is the code for the loadStockInfo() function:

function loadStockInfo(stock) {
  // Clear the previous stock info text
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockname"),
    "Loading...");
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockprice"), "");
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockcap"), "");
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockvolume"), "");
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockchange"), "");
  document.getElementById("stockchange").style.color = "#000000";
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockpe"), "");
  replaceText(document.getElementById("stockdatetime"), "");

  // Send the Ajax request to load the new stock info
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "quoter.php?symbol=" +
    encodeURIComponent(stock), handleStockInfoRequest);
}

Other than clearing the stock display elements on the page to prepare them for new stock information, this function is about as minimal as loadStockList(). Notice that the stock name div element is set to "Loading..." so that the user knows data is in the process of being delivered. All of the other elements are blanked out until the request finishes. Upon completion, the stock information Ajax request gets handled by the handleStockInfoRequest() function, which looks like this:

function handleStockInfoRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Store the XML response data
    var xmlData = request.responseXML.getElementsByTagName(
      "Stock")[0];

    // Display the detailed stock info
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockname"),
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Name")[0]) +
      " (" +
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Symbol")[0]) +
      ")");
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockprice"),
      "$" + getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Last")[0]));
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockcap"),
      "$" + getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("MktCap")[0]));
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockvolume"),
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Volume")[0]) +
      " shares");
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockchange"),
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
        "PercentageChange")[0]));
    if (getText(
      xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
      "PercentageChange")[0]).charAt(0) == '+')
      document.getElementById("stockchange").style.color = "#009900";
    else if (getText(
      xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
      "PercentageChange")[0]).charAt(0) == '-')
      document.getElementById("stockchange").style.color = "#990000";
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockpe"),
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("P-E")[0]));
    replaceText(document.getElementById("stockdatetime"),
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Time")[0]) + " - " +
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Date")[0]));
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

Remember that this function is handling XML data in the following format:

<string>
  <StockQuotes>
    <Stock>
      <Symbol>SBUX</Symbol>
      <Last>36.29</Last>
      <Date>1/17/2007</Date>
      <Time>2:41pm</Time>
      <Change>+0.07</Change>
      <Open>36.26</Open>
      <High>36.52</High>
      <Low>36.20</Low>
      <Volume>4327880</Volume>
      <MktCap>N/A</MktCap>
      <PreviousClose>36.22</PreviousClose>
      <PercentageChange>+0.19%</PercentageChange>
      <AnnRange>28.72 - 40.01</AnnRange>
      <Earns>0.00</Earns>
      <P-E>N/A</P-E>
      <Name>STARBUCKS CP</Name>
    </Stock>
  </StockQuotes>
</string>

With this data format in mind, you can more easily see that the JavaScript code is navigating through the XML data and plucking out relevant pieces of information based upon the XML tag name. For example, the following two lines of code are used to grab the current stock price and set it as the value of the stock price span element:

replaceText(document.getElementById("stockprice"),
  "$" + getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("Last")[0]));

This code not only retrieves the stock price from the <Last> tag in the XML code, but also appends a dollar sign ($) to the value to clarify that it is a monetary value.

Another interesting bit of code in the handleStockInfoRequest() function relates to how the percentage change of the stock is displayed. The percentage is colored green or red to more clearly indicate whether the percentage change is a gain or loss. Here’s the code that handles the color tweaking based upon the value of the percentage change:

if (getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
  "PercentageChange")[0]).charAt(0) == '+')
  document.getElementById("stockchange").style.color = "#009900";
else if (getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName(
  "PercentageChange")[0]).charAt(0) == '-')
  document.getElementById("stockchange").style.color = "#990000";

This code looks for a plus sign (+) or minus sign (-) preceding the percentage-change value as the basis for changing the color of the stockchange element to green (#009900) or red (#990000).

That finishes up the code for the Picker Web page, which is admittedly a little heftier than earlier examples. However, keep in mind that most of the JavaScript code is related to setting the various stock information elements.
