If you wanted to simplify the application to just show the current stock price, the JavaScript code would get a lot smaller. But what fun would that be?

The Quoter Server Script

Earlier in the chapter I explained how the Picker application requires a script on the server side to sidestep the limitation of not being able to make a direct Ajax client request on a third-party domain. The third-party domain is important because the Picker application relies on it for serving up live stock information. It is possible to use a PHP script as somewhat of a security proxy by accepting an Ajax request on behalf of the client, retrieving the stock data from the third-party server, and then responding to the client with the data. In this way, the Ajax request itself is made purely with respect to your own domain, and is therefore within the security limitations of Ajax.

The third-party Web service I’m talking about is webserviceX.net, which offers several XML-based data feeds. One of these feeds is a stock quote feed where you specify a ticker symbol and the server responds with an XML document containing detailed information about the stock. You’ve already seen the code within this XML document a couple of times throughout the chapter, but you haven’t seen the PHP script that makes it possible to receive the code via an Ajax request. Following is the code for the quoter.php server script:

<?php
header('Content-Type: text/xml');

// Load the XML stock quote data from the server
$xml = html_entity_decode(file_get_contents(
  "http://www.webservicex.net/stockquote.asmx/GetQuote?symbol="
  . $_REQUEST['symbol']));

//
Return the XML stock quote data
echo $xml;
?>

Remember that the job of this script is to retrieve an XML document from the stock quote Web service at webserviceX.net, and then simply pass that data along to the client without any modifications. It is then up to the client to do something useful with the XML code, such as display portions of it on the page.

The server script begins by making sure that the response gets treated as XML. The second chunk of code in the script then loads the XML document from the stock quote Web service, making sure to pass along the symbol parameter so that the Web service knows which stock to look up. The last section of code is where the XML data is actually returned from the script, which serves as the response to the Ajax stock information request.

Before moving on to test the completed application, it’s worth taking a quick moment to test the PHP script by itself. You saw how this is done in the previous chapter with the Booker application, but here’s a sample URL for testing the Quoter script:

http://www.yourdomain.com/quoter.php?symbol=SBUX

Assuming the quoter.php file is stored on your server, change the domain to match yours in this line of code and then enter the URL into your Web browser. You can also try this trick directly on the live Linux CD that accompanies this book. Either way, Figure 4.5 shows the XML response data generated by the PHP script.

Figure 4.5. You can examine the XML stock information returned by the Quoter PHP script by opening the script directly in a browser.

Feel free to experiment with different stock symbols in the URL for the Quoter script. This is a great way to study the data returned by a server script to make sure it is consistent with what you expected—it’s much tougher to diagnose server script problems when all you’re looking at is the client Web page.

Testing Picker

The Picker application is now all assembled and ready for a test. To try it out in your own browser, open the picker.html page in the chap04 folder on the accompanying live Linux CD. Figure 4.6 shows the application running with a stock selected from the initial list of hot stocks.

Figure 4.6. The Picker application begins by initially selecting the first stock type as well as the first stock in the stock list.

The figure shows how Google is initially displayed as the first hot stock. To see the dynamic population of the selection list in action, select a different stock type from the first list. Figure 4.7 shows the application with Cold Stocks selected from the list.

Figure 4.7. Selecting a different stock type results in the dynamic reloading of the stock list as well as new stock information being displayed.

Despite being a chocolate lover, I put the Hershey Company on the cold stock list. Because this stock is the first one in the list, its information is automatically loaded and displayed below the selection lists.

The last test for the application is to try choosing a different stock from the stock list. Figure 4.8 shows the application with a different stock (Amgen) selected in the cold stock list.

Figure 4.8. When you select a different stock, the stock list isn’t reloaded, but new stock information is displayed on the page.

You can see that the Picker application is deceptively simple in that the user interface is very basic and not a whole lot is going on visually. Behind the scenes, however, you know better. Two Ajax requests are made for every selection change in the stock type list, and one request is made each time you select a different stock in the stock list. Like any good Ajax application, the benefits are subtle, with a focus on improving the feel and responsiveness of traditional Web pages.

Giving Picker an Extreme Ajax Makeover

The Picker application does a pretty good job of presenting a dynamic user interface for selecting stock recommendations and then providing hard data to go along with each stock selection. The two-tiered selection list design of the application could certainly have benefits beyond investing. One idea I like involves using such an application to keep up with a collection of some kind, such as a movie collection.

When you think about the Picker application in a more abstract sense, you start to get a feel for how it could be applied to other types of data. In the case of a movie collection, the stock types could be converted to movie types, such as action, horror, comedy, etc. Within each movie type you would list your individual movies using a similar XML coding approach. However, in this case there is no established standard for uniquely representing a movie in shorthand as there is with stock ticker symbols. Instead, you may just want to go ahead and list the entire movie name because coming up with a “ticker symbol” for each movie would probably be difficult.

The primary challenge in creating a movie collection application based upon the Picker code is finding a Web service that can deliver movie information along the lines of how webserviceX.net delivers stock information in Picker. As it turns out, there is a Web service out there that makes the movie collection application a very tangible prospect with minimal work. Take a look at the following URL to see what I’m talking about:

http://www.trynt.com/movie-imdb-api/v2/?t=Raising%20Arizona

This Web service is called the TRYNT Movie IMDb Web Service, and it provides you with programmatic access to the popular Internet Movie Database (IMDb). If you’ve never explored the IMDb, feel free to do so at http://www.imdb.com/. Quite a few movie arguments have been resolved by that Web site! For more information about TRYNT, check out http://www.trynt.com/.

Getting back to the movie Web service, following is the XML data returned by the Raising Arizona URL you just saw:

<trynt>
  <movie-imdb>
    <search>Raising Arizona</search>
    <matched-id>tt0093822</matched-id>
    <matched-url>http://www.imdb.com/title/tt0093822/
    </matched-url>
    <matched-title>Raising Arizona (1987)</matched-title>
    <data>
       <movie-id>
         <data>
           <id>tt0093822</id>
           <score>6</score>
         </data>
       </movie-id>
       <movie-title>
         <data>
           <id>Raising Arizona (1987)</id>
           <score>6</score>
         </data>
       </movie-title>
    </data>
  </movie-imdb>
</trynt>

Granted, there isn’t a whole lot of useful information other than the year of the movie, which is unfortunately contained in the same field with the name, thus making it a bit tougher to extract. However, what is incredibly useful in this code is the link to the movie in the IMDb. This means you could dynamically build a link to the movie as part of the movie information displayed on the movie collection page. Making your application one click away from just about anything a person could want to know about a movie is quite a slick addition to the application.

To transform the Picker application into a movie collection application, it definitely wouldn’t be a bad idea to redesign the XML tags in the stocks.xml file to make sense for a movies.xml file. Just keep in mind that every tag reference in the Web page would also need to be modified. Before you start ripping apart code, however, it might be worth taking a stab at altering the application purely by plugging movie data into the existing framework. Don’t forget to change the first selection list in the Web page of the application so that it lists movie types instead of stock types.

Summary

This chapter presented you with the first sample application that makes multiple Ajax requests to different URLs. Fortunately, the requests occur back-to-back, not simultaneously; otherwise, the Ajax Toolkit would need to be modified to allow for multiple requests taking place at the same time. The Picker application is also a good example of how you can get around the limitation of not being able to make an Ajax request on a domain other than where the request originates. By putting this responsibility on a server script, you enable the client Web page to ask the server script to do the work of talking to the third-party server. In using a third-party Web service, the Picker application is a great example of how you can blend your own XML data (a list of stocks) with data from somewhere else (live stock quotes) that would be difficult if not impossible to come by otherwise.

Chapter 5. Tapping into RSS News Feeds

Ajax in Ancient Greece

Not to lessen the significance of asynchronous Web applications, but Ajax at one point in time had a much more significant meaning. I’m referring to ancient Greece, where a legendary Greek hero named Ajax figured heavily into the Trojan War. Ajax’s legendary strength and courage were chronicled by the Greek poet Homer in the epic poem the Iliad.

As someone who has created and maintained numerous Web sites over the years, I have to say I was quite excited the first time I experimented with news feeds. They allow you to syndicate content from other places, and you can incorporate them into your own pages, with the net effect being that your pages update and stay refreshed automatically—or at least the news feeds do. So unless you have a knack and desire for painstakingly reporting the news, you may find that a relevant news feed injected into your site can provide interesting content that is automatically refreshed. And with a tiny amount of research, you can likely find a feed perfectly matched to your content.

This chapter shows how Ajax makes it possible to dynamically query a news feed for news items and then display these items on a page. The sample application you explore in this chapter provides a simple interface for selecting from multiple news services, in which case the list of news items updates to reflect the selected source. Almost as cool as the dynamic retrieval of news items via Ajax is the slick little “loading” image displayed while the Ajax request is being carried out.

The following files are used by the News Feeder application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap05 example code folder:

  • newsfeeder.html—Main Web page

  • newsfeeder.php—Server script for loading a news feed

  • newsfeeder.css—Style sheet for formatting news feeds

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • newspaper.gif—Newspaper icon that is displayed next to each news item

  • wait.gif—Animated “loading” icon that is displayed during an Ajax request

The Challenge: Querying News Feeds Dynamically

The challenge in this chapter is to use Ajax to dynamically query a news feed Web service for data (news items), and then display those news items on the page. More specifically, the news items are the titles of news stories, so what you actually need to display are news titles that serve as links to each individual news story. As it turns out, each news item in a news feed includes both a title and a link, among other things, so the news feed provides all the data you need to meet the challenge.

Before going any deeper into the challenge itself, we need to first take a moment to get up to speed on news feeds and how they fit into the Web equation. The technology that makes most news feeds tick is RSS. This technology allows you to syndicate Web content, which means you can subscribe to Web sites and easily find out about new publications to a site without actually having to visit it. Although there have been several interpretations of the RSS acronym, the latest and perhaps most accurate is Really Simple Syndication. RSS news feeds are actually XML documents that encode news information; you learn more about the specific format of RSS news feeds a bit later in the chapter.

Note

The history of RSS and its different versions is fairly complex, so suffice it to say that RSS 2.0 is the latest and most commonly used version of RSS. You may also see news feeds in the Atom format, which is very similar to RSS 2.0.

Using special software called a news aggregator, you can monitor RSS news feeds from multiple sites and effectively keep tabs on a wide range of information without having to stop by every different site on a regular basis. You can think of RSS as providing somewhat of a “stock ticker” for Web content—it allows you to keep constant tabs on when your favorite sites post something new.

Although they aren’t as full-featured as standalone news aggregator applications, most modern Web browsers offer built-in support for viewing news feeds. Figure 5.1 shows the main Wired News feed as viewed in the Firefox browser.

Figure 5.1. You can view an RSS news feed directly in most Web browsers without any special plug-ins or software.

Of course, viewing a news feed directly in a Web browser isn’t quite the same as embedding a feed into your own pages. This brings us back to the challenge at hand—using Ajax to dynamically load and display a feed in a page. Because each news feed has its own unique URL, the Ajax part of the challenge is actually very straightforward—it involves retrieving an entire XML document from a URL. This means you don’t have to do any special processing on the news data on the server side of the application, which simplifies things considerably.

Because the Ajax request for this application is so straightforward, we’ll jazz up the user interface a little by giving the user a visual cue that the data is being loaded. This is also important because some news feeds contain enough information that there can be a noticeable delay in receiving the data. Even a delay of a few seconds can be confusing if you don’t know what’s going on. Therefore, a slick way to deal with the problem is to display a “loading” image while the request is taking place.

One last part of the challenge is to give the user the option of choosing from several news feeds. Not only does this provide a good way to stress-test the application with different data sets, but it also makes the application more flexible from the user’s perspective. Of course, you may opt to pick a single feed and forego the selection approach when incorporating RSS into your own pages. Either way, the News Feeder sample application serves as a great starting point for your RSS Ajax adventures.

The Design: Designing a News Feed Application

The design of the News Feeder application begins with the Ajax request that shuttles data from a news feed server to the application. This request begins with a URL for the feed server, which then returns an XML document containing detailed information about the news feed, including a list of individual news items with links and publish dates. The Ajax request is triggered by the user selecting a news feed from a selection list.

Given that each news feed has its own URL that is used as the basis for the Ajax request, you need a way to tie a feed URL to a feed name for the purposes of allowing the user to select a feed. The value attribute of the <option> tag is the perfect place for storing the URL for a feed, and it allows you to build a news feed selection list with ease.

The news feed selection list serves as a major component of the News Feeder user interface. This interface consists of two parts: the news feed selection list and an area for displaying the feed data once it is successfully loaded. Feed data in this case consists of the feed title and a listing of individual feed items that serve as links to each news story. Figure 5.2 shows how the News Feeder user interface factors into the news feed Ajax request.

Figure 5.2. The user interface for the News Feeder application consists of a selection list and an area containing the news feed title and individual news item links.

Earlier I mentioned that the application should make use of an animated image to let the user know when an Ajax request is busy downloading news feed data. Figure 5.3 shows what this animated image, wait.gif, looks like.

Figure 5.3. The News Feeder application makes use of an animated “loading” image (wait.gif) to let the user know the Ajax request is being processed.

Granted, it’s hard to make out the animation on the printed page, but the movement of the image gives users a sense that something is taking place and that they’re not just staring at a frozen page. Now is the time when you might question the idea of displaying a “loading” image in an asynchronous application. Isn’t the whole point of Ajax to not ever make the user wait on something? Yes, but keep in mind that the application actually isn’t frozen during the request—the user is free to select a different news feed, in which case the current feed request is interrupted and the new feed is loaded. The animated image is just there to let the user know that some kind of work is being done in the background.

You should now have a good enough feel for the application’s general design to dig a little deeper into the Ajax request and response.

The Client Request

Without any security limitations, the client request in the News Feeder application would be the most predictable part of the Ajax equation because it would simply perform a GET request on a URL with no special parameters or anything else. However, it isn’t possible to make an Ajax request directly on a remote Web server, which means you must create your own server script to accept the Ajax request and fulfill it.

All that is required to obtain news feed data is the URL of a news feed Web server. Therefore, the News Feeder client request must send along the URL of the news feed to your server script, which then handles getting the data and returning it in an Ajax response. The key to all this is identifying suitable RSS news feeds, which means finding their URLs.

If you start to pay attention to the Web sites you visit, you might see a “syndicate” link or possibly even an RSS icon somewhere near the main menu or navigation links. This is how you find the URL for a news feed, and this URL is all you need to load the news feed via Ajax.

Note

Most content-driven Web sites such as news Web sites offer numerous news feeds that are specific to different interests. For example, USA Today offers over 30 different sports feeds ranging from college hockey to horse racing. To access them, just scroll to the bottom of the main USA Today Web page (http://www.usatoday.com/) and click the little orange XML icon. This icon is often used instead of the newer RSS icon.

The Server Response

On the server side of the News Feeder application, the server is responsible only for delivering an XML document containing the news feed information in the RSS data format, which is an XML format. Unlike some of the other examples in the book, which rely on a server PHP script in order to somehow process or manipulate data before returning it in an Ajax response, the News Feeder application only requires a PHP script to get around the inherent limitation of not being able to issue a client request on a third-party domain. This is a security restriction that prevents client-side JavaScript code from venturing beyond the domain from which it is served.

The News Feeder application therefore does need a PHP script on the server, but its only role is to grab news feed data from a remote server and then shuttle it back to the client via an Ajax response. You can think of the server script for News Feeder as a proxy in that it serves as the middle man between the client JavaScript code and the remote server that actually hosts the news feed. Again, the key is that your Ajax code can only make requests to your server, which is a big problem when you need to request data from somewhere else. The solution is to allow a server script to retrieve the data, and then have that script serve as the basis for responding to Ajax requests.

The third-party (remote) servers I keep talking about can be any server that sources data in the RSS format. You’ll find that if you do a little exploring, most major Web sites offer news feeds in the RSS format. Just take note of the URL in your browser when you find a news feed—this is the URL you’ll plug into your code when making an RSS Ajax request.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

If you put together what you’ve learned about the communication that takes place within the context of the News Feeder application, you get a flow of data as shown in Figure 5.4.

Figure 5.4. The News Feeder application receives XML news feed data in response to making a request with a news feed URL.

The data sent to the server as a parameter of the Ajax request is the URL of the news feed data source. Although this data is always in the form of a URL, it can take several forms. For example, following is the URL for the news feed associated with the Scientific American Web site: http://www.sciam.com/xml/sciam.xml.

Notice that this URL references a specific XML document, sciam.xml. This makes sense given that RSS feeds are actually XML documents, but news feed URLs don’t always reference XML files. As an example, the following URL is for the Wired News feed: http://feeds.wired.com/wired/topheadlines.

In this example, the URL is a path as opposed to a filename. The bottom line is that you can’t always predict the exact form of a news feed URL. The good news is that you can usually just take and use the syndication URL as provided to you by a Web site.

Once you know the URL of a news feed Web server, it needs to get packaged into a larger URL for submission to the News Feeder server script as part of an Ajax request. Here’s an example of how this is done:

newsfeeder.php?rssurl=http://www.sciam.com/xml/sciam.xml

This code reveals how the news feed URL is packaged into a URL as a single parameter (rssurl). This entire URL is used as the basis for an Ajax request, in which case your server script, newsfeeder.php, will take the rssurl parameter and use the URL it contains to obtain the feed information.

Speaking of the server script, you now understand that it receives the news feed URL via the rssurl parameter and then uses the URL to retrieve an RSS document. This document is then passed back to the client JavaScript code through an Ajax response. Following is an example of what such RSS code actually looks like:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<rss version="2.0" xmlns:dc="http://purl.org/dc/elements/1.1/">
<channel>
  <title>Scientific American - Official RSS Feed</title>
  <link>http://www.sciam.com/</link>
  <image>
    <url>http://www.sciam.com/media/logo/SAlogo_144px.gif</url>
    <title>Scientific American.com</title>
     <link>http://www.sciam.com/</link>
    <width>144</width>
    <height>45</height>
  </image>
  <description>Science news and technology updates from
    Scientific American</description>
  <language>en-us</language>
  <managingEditor>editors@sciam.com</managingEditor>
    <dc:publisher>Scientific American</dc:publisher>
  <copyright>Copyright 1996-2007 Scientific American</copyright>
  <item>
    <title>News: Snake Bites the Toxic Toad That Feeds It—and
      Spreads Its Poison</title>
    <link>http://www.sciam.com/article.cfm?chanID=sa003&amp;
      articleID=6FD96483-E7F2-99DF-3C8A12ACF6BB25D8&amp;
      ref=rss</link>
    <guid>http://www.sciam.com/article.cfm?chanID=sa003&amp;
      articleID=6FD96483-E7F2-99DF-3C8A12ACF6BB25D8&amp;
      ref=rss</guid>
    <author>JR Minkel &lt;editors@sciam.com&gt;</author>
    <dc:date>2007-01-29T17:00:00-05:00</dc:date>
  </item>
  <item>
    <title>News: Did Honest Abe Have Nerves of Glass?</title>
    <link>http://www.sciam.com/article.cfm?chanID=sa003&amp;
      articleID=6FD6E124-E7F2-99DF-3E0EDA7A34E03A06&amp;
      ref=rss</link>
    <guid>http://www.sciam.com/article.cfm?chanID=sa003&amp;
      articleID=6FD6E124-E7F2-99DF-3E0EDA7A34E03A06&amp;
      ref=rss</guid>
    <author>Nikhil Swaminathan &lt;editors@sciam.com&gt;</author>
    <dc:date>2007-01-29T17:00:00-05:00</dc:date>
  </item>
...
</channel>
</rss>

At first glance this code looks a little threatening, but much of it can be ignored for our purposes. Keep in mind that the challenge of the News Feeder application is to display a list of news items that link to the actual news stories. You can extract this data from the XML document and ignore a lot of the other details. To see what I’m talking about, look through the code and find an <item> tag. Now look immediately below that tag and find tags called <title>, <link>, and <dc:date>. The code looks like this:

...
  <item>
    <title>News: Snake Bites the Toxic Toad That Feeds It—and
      Spreads Its Poison</title>
    <link>http://www.sciam.com/article.cfm?chanID=sa003&amp;
      articleID=6FD96483-E7F2-99DF-3C8A12ACF6BB25D8&amp;
      ref=rss</link>
...
    <dc:date>2007-01-29T17:00:00-05:00</dc:date>
  </item>
...

The <title> tag stores a single news item title, whereas the <link> tag stores its associated link. The <dc:date> tag can actually be thought of simply as the <date> tag because the dc part is a namespace prefix and not technically part of the tag name. Most RSS feeds actually use the <pubDate> tag to encode the publish date instead of the <date> tag, so it would be wise to factor in both options when processing the XML data. It’s also important to consider the possibility of a news item not having either of these tags because the publish date is optional in RSS documents. The bottom line to all of this RSS tag talk is that you can drill through the code, extracting specific tags and their content to build a news item list without too much trouble.

Note

The reason why two different tags are used to encode the publish date in RSS news feeds is complicated and has to do with competing specifications and the fact that the RSS format has evolved rapidly. Rather than give you a boring and unnecessary history lesson of RSS, I’ll suffice it to say that you will likely encounter the <pubDate> tag much more often than <date> (<dc:date>), but it is still worth supporting both because a few prominent sites do use <date>.

Of course, you also need to obtain the name of the news feed, and perhaps even a link to its Web site. This information is also contained within <title> and <link> tags, but this time the tags appear beneath the <channel> tag; a distinct news feed is also sometimes referred to as a channel, as in a television channel. So, examining the Scientific American code once more, following is the code containing the news feed title and link:

...
<channel>
  <title>Scientific American - Official RSS Feed</title>
  <link>http://www.sciam.com/</link>
...

To summarize, the following pieces of information must be plucked from the RSS (XML) news feed document for the purposes of the News Feeder application:

  • News feed title (<title> inside of <channel>)

  • News feed link (<link> inside of <channel>)

  • News item titles (<title> inside of <item>)

  • News item links (<link> inside of <item>)

  • News item publish dates (<pubDate> or <date> inside of <item>)

The first two pieces of information only occur once in any given news feed document, whereas the latter two can appear multiple times if there are multiple news items—and there usually are. When there are multiple news items, each of the items has its own <item> tag.

Although I could spend a lot more time revealing the finer points of the RSS data format, I won’t; it’s just not necessary. You have all you need to know to put together the News Feeder application, so let’s get to it!

Putting Together the News Feeder Application

You’re probably ready for the major parts of the News Feeder application: a Web page and a server script. However, there is also a third piece of the puzzle in the form of a CSS style sheet. The style sheet is not absolutely necessary, but it does add a considerably more pleasant look to the news information that is displayed. So, following are the components required for the application:

  • HTML Web page (newsfeeder.html)

  • CSS style sheet (newsfeeder.css)

  • PHP server script (newsfeeder.php)

Keep in mind that a remote server is also involved in this equation for each different news feed URL. This remote server isn’t part of the application in a sense that there are no files or other resources you have to worry about, but it does nonetheless play a crucial role in the functioning of the application. So as you work through the News Feeder application files in the next few sections, understand that they are still dependent upon a remote server for getting the actual news feed data.

The News Feeder Web Page

The News Feeder Web page (newsfeeder.html) is undoubtedly the most significant part of the application because it is responsible for making sense of the RSS data sent back from the PHP server script and displaying this data in a meaningful fashion. As revealed in Figure 5.5, this page contains a news feed selection list followed by a div element that contains the linked title of the currently selected news feed, along with a list of each linked news item.

Figure 5.5. The News Feeder Web page consists of a news feed selection list followed by information related to the currently selected feed.

The HTML code establishes the user interface for the News Feeder Web page in the <body> tag, whose code follows:

<body onload="loadFeed()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>Choose your news:
    <select id="feed" onchange="loadFeed()">
      <option value="http://feeds.wired.com/wired/topheadlines">
        Wired News</option>
      <option value=
        "http://feeds.engadget.com/weblogsinc/engadget">
        Engadget</option>
     <option value=
       "http://feeds.ziffdavis.com/ziffdavis/extremetech">
       ExtremeTech</option>
     <option value=
       "http://feeds.feedburner.com/ajaxian">Ajaxian</option>
     <option value="http://www.sciam.com/xml/sciam.xml">
       Scientific American</option>
     <option value="http://news.google.com/?output=rss">
       Google News</option>
     <option value=
       "http://www.michaelmorrison.com/mambo/index2.php?
       option=com_rss&feed=RSS2.0&no_html=1">
       Michael Morrison's Blog</option>
   </select>
 </div>
 <br />
 <div id="feedcontent"></div>
</body>

If you exclude the selection list created by the <select> tag, you’ll see that this code is quite minimal. In fact, there are really only two visual elements on the page: the selection list (feed) and a div element (feedcontent) used to display the dynamically loaded news feed information.

One piece of code that shouldn’t get overlooked in the Web page body is the onload event handler, which is responsible for loading feed data for the first news feed in the selection list with a call to the loadFeed() function. Before you see the loadFeed() function, it’s worth pointing out that you can fully customize the News Feeder application with news feeds you’ve found without dealing with any of the other code in the page. In other words, you can completely customize the application without looking at any of the remaining JavaScript code—just add new <option> tags or modify the existing <option> tags with different news feed titles and URLs.

If you want to go a bit deeper and understand how things work behind the scenes of the application, then of course you’ll have to dig into the JavaScript code. Following is the code for the loadFeed() function, which is responsible for initiating the Ajax request that loads news feed data:

function loadFeed() {
  // Clear the previous feed content
  document.getElementById("feedcontent").innerHTML =
    "<img src='wait.gif' alt='Loading...' />";

  // Send the Ajax request to load the new feed content
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "newsfeeder.php?rssurl=" +
    encodeURIComponent(document.getElementById('feed').options[
    document.getElementById('feed').selectedIndex].value),
    handleFeedRequest);
}

The first thing the loadFeed() function does is one of the most interesting features of the application, and that is display the animated “loading” image (wait.gif) that appears while the request is being processed. This image was shown back in Figure 5.3, but it makes much more sense when seen in context—check out Figure 5.6.

Figure 5.6. An animated “loading” image is used to let the user know that a news feed Ajax request is being processed.

Getting back to the loadFeed() code, the “loading” image is displayed by changing the div with the ID of feedcontent so that it contains an <img> tag. This change is accomplished by setting the innerHTML property of the div element. You can use the innerHTML property to dynamically alter the content of an element on the page. You see this property used again in just a moment in the code that displays the news feed title and news items in the same feed content div.

The last chunk of code in the loadFeed() function takes care of issuing the Ajax request. This code builds a URL for communicating with the PHP server script, newsfeeder.php, including the rssurl parameter that contains the news feed URL. The Ajax handler function is set to handleFeedRequest(), and it is this function that does the heavy lifting in the application.

Note

At this point I have to let you in on a little secret—this is the first time in the book I’ve used the innerHTML property to change the content in a Web page. I feel a little guilty because this property isn’t officially endorsed by the World Wide Web Consortium (W3C) as a Web standard, making its usage somewhat questionable. However, it is incredibly handy and is used in countless Web sites by expert developers, making it unlikely this property will go away any time soon. The alternative is to modify content in the page purely through DOM Application Programming Interface (API) functions. This latter technique is preferred, at least from a standards perspective, but in most cases it is more complex. I opted to err on the side of simplicity, and thus used the innerHTML property. If you want to learn more about the innerHTML versus DOM API debate, just do a Web search on “innerHTML” and “DOM.” You won’t find a shortage of strong opinions!

Before getting into the code for the handleFeedRequest() function, there is one other little piece of code worth addressing. I’m talking about the code just below the loadFeed() function that preloads the wait.gif image:

var waitImage = new Image();
waitImage.src = "wait.gif";

The significance of this code is that you don’t want any noticeable delay in loading the “loading” image. The whole point of the image is to let the user know that something meaningful is going on behind the scenes. So, we get the ball rolling as quickly as possible by preloading the image before the page is even rendered. This works because the image preloading code appears outside of any other JavaScript functions, which means that it is automatically run when the page is loaded.

Getting back to the handleFeedRequest() function, its job is to react to an Ajax response by picking through the RSS data returned and then displaying meaningful news feed information. Following is the code for this function:

function handleFeedRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Store the XML response data
    var xmlData = request.responseXML;

    // Generate the feed content by starting with the feed title
    var feedContent = "";
    var channelElem = xmlData.getElementsByTagName("channel")[0];
    feedContent += "<div class='feedtitle'><a href='" +
      getText(channelElem.getElementsByTagName("link")[0]) +
      "'>" + getText(channelElem.getElementsByTagName(
      "title")[0]) + "</a></div>";

    // Now generate the list of feed items as an unordered list
    feedContent += "<ul>";
    var feedItems = channelElem.getElementsByTagName("item");
    for (var i = 0; i < feedItems.length; i++) {
      var itemTitle =
getText(feedItems[i].getElementsByTagName("title")[0]);
      var itemLink = getText(feedItems[i].getElementsByTagName(
        "link")[0]);
      var itemPubDate =
getText(feedItems[i].getElementsByTagName("pubDate")[0]);
      if (itemPubDate == "")
        itemPubDate = getText(feedItems[i].getElementsByTagName(
          "date")[0]);
      if (itemPubDate == "")
        feedContent += "<li><a href='" + itemLink + "'>" +
          itemTitle + "</a></li>";
      else
        feedContent += "<li><a href='" + itemLink + "'>" +
          itemTitle + " <span class='itemdate'>(" +
          itemPubDate + ")</span></a></li>";
    }
    feedContent += "</ul>";

    // Display the feed content
    document.getElementById("feedcontent").innerHTML = feedContent;
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

Admittedly, there is a fair amount of code here, but it isn’t too terribly difficult to figure out. Just keep in mind that it is basically constructing a chunk of HTML code on the fly. In other words, the function is building a portion of a Web page so that it can be inserted into the feed content div for display. The generated HTML code contains the title of the news feed, which also happens to serve as a link to the news source Web site. The HTML code also contains an unordered (bulleted) list of news items that each consists of a linked title and a publish date. It’s important to note that the generated HTML code contains a couple CSS style sheet class references, which helps the style sheet know which content it is applied to.

Near the end of the handleFeedRequest() function you see a line of code that finally inserts the dynamically generated HTML code into the page:

document.getElementById("feedcontent").innerHTML = feedContent;

Although short and sweet, this single line of code does quite a lot when you consider that it is responsible for replacing the “loading” image with the carefully formatted news feed title and news items.

The look of the News Feeder page is provided primarily by the newsfeeder.css style sheet, which is referenced in the Web page head via the following link:

<link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="newsfeeder.css" />

The newsfeeder.css style sheet is located in the same chap05 folder as the rest of the News Feeder source files. The next section quickly examines this style sheet and explains how it impacts the final look of the News Feeder application.

The News Feeder Style Sheet

I’ll be the first to admit that I’m quite reluctant to list CSS style sheet code in a book about Ajax. However, the News Feeder application is an excellent example of how much pizzazz a style sheet can add to an application in a relatively small amount of code. If you don’t believe me, check out Figure 5.7, which shows the News Feeder application without the benefit of the style sheet.

Figure 5.7. When the style sheet is removed, the News Feeder application looks somewhat dull.

Compare this figure to Figure 5.5, which shows the exact same news feed with the benefit of the style sheet. Or just pop in the Live Linux CD that comes with the book and try out the News Feeder example in your own Web browser. The point is that even though CSS has nothing directly to do with Ajax, it can make just about any Ajax application look significantly more appealing and professional.

Following is the CSS code responsible for styling the news feed title in the News Feeder application:

div.feedtitle {
  padding-left : 5px;
  background-color : #EEEEEE;
  font-size : 22px;
  font-weight : bold;
}

div.feedtitle a {
  color : #666677;
  text-decoration : none;
}

div.feedtitle a:hover {
  color : #333344;
  text-decoration : none;
}

The news feed title is generated in HTML code that has a class attribute of feedtitle, which is what links the code to this group of styles. The left-side padding, background color, font size, and font weight are first established for the title as a whole. Then the color of the text is set, along with a slightly different color for when the user hovers the mouse over the title (it is a link, if you recall). The text-decoration style is used to override the default HTML behavior of underlining a link when the user hovers over it—changing the font color on a hover is a bit cleaner and more professional looking in this case.

Similar CSS styles are applied to the individual news items that appear in the unordered list:

li {
  font-size : 18px;
  padding-left : 25px;
  background : url(newspaper.gif) no-repeat;
  list-style : none;
}

This is the code responsible for the neat little newspaper icons that appear next to each item in the list (refer to Figure 5.5). Each list item is also a link, so it makes sense to apply the same general “link hover” coloring concept to the news items as was done for the feed title. Here’s the CSS code for it:

a:link {
  color : #333344;
  text-decoration : none;
}

a:visited {
  color : #9999AA;
  text-decoration : none;
}

a:hover, a:active {
  background : #EEEEEE;
  text-decoration : none;
}

Finally, you might have noticed that the publish date for each news item appears in italics and in a very light color so as not to compete with the title. Following is the CSS style responsible for the look of the publish dates of the news items:

span.itemdate {
  font-style : italic;
  color : #CCCCDD;
}

That wraps up the style sheet for the News Feeder application. All that’s left is the PHP server script and this application will be finished.

The News Feeder Server Script

Have you ever seen a relay event in track and field where one runner hands a baton to another runner? That’s pretty much what the newsfeeder.php server script does for the News Feeder application. It runs off and grabs the news feed RSS data from a remote server, and then hustles back and hands that data off to the client JavaScript code. The server script knows what server to grab the RSS data from, thanks to a URL parameter (rssurl) passed to it in an Ajax request. Following is the code for this PHP script:

<?php
header('Content-type: text/xml;');

// Return the RSS feed data
echo file_get_contents($_REQUEST['rssurl']);
?>

Note

Remember that the purpose of this “proxy” server script is to get around the limitation of not being able to issue Ajax requests on remote servers. If this security limitation didn’t exist, the server script would be unnecessary and the client would just make an Ajax request directly on the news feed URL. However, there is a good reason why remote request permission is limited to servers: security! It would be far too dangerous to give client scripts free reign to initiate remote requests, so having to create your own Ajax proxy is a small price to pay for a safer Web.

After setting the header so that the response is sent as XML, this script really only consists of one line of code. The file_get_contents() function retrieves data from a specified URL, which is exactly the functionality you need. So, all the script does is pass the news feed URL into this function and echo the results back to the client. The end result is a cleanly formatted RSS news feed document that is sent back to the client as a response to the Ajax request.

Testing the News Feeder

When you first open the News Feeder Web page, the application initially loads the first news feed in the selection list, which happens to be the top stories from Wired News. Figure 5.8 shows the application with this news feed selected.

Figure 5.8. The News Feeder application begins by loading the first news feed in the selection list.

When you change to another news feed by selecting a different feed in the selection list, the animated “loading” image is displayed while the Ajax request is made. Figure 5.9 shows the feed being changed from Wired News to Engadget.

Figure 5.9. When you change the news feed, the animated “loading” image is displayed to let you know that the page is working on something in the background.

News feeds aren’t terribly large in size, so it shouldn’t take long at all for the new feed to be displayed. When this does take place, the information for the new feed replaces the “loading” image on the page, as shown in Figure 5.10.

Figure 5.10. When a newly selected news feed finishes loading, the feed information replaces the “loading” image on the page.

It’s important to point out that you have now created a surprisingly handy little news aggregator of your own. What I mean is that once you get past the immediate aspect of how Ajax works in the News Feeder application, you may find that you actually enjoy using the application to obtain news. Just click a news item to follow its link to the complete news story. In this case, clicking a news item leads you to the news story on the Engadget Web site.

In addition to having interesting gadget news, the Engadget Web page is also a great example of how a site with an RSS news feed makes it easy for you to syndicate the feed. Look in the upper-right corner of the Engadget Web page and you’ll see the standard orange icon for RSS. Click this icon and you’ll get the URL for the Engadget news feed, which is a good one I might add!

Giving News Feeder an Extreme Ajax Makeover

No application in this book is ever truly complete. By that I mean that the Sample applications are all meant to be starting points for you to experiment with Ajax on your own. The News Feeder application is no exception, and in fact it is a great example of an application that is extremely customizable.

Perhaps the most obvious way to customize the News Feeder application is to tinker around with the feeds themselves. This is also the simplest customization because all you have to do is manipulate the <option> tags in the Web page, changing titles and values so that they correspond to other news feeds. Keep in mind that you can add as many new <option> tags as you want, ultimately building a selection list with numerous different news feeds. Just make sure to place the name of the feed between the <option> and </option> tags and then stick its URL in the value attribute.

You may decide that being able to choose from a list of news feeds isn’t exactly what you want, in which case you may elect to simply embed a single news feed in a Web page of your own. In this case, you need to carefully extract the important code from the newsfeeder.html Web page and place it into your page. This primarily involves copying and pasting all of the script code in the head of the page, as well as making sure not to forget the onload event handler for the <body> tag, which calls the loadFeed() function. You also need to make sure you create a div element named feedcontent. And finally, you need to tweak the loadFeed() function slightly so that it uses the news feed URL of your choice instead of grabbing it from the selection list.

If you elect to embed a news feed into a page that has other content, there’s a good chance you will want to limit the number of news items displayed on the page. For example, you may only want to display the first five or ten news items. To limit the number of items shown, change the for loop in the handleFeedRequest() function to this:

for (var i = 0; (i < feedItems.length) && (i < numItems); i++) {

In this code, the numItems variable controls how many news items are displayed. You can either use a variable or just hard-code a number, such as this:

for (var i = 0; (i < feedItems.length) && (i < 10); i++) {

This example limits the number of news items to ten. The for loop still checks the length of the feedItems array just in case the news feed has less than ten items in it, in which case you only want the number of available items displayed.

The final trick you might want to consider now that you have some RSS skills is to create an RSS news feed of your own. Whether you have a blog or some other Web site with content that changes on a regular basis, allowing users to syndicate your site is a neat feature. You’ve hopefully gleaned enough in this chapter about the RSS format to take a stab at creating your own RSS documents. In fact, the ideal solution is to develop a script that automatically converts HTML Web content into RSS form so that your site becomes automatically syndicated. The specifics of that solution are a bit beyond the scope of this chapter, but you now have a great tool to test out your news feeds should you create any.

Summary

This chapter introduced you to an exciting area of Web content management known as news feeds, which use a standard XML-based data format known as RSS (Really Simple Syndication). Using RSS, it is possible to get a snapshot of the changing content on a Web site without actually having to visit the Web site. More importantly, it becomes possible to syndicate content from another Web site and make it available on your Web site. This is a great way to keep your pages fresher without having to always dream up your own content. The News Feeder application you developed in this chapter demonstrated how Ajax makes it possible to dynamically load RSS data and present it on the page cleanly so that news items can be navigated and read. The application contains enough core code so that you can inject RSS news feeds into your own Web pages with little effort.

Chapter 6. Reading the User’s Mind with Auto-Complete

Ajax the Thoroughbred Racehorse

If you had been a gambler in Australia in the 1930s, you might’ve thought of Ajax in a much different light. You could’ve made quite a bundle betting on Ajax the champion Australian thoroughbred racehorse, who is remembered for winning 18 races in a row and was later inducted into the Australian Racing Hall of Fame.

When Ajax first hit the Web scene, one of the most immediately beneficial examples of it was Google Suggest, which uses Ajax to assist you in performing searches by attempting to auto-complete the search text. Google Suggest is a particularly compelling example of Ajax because it is both subtle and powerful—a tough combination to put together. Too many technological Web improvements have ended up being distracting to the Web experience despite their technical merits. Not so with Ajax. Google Suggest’s auto-complete feature is one of many Ajax features that dramatically improve the Web experience without interfering with it.

This chapter guides you through the design and development of an auto-complete application that uses Ajax to dynamically look up user input on the server and assist with a possible input completion. In some ways, you can think of this application as a rudimentary form of artificial Web intelligence because it gives a text input control the ability to guess what the user is typing. The application is both simple and powerful, and is a very handy tool to add to your Ajax arsenal.

The following files are used by the Completer application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap06 example code folder:

  • completer.html—Main Web page

  • completer.php—Server script for looking up auto-completion text

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

The Challenge: Anticipating Data Entry

The challenge in this chapter is to create an application with functionality similar to the auto-complete feature made popular by Google Suggest. Before you go any further, it would be helpful to take a quick look at Google Suggest to see exactly how it works. So point your browser at http://www.google.com/webhp?complete=1&hl=en and try performing a search.

Note

Google Suggest is still considered an experimental project within the Google Labs area of Google, which is why it has such a cryptic URL. You may see this URL change to something more readable if Google Suggest is ever promoted to mainstream use within the Google search engine.

Google Suggest is actually fairly sophisticated in that it dynamically displays a dropdown list from which to choose suggested search phrases. Google Suggest is interesting because it also displays the number of hits for each suggestion, thereby giving you some kind of gauge for the importance of the suggestion. What Google Suggest doesn’t do is manipulate the text field itself in any way, unless of course you choose one of the suggestions from the dropdown list.

The Completer application you develop in this chapter takes a slightly different approach to the auto-complete feature than Google Suggest. To get a feel for how it will work, let’s check out another handy Web site that offers auto-complete, Lyricsfly.com. Feel free to surf on over to http://www.lyricsfly.com/, which is shown in action in Figure 6.1.

Figure 6.1. Lyricsfly.com is a music lyric search engine that uses the auto-complete feature.

In the figure I’ve clicked the Song link to search specifically for songs. Also notice in the figure the major distinction between Lyricsfly.com and Google Suggest—Lyricsfly.com actually adds the suggested text to the text field as it makes suggestions. This means you don’t have to use the dropdown list if you don’t want to. In fact, it means you really don’t need the dropdown list at all if you don’t mind continuing to type and hone in on the song. Granted, the dropdown list is a big help, and it does convey extra information such as the artists, but it isn’t essential to the auto-complete feature. Figure 6.2 reveals how the dropdown list in Lyricsfly.com will eventually resolve to a single song if you continue to type the song name.

Figure 6.2. The more you type into Lyricsfly.com, the less critical the dropdown list becomes.

In this figure I’ve entered enough of the song name to reduce the dropdown list to a single song. Of course, the whole idea is to prevent the user from having to type a lot, but you get the idea that the auto-complete feature could function reasonably well without the dropdown list.

The main point in showing how auto-complete can be implemented without a dropdown list is to set up the challenge for the Completer sample application. There is a fair amount of complexity in dynamically creating and managing a custom dropdown list, and I want to keep this example as clean as possible. You get to Ajax-driven custom popup windows later in the book, but for now I want to stick with a simple user interface without a bunch of tricky overhead code. Therefore, we’ll scrap the dropdown list and create an auto-complete feature purely within a text input control.

This means the application will only be suggesting one word or phrase at any given time, but you’ll see that even a single suggested phrase can go a long way toward making an application more helpful and intuitive.

So the task at hand for the Completer application is to provide an auto-complete feature solely for a text input control. In terms of how this challenge impacts Ajax, the client part of the application is responsible for sending the server the text the user is entering as each character is typed. The server takes a given piece of input text and finds the closest match in its list of possible completions (suggestions). The server then returns the remaining part of the completion to the client so that it can be added to the text input control and selected.

To make the application mildly educational, Completer uses a list of famous historical people as the basis for its auto-complete functionality. This means the user will begin by starting to enter a name, and the application will quickly (ideally) fill in the text field with its best guess as to who the person might be. If the user is happy with the suggestion, he or she doesn’t need to type any more. If not, the user can elect to continue typing and the application will try again with another completion attempt. Unless the user is typing too fast for the Ajax requests to keep up, a request will be made for each individual keypress (key release, actually, but that’s not the point).

In summary, the Completer application issues an Ajax request for each character entered by the user in an attempt to automatically complete the entry of a historical name. Once the name is successfully entered, the user can click a button to view a biography of the historical person on Wikipedia, the popular online encyclopedia. The Wikipedia facet of the application is just a token prize for successfully using Ajax to enter a historical name.

The Design: Designing an Auto-Complete Application

You already have a basic idea as to what is taking place in regard to the data being passed back and forth between the client and server in the Completer application, and you even know that the user interface consists primarily of a text input control. Figure 6.3 shows how the user interface factors into each Ajax request within the application.

Figure 6.3. Each character of user input results in an Ajax request that returns a completion string.

The figure shows how a single text input control serves as both the source and the destination for Ajax requests. Although this may seem unusual at first, it makes perfect sense when you consider that the text input control is playing two roles: It is both accepting input from the user and attempting to give the user information by guessing what is being typed. The key thing to note here is how the completion text in the figure is highlighted (selected), which means that if the user types another character it will overwrite the completion text. This of course triggers another Ajax request with a new response and new completion text. And so the dance continues until the user arrives at the desired input.

One interesting thing to note about the data in the Ajax request for the Completer application is how simple it is. There isn’t any XML, or any data formatting at all for that matter. In fact, you’re really only sending a few characters back and forth to the server at a time. Just so you understand what I mean, let’s step through a quick example involving a notable figure in American history, Thomas Edison. You would start entering this name by typing a t or a T in the text box. At this point the text box contains the following:

T

Because you’ve entered a character, an Ajax request is immediately dispatched and the text ecumseh is returned to the text box to appear along with the T you entered:

Tecumseh

Tecumseh is the famous Shawnee Indian leader who died in the War of 1812. The name Tecumseh is initially suggested by the Completer application because Tecumseh appears before Thomas Edison alphabetically in the list of possible completions. You see this list a bit later in the chapter, but for now you can hopefully see how the alphabetic nature of the list plays into the completion process.

If you continue by typing another letter, h this time, the text ecumseh is immediately replaced by the h, giving you the following:

Th

Another Ajax request is sent out, and this time the text eodore Roosevelt is returned. Geez, are we ever going to get to Thomas Edison? Here’s what the text box now holds:

Theodore Roosevelt

Clearly, Theodore Roosevelt is a step closer to Thomas Edison but still alphabetically before it, which is why this name was suggested. As it turns out, typing one more character is all it takes to finally hone in on Mr. Edison:

Tho

The server receives one more Ajax request and returns mas Edison, resulting in the following text box contents:

Thomas Edison

Now you have your name, a total of 13 characters, but you only had to type three of them. That’s the power of the auto-complete feature. Hopefully this little exercise not only shines some light on the usefulness of the feature but also on the nature of the Ajax requests and responses required to carry it out.

The Client Request

The client request for the Completer application is very simple in that it sends along the user input as it is being entered in the text input control. This user input is just raw characters of text without any special formatting. In many cases, the input is just a handful of characters, although it could be more in cases where there isn’t an auto-complete match and the user continues to type. Even so, you’re talking about tens of characters, not hundreds of characters.

Note

Fortunately, it doesn’t really matter to an Ajax request how many characters you send its way, although the more data sent the longer the request transmission will take. However, you’re still talking about relatively tiny amounts of data in the grand scheme of things.

Before moving on to the server response details, it’s worth addressing a concern that has been brought up regarding Ajax applications such as Completer. I’m referring to the fact that Completer is capable of issuing a flutter of requests in response to the user typing characters. Although the server-side script of Completer is simple and speedy, relying solely on data embedded in the PHP code, an application with a larger data set would likely get the completion text from a database. This means you’re looking at a database hit every time the user types a character, which is potentially a problem.

The problem here is the prospect of lots and lots of database hits overwhelming a database server. This may or may not be a problem for any Ajax applications that you build using the auto-complete feature, but it’s a bit of a warning to make any frequently called Ajax code extremely efficient; that is, minimal processing of minimal data. And if you should still have problems, you may need to explore a server configuration that yields better results for a high frequency of small data requests. Don’t panic just yet—consider this a tiny alert to file away for future reference. Chances are you won’t have any problems if you use the auto-complete feature with a little healthy restraint.

The Server Response

The server script in the Completer application is given the task of matching and returning completion text given a piece of text that has been entered by the user. Unlike many of the other applications in this book, which return data that has been carefully formatted in XML, Completer simply returns a text fragment that serves as a completion for what the user has already entered. This is just raw text with no special formatting, which means you can grab it on the client side and use it directly without parsing any XML code.

Although the server responses in the Completer application are short and sweet, they do occur much more often than Ajax responses you’ve seen in other applications because they are triggered every time the user types a character. This isn’t necessarily a problem, but it is an argument for not using huge completion text in the list of possible completions. This list is sourced by the completer.php server script, by the way, which you see a little later in the chapter.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

Although you probably already have this particular client-to-server communication exchange figured out, Figure 6.4 shows exactly how the Ajax request and subsequent response relate to the browser (client) and Web server.

Figure 6.4. The Completer application involves unformatted user input and completion text flowing back and forth between the client and server.

The text data sent back and forth on each side of the Completer application is very small, typically on the order of a few characters or words. If you look back at the exchange of data earlier in the chapter for the text completion example of “Thomas Edison,” you’ll notice a sequence of user input characters sent out with each Ajax request. These requests are handled by the completer.php server script. Following is an example of a URL for an Ajax request that sends along the character T as the user input:

completer.php?input=T

At this point, the server responds and tries to guess that the user is entering Tecumseh, which isn’t the case. If the user continues to type by entering the character h, the following URL serves as the Ajax completion request:

completer.php?input=Th

This time the text Th is being sent to the server script, which should result in a more accurate completion. The server responds by guessing Theodore Roosevelt, which is closer but not quite there. Finally, a URL with a three-character input is what it takes to get the correct completion:

completer.php?input=Tho

In this example, the server responds with the text mas Edision, which is the completion text for the name Thomas Edison (Tho + mas Edison). The user can now accept the completion and click the Learn More button to find out more about Thomas Edison. That’s really all there is to the data exchange in the Completer application.

Putting Together the Completer Application

Not only is the data exchange straightforward in the Completer application, but the structure of the application is pretty simple, too, consisting of only two files:

  • HTML Web page (completer.html)

  • PHP server script (completer.php)

These two files directly correspond to the client (completer.html) and server (completer.php) portions of the application; there are no separate data sources or third-party Web services to contend with. The Completer Web page issues Ajax requests that are handled directly by the PHP server script, which then responds with data that is handled by the JavaScript code in the page.

In reality, the list of completion words and phrases, which you see in a moment as part of the completer.php code, should ideally reside in a database of some sort. Some auto-complete lists can get fairly large, and a true database is likely to be more efficient at performing searches within large data sets than straight PHP code. However, I opted for simplicity in this example and just coded the completion list as an array within the PHP server script. As long as your completion lists don’t get too terribly unwieldy in size, you should be fine doing the same.

The Completer Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Completer application (completer.html) has an extremely simple user interface, consisting solely of a text input control and a button for looking up the historical name entered. Figure 6.5 shows the page open in a Web browser awaiting user input.

Figure 6.5. The Completer Web page starts out with an empty text-input box waiting for the user to enter a historical name.

This user interface is primarily established by way of the body of the HTML code, which follows:

<body onload="document.getElementById('name').value = '';
  document.getElementById('name').focus()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>
    Enter a historical name:
    <input id="name" type="text" size="32"
      onkeyup="getCompletion(this, event)" />
    <input id="learnButton" type="button" value="Learn More..." onclick="showBiography()" />
  </div>
</body>

This code reveals how the page consists of two input controls—one a text box and the other a button. Notice that the text box is cleared in the body’s onload event handler, and focus is set to it as well. This latter action is a good idea for any Web page where the initial action is the user entering text. Google is a good example of this same strategy—the focus is always set to the search text box.

Also notice that the onkeyup event handler for the text input control is set so that it calls the getCompletion() function. This is the function responsible for initiating an Ajax request that retrieves a completion suggestion from the Web server. Here’s the code for it:

function getCompletion(inputControl, evt) {
  evt = evt ? evt : (event ? event : null);
  if (evt) {
    // Ignore keys that don't factor in to the text entry
    var key = evt.charCode ? evt.charCode : evt.keyCode;
    if (key < 32 || (key >= 33 && key <= 46) ||
     (key >= 112 && key <= 123))
      return;

    // Store away the current input
    currentInput = inputControl.value;

    // Send the Ajax request to load the completion
    ajaxSendRequest("GET", "completer.php?input=" +
      encodeURIComponent(inputControl.value),
      handleCompletionRequest);
  }
}

Note

Earlier in the chapter I mentioned a potential performance problem related to Ajax requests such as this one where lots of requests are issued in response to key presses. One clever way around such a problem is to set a timer for issuing completion requests instead of issuing a request in response to every single key press. For example, you might check for a new key press twice a second, in which case the net effect is roughly the same in terms of completion text appearing but the maximum number of requests are throttled back to two per second.

Most of the initial code in this function takes care of browser inconsistencies with respect to how events are handled. In this case you are interested in getting the value of a key release, which involves a few tricks so that the code will work in any major browser. Once the character code of the key is resolved, it is checked against several ranges of keys to make sure that it is indeed valid entry text. This prevents a control key such as Shift or Enter from being interpreted as valid data entry, which would otherwise issue an Ajax auto-completion request.

If the user input is indeed valid, it is stored away in a global variable called currentInput. The server URL for the Ajax request is then assembled based upon the text in the text box, and the request is sent to the server. Notice that the handleCompletionRequest() function is responsible for answering the call when the Ajax request returns with a response. Following is the code for this function:

function handleCompletionRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Grab the response text and kill any trailing whitespace
    var completion = request.responseText.replace(/\s*$/g, "");

    // Only issue an Ajax request if the input control still
    // has the current input
    var inputControl = document.getElementById("name");
    if (completion && (inputControl.value == currentInput)) {
      if (inputControl.setSelectionRange) {
        // Add the completion to the input control for non-IE
        // browsers
        var inputLen = inputControl.value.length;
        inputControl.value += completion;
        inputControl.setSelectionRange(inputLen,
          inputControl.value.length);
      }
      else if (inputControl.createTextRange) {
        // Add the completion to the input control for IE
        var sel = inputControl.createTextRange();
        sel.text += completion;
        sel.moveStart("character", -completion.length);
        sel.moveEnd("character", 0);
        sel.select();
      }
    }
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

The handleCompletionRequest() function receives each Ajax server response, and is responsible for carefully inserting the completion text into the text box and selecting it. Although this task sounds simple enough, yet again browser incompatibilities result in two branches of code: one to accommodate IE and another to accommodate other browsers. It’s unfortunate that at this stage of the Web’s evolution we’re still dealing with completely different approaches to something as simple as selecting text in a text box. But complaining isn’t going to get this application working any sooner!

Looking back near the beginning of the handleCompletionRequest() function, you’ll see a line of code that manipulates the server response text and stores it in the completion variable. This manipulation is necessary because the response text picks up some extra characters in the process of being sent from the server to the client. These characters appear at the end of the text and are likely a result of the echo command in the PHP script, even though it isn’t supposed to append a line-feed, newline, or any other character to the end of a piece of text. At any rate, the standard text replace() method is used to trim excess characters off the end of the response text before proceeding.

The remainder of the handleCompletionRequest() function includes two different techniques for adding the completion text to the text box and then selecting it. Notice that before the code branches to browser-specific code, the currentInput variable is checked against the value of the text box. This is an important check because it makes sure the Ajax request matches the input that triggered it. It’s possible for someone to type really fast and enter characters faster than the Ajax requests can resolve. In this case, you only want the last request to count. This piece of code helps to make sure that happens.

Although the two browser-specific code branches are different in their details, they both do the same thing:

  • Append the completion to the end of the user input in the text box.

  • Select the completion in the text box so that if the user enters another character, the completion will be automatically erased.

That wraps up the client-side functionality of the auto-complete feature, but this isn’t quite the end of the Completer Web page. If you look back at the HTML code for the body of the page, a function called showBiography() is called in response to the user clicking the Learn More button. Here’s the code for this function:

function showBiography() {
  // Open a link in Wikipedia based upon the historical name
  // entered
  if (document.getElementById("name").value != "")
    window.location = "http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/" +
      encodeURIComponent(document.getElementById("name").value);
}

The idea here is to make the application do something meaningful with the historical name that has been entered. So the showBiography() function takes the historical name (contents of the text box) and uses it to build a URL for the Wikipedia online encyclopedia. This URL takes the following form: http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Edison

In this example, the name Thomas Edison is simply grafted onto the end of a boilerplate URL, which you can see in the code for the showBiography() function. The URL is then opened in the current browser instance by assigning it to the window.location property, which is akin to typing in the address bar of the browser. In other words, when you set the window.location property to a certain URL, it forces the browser to navigate to that URL.

The Completer Server Script

The server side of the Completer application involves a PHP server script named completer.php. This script is not only responsible for receiving a request and responding with a completion, but also serves as the data repository for the list of possible completions. I mentioned earlier in the chapter that this list might be better off stored in a true database, especially for applications that utilize large completion lists, but for the sake of simplicity the list works fine in the PHP script for this application.

Following is the abbreviated code for the completer.php server script:

<?php
$people = array(
  "Abraham Lincoln",
  "Adam Smith",
  "Adolf Hitler",
  "Albert Einstein",
  "Alexander Fleming",
  "Alexander Graham Bell",
  "Alexander Hamilton",
  "Alexander the Great",
  "Alfred T. Mahan",
...
  "William Harvey",
  "William Shakespeare",
  "William T.G. Morton",
  "William the Conqueror",
  "Willis Haviland Carrier",
  "Woodrow Wilson",
  "Ziryab"
);
$input = strtolower($_GET['input']);
$inputLen = strlen($input);

if ($inputLen > 0) {
  for ($i = 0; $i < count($people); $i++) {
    if (strtolower(substr($people[$i], 0, $inputLen)) ==
      $input) {
      echo substr($people[$i], $inputLen,
        strlen($people[$i]) - $inputLen);
      break;
    }
  }
}
?>

The most significant code in this script is the array of historical people, which is laid out as a list of alphabetized names in quotes. Notice that each name is separated by a comma, and that the entire list is enclosed within parentheses. This is standard formatting for arrays in PHP, and I point it out so that you know to maintain the format when modifying the completion list or creating one of your own.

One subtle detail of the Completer application that I haven’t mentioned yet is how it doesn’t care about the case of the user input. In other words, it will match Thomas Edison if you enter, tho, Tho, or tHo. The code responsible for this is the first line below the completion list that calls the standard strtolower() function.

The remaining code in the server script takes on the task of searching through the completion list one name at a time, looking for a match with the input text. If a match is found, it is echoed to the client, and the script ends. This is an important detail that reveals why the completion list must be in alphabetical order—the first match of the input text is always the one returned. Because the first match always wins, the only way to get consistent results is to alphabetize the list. A bit later in the chapter I offer a little trick where you can use Microsoft Word to automatically alphabetize a list—this can be very helpful if you have a huge list that isn’t in any order.

You now have the two pieces of the application put together, so it’s time to move on to the most fun development phase, testing!

Testing Completer

The Completer application is pretty simple when it comes to testing, seeing as how all you have to do is start typing and see what happens. Figure 6.6 shows the application after the character C is entered in the text box.

Figure 6.6. Entering a single character in the text box of the Completer application results in an attempted completion.

The first historical person found whose name begins with C is Charlemagne, which is evident in the figure. Because the completion text is automatically selected, you can continue typing if that name isn’t the correct match. Continue by typing the character o, which results in a different name, as you might expect (see Figure 6.7).

Figure 6.7. As you continue typing characters, the application continues to find the best match for the completion.

Ah, Confucius says this is the name I want, so I’m going to stop typing and click the Learn More button to find out more about the Chinese philosopher.
