A simple click of a button leads you to a wealth of information about the person whose name you entered with the help of the auto-correct feature. The Wikipedia facet of the Completer application certainly isn’t critical in any way, but it does add a touch of practical sizzle.

Giving Completer an Extreme Ajax Makeover

The Completer application is one of the most broadly applicable examples in this book because it can be applied to virtually any text input, provided that it is somehow possible to predict what the user might type. As you think about using the auto-complete feature in your own pages, the key is to isolate text input that is predictable enough to be modeled with a list of words or phrases, but also not so predictable that a selection list would better serve your purposes.

As an example of where auto-complete might be overkill, consider the example of helping the user type in a state in the United States. Because there is a finite and relatively small list of states, it probably makes more sense to just have the user select from a list of states. On the other hand, maybe you want to use auto-complete to assist someone in entering the name of their pet. Although this might ultimately be a viable application of auto-complete, the list of possible names is so huge and open-ended that it may not be worth trying to help. The point is that auto-complete is certainly an extremely useful user input tool, but you should make sure to think through the specific type of input before assuming that it makes sense to use auto-complete.

With that little rant out of the way, let me say that the first thing you should try to do with the Completer application is change out the completion list entirely. Think of some other text-entry scenario that would be cool to assist the user with. Then change the data set in the completer.php server script; just make sure that the list remains in alphabetical order. The application will immediately start trying to auto-complete the text input using your new list of completions.

Tip

If you’re building a list of words and/or phrases for the Completer application and they aren’t already in alphabetical order, I have a little trick to save you some time and energy. You need access to Microsoft Word, but that shouldn’t be too terribly difficult considering how prevalent Microsoft Office is these days. Create a new document in Word, and then copy and paste your list of completions into it. Select all of the completions, and then click Table on the Word menu. Then select Sort from the Table menu. A dialog box will appear. Make sure the Ascending radio box is selected, and then click OK. Presto! Your list is now alphabetized.

Another practical possibility for applying the Completer application to your own Web site is adding a context-sensitive search box to your site. For example, let’s say you run a Web site devoted to extreme sports. You could offer a search box that uses auto-complete to suggest extreme sports terms and people. You already know how to help the user enter text into a text control via auto-complete, so all you’re missing is the trick to route the input text to Google and perform the search.

Initiating a Google search is as easy as redirecting your Web page to a Google URL that includes the search string. Following is an example of such a Google URL: http://www.google.com/search?q=tony+hawk

This sample URL searches for the text “tony hawk”, which would find tons of matches for professional skateboarder Tony Hawk. Notice that a plus sign (+) is used to separate the first and last name. You must use + in place of any spaces in the search terms.

Your remaining task is to redirect the page to the Google URL. Assuming the text input control in your application still has the ID name, as in the Completer application, the following code grabs the input text, converts its spaces to plus signs (+), builds a Google search URL, and redirects the browser to it:

var searchText = document.getElementById("name").value.replace(  " ", "+");
window.location = "http://www.google.com/search?q=" + searchText;

That’s all the code you need to convert an auto-completed context-sensitive text field entry into a Google search.

Summary

This chapter added an extremely handy Ajax technique to your rapidly expanding bag of tricks. The Completer application is very focused and simple in that it only applies to a text control, but when used properly it yields surprisingly effective results in the usability of a Web page. The fact that you can completely ignore the auto-complete suggestion and continue typing means that the feature doesn’t get in the way if the completion suggestions happen not to help. This is a common aspect of many Ajax techniques, and a point you should always keep in mind when injecting Ajax into your own pages.

Chapter 7. Creating Responsive GUIs with Real-Time Validation

Ajax the Cleanser

You knew it was coming sooner or later, so why wait any longer? Perhaps the most known usage of the term Ajax is the all-purpose cleanser that was introduced by Colgate-Palmolive in 1947. The cleanser was named after the powerful Greek hero Ajax, which led to the Ajax product slogan: “Stronger than dirt!”

One of the truly killer applications of Ajax is real-time user input validation, which means that data entered by the user is checked for validity as the user enters it. This can dramatically improve the efficiency of user interfaces because errors are caught as they are made, thus eliminating the need to submit an entire form to find out whether a problem exists. Additionally, applications just feel smarter when they give immediate feedback to the user regarding data entry. As it turns out, Ajax isn’t technically required for a lot of real-time validation tricks, but it still does play a role in some instances when it is necessary to load data from a server as part of the validation.

This chapter digs into real-time validation and explores when and where it makes sense to inject such functionality into your own applications. You also take a quick break from Ajax to learn how to validate popular data types such as phone numbers, dates, and email addresses. Ajax then comes back into play as you work through a practical example involving ZIP code validation that performs a live look-up of the respective city and state. This is an extremely handy Ajax trick you can use to significantly streamline the entry of location/address information.

The following files are used by the Validator application in this chapter and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap07 example code folder:

  • validator.html—Main Web page

  • ziplookup.php—Server script for looking up a city/state based upon a ZIP code

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • validatekit.js—Validation functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • wait.gif—Animated “loading” icon that is displayed during an Ajax request

The Challenge: Checking User Input in Real Time

The challenge laid down in this chapter is quite broad—improve the retrieval of information from the user by eliminating input errors immediately at the point where they are made. If you use a word processor such as Microsoft Word, which has a real-time spell checker, then you understand how handy this feature can be. In many ways the auto-completion application in the previous chapter can be thought of as roughly similar to a real-time validator. However, auto-complete is more about improving efficiency than it is about correcting mistakes, even though the two tasks often go hand in hand.

To see a practical validator in action, check out Google’s Gmail online email service at http://mail.google.com/. The invitation feature in Gmail allows you to send an invitation to a friend to join Gmail. The invitation request requires the email address of your friend, which is what you’re supposed to enter in the text box. An improperly formatted email address results in Gmail displaying an “invalid address” message just below the email address text box.

Gmail isn’t quite as aggressive with its validation as some Ajax applications, which is evident by the fact that you have to click a Send Invite button before Gmail performs the validation. A more aggressive approach would involve validating the address as soon as the user leaves the email text box. This latter approach is often employed in Ajax applications, and in most cases serves as an improvement because it can head off invalid data entry as soon as it is entered.

The general idea behind a modern approach to user input validation is to eliminate the need for a page reload in order to see if data is valid. In many cases you can eliminate the entire trip to the server and perform the validation entirely within the client. Strictly speaking, if the server isn’t involved, the validation isn’t truly using Ajax. However, there is a notion of an Ajax “feel” to an application that can still be gained via client-side validation. The challenge in this chapter involves both client-only and true Ajax validation.

Speaking of the challenge, this chapter gets away from accomplishing a central task and instead focuses on demonstrating how to validate several different kinds of user input. Sure, it would’ve been possible to dream up some example involving numbers, dates, email addresses, phone numbers, and ZIP codes, but the additional overhead would just distract from the real emphasis, validating data. So the challenge in the Validator application is to simply present several user-input text boxes, each associated with a particular data type, and validate each one in real time as the user moves through the user interface.

Following are the data types targeted for validation by the Validator application:

  • Integer

  • Number

  • Phone number

  • Email address

  • Date

  • ZIP code

The key to validation isn’t just popping up an annoying alert box to let the user know his or her input is bad. In fact, an alert box is the worst way you can go about notifying the user of an input problem. A better approach is to sneakily provide help fields on the form that are invisible unless a validation problem occurs, in which case you display help text for the user. For example, you might display the help text

"Please enter a date (for example, 01/14/1975)."

when the user enters an invalid date. The help text appears when the data is bad and then magically goes away once the data is fixed.

Of course, the “magic” is in the validation code that supports the help text feature. This code is part of the validatekit.js file included as part of the Ajax Toolkit on the CD-ROM with this book, and is also a part of the source code files for the Validator sample application. As was originally mentioned back in Chapter 2, “Inside an Ajax Application,” the validatekit.js file offers several JavaScript functions you can use to validate different types of data. More specifically, the validatekit.js file exposes the following JavaScript functions that can be used for validating user input data:

  • validateNonEmpty()—Is the data empty?

  • validateInteger()—Is the data a valid integer?

  • validateNumber()—Is the data a valid number?

  • validateZipCode()—Is the data a valid ZIP code?

  • validatePhone()—Is the data a valid phone number?

  • validateEmail()—Is the data a valid email address?

  • validateDate()—Is the data a valid date?

As you can see, these functions just so happen to match up perfectly with the data types targeted by the Validator application. This means that the challenge just got a whole lot easier because you now have standard JavaScript functions you can use to validate each data type. The challenge primarily becomes figuring out how to wire these functions into the HTML elements on the Validator page so that data within text boxes is properly routed to the JavaScript functions. Also, you’ll need to create HTML elements for displaying the help text for each text box so that the validation functions can display help text.

At this point you may be wondering exactly where Ajax fits into the Validator equation, and the answer is that it doesn’t... at least not yet. All of the data types targeted by the application can be validated perfectly fine within the client without any assistance from a server. Or at least the format of the data can be validated on the client. ZIP code data is a little unusual because you can validate the format of a ZIP code (exactly five integer numbers, for example) on the client but you won’t know if the ZIP code is truly valid in the real world. In other words, is the ZIP code actually in use?

The only way to check for the validity of a ZIP code is to look it up in a database of real ZIP codes to see if it is indeed real. This database would be able to tell you that 90210 is indeed a real ZIP code and that it represents Beverly Hills, CA. As it turns out, such databases already exist and can be accessed via Ajax requests. Even better, most of them can provide you the data in an XML format, making it easy to sort out in your Ajax code. Ajax therefore enters the picture with the Validator application by way of looking up the city and state for a ZIP code to verify that the ZIP code is real.

So now you have a fairly complete validation challenge involving several client-side data validations as well as a true Ajax validation involving ZIP codes and their respective cities and states. You can now turn your attention to the design of the Validator application.

The Design: Designing a Validator Application

The design of the Validator application is hopefully a little more obvious than some of the other applications in the book, primarily because discrete pieces of user input data are validated independently of one another. Your design job is basically just to call the appropriate validatekit.js validation function for each user-input text box. Then there is the Ajax request that provides an additional level of validation for ZIP codes by looking up the city and state from a Web service.

Figure 7.1 shows a rough interpretation of the user interface for the application, which reveals how the help fields are arranged next to each input text box.

Figure 7.1. The user interface for the Validator application consists of several text boxes and their associated help text fields.

You can see in the figure how each text box is associated with a particular kind of data, such as a phone number, an email address, etc. The help field next to each text box is used to convey help information about the validity of the data in that text box. The only exception is the help field for the ZIP code, which serves double duty as a display field for the city and state retrieved via an Ajax request.

Speaking of the Ajax request for the ZIP field, it would be quite handy to display an animated “loading” image to let the user know that the application is busy looking up the city and state based upon the ZIP code. This image, wait.gif, is shown in Figure 7.2.

Figure 7.2. The Validator application makes use of an animated “loading” image (wait.gif) to let the user know the Ajax request is being processed.

The “loading” image is displayed in the same field that holds the help text for the ZIP code, which I suppose makes the field actually perform triple duty. The following three pieces of information are capable of appearing in the ZIP code’s help field at different times throughout the process of entering a ZIP code:

  • Help text is shown when the ZIP code is invalid.

  • The “loading” image is shown while an Ajax request is being carried out.

  • The dynamically loaded city and state are shown after a successful Ajax request.

This may seem like a lot of stuff is going on with respect to the ZIP code and its helper field, but the Validator application handles it all without much complication. Keep in mind that the application is really only dealing with one Ajax request, which also helps to simplify things. You’re now ready to learn a little more about the ZIP code Ajax request.

The Client Request

The client request for the Validator application is very basic in that it sends along the ZIP code when the ZIP code text-input control loses focus. This approach is very different from the Completer application in Chapter 6, “Reading the User’s Mind with Auto-Complete,” which issues an Ajax request after each individual character is typed. The character-by-character request technique doesn’t make sense for Validator because it takes multiple keypresses to enter a meaningful piece of data in Validator—you must wait until the user is finished entering the data before attempting a validation. Your cue that the user is finished entering data in the ZIP code input control is when he or she leaves the control by tabbing away from it or clicking off of it.

Once the user has left the ZIP code text box, you’re free to send along the input as part of an Ajax request. This user input is just raw characters of text without any special formatting. The integer validation that has already been performed does ensure that the ZIP code text consists of five integer characters, but the text is still considered unformatted in a sense that it isn’t encoded in XML or any other standard data format.

Note

I realize there is a longer ZIP code format of the form #####-#### that could feasibly enter the picture here. To keep things simple, I’ve opted to stick with the basic five-digit ZIP code, which has the form #####.

That’s all you need to know about the minimal amount of data sent from the client to the server for a ZIP code lookup. Let’s now address what the server sends back.

The Server Response

The Ajax server response in the Validator application returns information about the ZIP code sent over in the request. This response is indirectly received from a third party Web service, webserviceX.net, that offers several handy data feeds in XML format. In this case, the webserviceX.net service uses a ZIP code to look up and return a matching city, state, area code, and time zone. Since all you need in the Validator application is the city and state, you can ignore the area code and time zone. The fact that the response data is formatted as XML code makes it easy to access only the specific data you need.

You may recall from some of the earlier applications that you can’t make an Ajax request on a third-party domain. Therefore, the trick is to alleviate this security limitation by making the third-party data request from a server script, and then using Ajax to get the data from that script, which resides on your server. This works because there isn’t a security issue with requesting data from a server script. So the Validator application needs to include a PHP server script that serves as a proxy for making ZIP code requests to the webserviceX.net service.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

The Ajax request within the Validator application is used to shuttle a user-entered ZIP code to the server and then receive a city and state in return. Figure 7.3 helps to clarify this transfer of data visually.

Figure 7.3. The Validator application involves an unformatted ZIP code and formatted city and state data flowing back and forth between the client and server.

The data sent to the server as a parameter of the Ajax request is a ZIP code that has been entered by the user. The ZIP code has already been validated in terms of its format but has not been checked to make sure it really matches up with a city and state. ZIP code requests are handled by the ziplookup.php server script. Following is an example of an Ajax request URL that sends along the ZIP code 85250 as the only parameter to the server script:

ziplookup.php?zipCode=85250

This code reveals how the ZIP code is packaged into a URL as a single parameter (zipCode). This entire URL is used as the basis for an Ajax request, in which case your server script, ziplookup.php, will take the zipCode parameter and use the URL it contains to obtain the city and state information.

The city and state data is returned as a compact XML document that actually contains the city, state, area code, and time zone for the ZIP code, assuming the ZIP code is successfully matched. Following is an example of what such an XML document actually looks like:

<NewDataSet>
  <Table>
    <CITY>Scottsdale</CITY>
    <STATE>AZ</STATE>
    <ZIP>85250</ZIP>
    <AREA_CODE>602</AREA_CODE>
    <TIME_ZONE>M</TIME_ZONE>
  </Table>
</NewDataSet>

Even if you aren’t an XML guru, this code isn’t too terribly hard to figure out. You’re really only interested in the <CITY> and <STATE> tags because they contain the city and state for the ZIP code. The remaining task is then to extract the city and state from this data on the client.

Putting Together the Validator Application

Aside from the standard toolkit JavaScript files, including validatekit.js, the Validator application consists of only two components:

  • HTML Web page (validator.html)

  • PHP server script (ziplookup.php)

Of course, the other major ingredient in the application is the third party location Web service that is responsible for providing the city/state data based upon a ZIP code. Although this service is an important part of the application, it doesn’t require a distinct component other than code within the HTML Web page that initiates an Ajax request and code within the PHP server script that handles relaying the response back to the page. This arrangement is necessary because Ajax requests aren’t allowed direct access to resources located on other domains.

The next couple of sections explore the Validator Web page and server script in detail in order to fully understand how the application works.

The Validator Web Page

The HTML Web page for the Validator application, validator.html, consists of a series of text box input fields, each with a non-input text field next to it that serves as a display area for help messages. The text boxes are created as input elements, while the help message fields are created as span elements. Figure 7.4 shows the Validator application open in a Web browser with the blank text fields ready for user input and all of the help messages indicating that the fields are missing data.

Figure 7.4. The Validator Web page consists of a series of text box input controls followed by text display areas.

Note

The application doesn’t automatically display help messages initially as shown in the figure. Instead, the “Please enter a value.” help message doesn’t appear until you actually navigate away from an input control without entering anything.

With the layout of the Validator application in mind, as revealed in the figure, check out the HTML code for the body of the Web page:

<body onload="document.getElementById('something').focus()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none; font-style:italic">
  </div>
  <br />
  <div>
    Enter something:
    <input id="something" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validateNonEmpty(this,
document.getElementById('something_help'))" />
    <span id="something_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter an integer:
    <input id="integer" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validateInteger(this,
      document.getElementById('integer_help'))" />
    <span id="integer_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter a number:
    <input id="number" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validateNumber(this,
      document.getElementById('number_help'))" />
    <span id="number_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter a phone number:
    <input id="phone" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validatePhone(this,
      document.getElementById('phone_help'))" />
    <span id="phone_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter an email address:
    <input id="email" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validateEmail(this,
      document.getElementById('email_help'))" />
    <span id="email_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter a date:
    <input id="date" type="text" size="32" onblur=
      "validateDate(this,
      document.getElementById('date_help'))" />
    <span id="date_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
  <div>
    Enter a ZIP code:
    <input id="zipcode" type="text" size="16"
      onblur="if (validateZipCode(this,
        document.getElementById('zipcode_help')))
        getCityState(this.value);" />
    <span id="zipcode_help" class="help"></span>
  </div>
</body>

After the focus is set to the first text box on the page via the onload event handler, the body of the page moves on to creating each of the text box input and associated help message span pairs. There isn’t much particularly unusual about any of this code except for the onblur event handler, which gets called when the input focus leaves an input control. So, when the user tabs away from a text box or clicks somewhere else on the page, the text box receives an onblur event notification. This is where you call a function to validate the input value in the text box.

The functions that take care of the validating in the Validator application are all part of the validatekit.js Ajax Toolkit file, which must be imported into the Web page with the following line of code (placed in the head of the page):

<script type="text/javascript" src="validatekit.js"> </script>

Each of the functions provided by this file accept the same two function parameters:

  • The input control containing the value to be validated

  • The help control used to display help text

To see how these parameters get passed into a validation function, take a look at the code that calls the integer validation function:

validateInteger(this, document.getElementById('integer_help'))

Because the function is called from within the context of the integer text box, you can pass this as the first parameter to the function. The second parameter is then specified using the standard getElementById() function to grab the integer help control, whose ID is integer_help.

If you pay close attention to the help controls on the page, you may notice that each of the span elements is styled to a CSS style class named help. This style class simply changes the color of the font to red (#FF0000) and the style of the font to italic, which makes the help messages jump out a little more on the page. Following is the code for the Validator Web page’s internal style sheet, which includes the span.help style class:

<style type="text/css">
  div { padding-bottom:5px; }
  span.help { color:#AA0000; font-style:italic; }
</style>

A global div style is included in the style sheet purely to help provide a bit of vertical spacing between the input controls on the page.

Although the Validator internal style sheet is helpful in making the page look a little better, it doesn’t do much to help on the Ajax front. The code that initiates Ajax requests for the Validator application is contained in the onblur event handler for the ZIP code text box. Although this code is in the listing you just saw, it’s easier to follow if you break it out of the onblur attribute:

if (validateZipCode(this,
  document.getElementById('zipcode_help')))
  getCityState(this.value);

This code first validates the ZIP code input data to see if it is indeed a five-digit number. This is accomplished with the call to the validateZipCode() function. The return value of the function indicates whether the data is valid (true) or invalid (false). If the data is valid, the getCityState() function is called to initiate an Ajax request. Here’s the code for this function:

function getCityState(zipCode) {
  // Display the wait image
  document.getElementById("zipcode_help").innerHTML =
    "<img src='wait.gif'
    alt='Looking up ZIP code...' />";

  // Send the Ajax request to load the city/state
  ajaxSendRequest("GET", "ziplookup.php?zipCode=" + zipCode,
    handleCityStateRequest);
}

Ah, you finally see some of the magic that makes this application look cool. The first task in the getCityState() function is to display the animated “loading” image, wait.gif, in the ZIP code help control. This image will remain displayed until the Ajax request is completed, thereby giving the user a visual cue that something is taking place behind the scenes.

The second task in the getCityState() function is to actually submit the Ajax request, which involves packaging the ZIP code into a URL for the ziplookup.php server script. The handleCityStateRequest() function is specified as the request handler for the ZIP code Ajax request. Here’s the code for it:

function handleCityStateRequest() {
  if (request.readyState == 4 && request.status == 200) {
    // Store the XML response data
    var xmlData = request.responseXML;

    // Display the city/state results
    if (xmlData != null &&
      getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("CITY")[0]) != "")
      document.getElementById("zipcode_help").innerHTML =
        "<span style='font-weight:bold'>" +
        getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("CITY")[0]) +
        ", " +
        getText(xmlData.getElementsByTagName("STATE")[0]) +
        "</span>";
    else
      document.getElementById("zipcode_help").innerHTML =
        "Could not find the ZIP code.";
  }
  ajaxUpdateState();
}

The job of this function, which is called when the Ajax request completes, is to extract the city and state from the response data and display them on the page in place of the “loading” image. The city/state data is extracted with a little help from the getText() Ajax Toolkit function, which is located in the domkit.js file. Notice that the XML element names CITY and STATE are used as the basis for accessing the city and state data. Not only is this data extracted, but it is carefully reformatted using HTML code constructed on the fly. If all goes well, the resulting HTML code looks something like this:

<span style='font-weight:bold'>Scottsdale, AZ</span>

Notice that an inline style is applied to the city and state so that they appear in a bold font, which helps to make them distinguishable from normal help text. If there is a problem with the XML data returned from the server, a help message is displayed indicating that the ZIP code could not be found.

That wraps up the code for the Validator Web page. All that’s left is to take a quick look at the PHP server script responsible for passing along the ZIP code request to a remote server.

The ZIP Lookup Server Script

Similar to the Picker application from Chapter 4, “Using Ajax to Dynamically Populate Lists: A Stock Picker,” Validator requires a script on the server side to sidestep the limitation of not being able to make a direct Ajax client request on a third-party domain. The third-party domain is important because the Validator application relies on it for looking up ZIP codes. The Validator PHP script serves as somewhat of a security proxy by accepting an Ajax request on behalf of the client, retrieving the city/state data from the third-party server, and then responding to the client with the data. In this way, the Ajax request itself is made on your own domain, which is perfectly acceptable given the security constraints of Ajax.

The third-party Web service I’m talking about is webserviceX.net, which offers several XML-based data feeds. The Picker application in Chapter 4 uses this service to obtain live stock quotes, whereas Validator uses it to obtain detailed information about a ZIP code. In this case you specify a ZIP code, and the server responds with an XML document containing detailed information about it, including the city and state associated with it. You’ve already seen the code within this XML document, but you haven’t seen the PHP script that makes it possible to receive the code via an Ajax request. Following is the code for the ziplookup.php server script:

<?php
header('Content-Type: text/xml');

// Load the XML location data from the server
$xml = html_entity_decode(file_get_contents(
  "http://www.webservicex.net/uszip.asmx/GetInfoByZIP?USZip=" .
  $_REQUEST['zipCode']));

// Return the XML location data
echo $xml;
?>

Keep in mind that the job of this script is to retrieve an XML document from the ZIP code Web service at webserviceX.net, and then simply pass that data along to the client without any modifications. It is then up to the client to do something useful with the XML code, such as display the city and state on the page.

The server script begins by making sure that the response gets treated as XML. It then loads the XML document from the ZIP code Web service, making sure to pass along the zipCode parameter so that the Web service knows which ZIP code to look up. The script concludes by returning the XML data from the script, which serves as the response to the Ajax ZIP code request.

Although I’m sure you’re itching to test out the application, let’s take a second to test the PHP script by itself. You saw how this is done in earlier chapters by opening PHP scripts directly in a browser—here’s a sample URL for testing the ZIPLookUp script:

http://www.yourdomain.com/ziplookup.php?zipCode=94965

Assuming the ziplookup.php file is stored on your server, change the domain to match yours in this line of code and then enter the URL into your Web browser. Figure 7.5 shows the XML response data generated by the PHP script.

Figure 7.5. You can examine the XML ZIP code information returned by the ZIPLookUp PHP script by opening the script directly in a browser.

It’s definitely worth experimenting with the ZIP codes passed into the script via the URL to see how they impact the XML data that is returned. You should always test PHP scripts directly in this manner to make sure their results are consistent with what you expected—it’s much tougher to diagnose server script problems when all you’re looking at is the client Web page.

Testing Validator

The Validator application presents an interesting testing opportunity because you have several different data fields to tinker with as you check the effectiveness of the validation. You’ll notice that the data types tend to progress in complexity as you move down the page. It’s easy to generate a help message by either entering nothing or entering invalid data into any of the input controls, and then tabbing off of the control. Figure 7.6 shows a help message being displayed next to invalid integer data.

Figure 7.6. Entering invalid data and then leaving an input control results in the display of a help message next to the control.

Of course, the text “more data” doesn’t exactly constitute a valid integer, which explains the help message in the figure.

Things get more interesting as you experiment with entering data into more complex types, such as the date input control. Figure 7.7 shows an invalid date message displayed in response to a nearly valid date.

Figure 7.7. The date input control is pretty good at detecting bad dates.

Obviously the month of January only has 31 days, so a date involving the 32nd day of the month is invalid in this example. The Validator application catches the input error and displays a help message.

Although date validation is handy, the most intriguing part of the Validator application is the lookup of ZIP codes, which takes place when you enter a ZIP code into the ZIP code input control and then leave the control. Figure 7.8 shows the animated “loading” image displayed as the ZIP code Ajax request is being carried out.

Figure 7.8. The “loading” image is displayed in lieu of a help message to let the user know that the ZIP code is being looked up.

Yeah, I know, I couldn’t resist using the most popular ZIP code on Earth to test out the application. If the ZIP code data is successfully retrieved via the Ajax request, the resulting city and state are displayed in place of the “loading” image, as shown in Figure 7.9.

Figure 7.9. A successful ZIP code Ajax request results in the display of the associated city and state next to the input control.

Success is great, but what happens when a ZIP code isn’t found? If you recall in the code for the application, a help message is displayed to indicate this. Figure 7.10 shows the help message displayed in response to a ZIP code that isn’t associated with a real city and state.

Figure 7.10. A ZIP code with no real-world city and state results in a help message indicating that the ZIP code couldn’t be found.

It’s important to note that a ZIP code that isn’t found isn’t necessarily invalid in a strict data entry sense. Yes, it isn’t in use in the real world, which is a problem if you’re entering a shipping address, for example, but that doesn’t mean it won’t be real someday. ZIP codes are not etched in stone, and in fact they are created anew on a fairly regular basis in fast-growing areas. That’s the beauty of relying on a third-party service to handle the ZIP code lookup—it is responsible for keeping its ZIP code database up to date, not you.

Regardless of how you handle the issue of a ZIP code not being used in the real world, hopefully you can see how the Validator application lays some important groundwork in data validation that you can reuse and repurpose in all kinds of useful ways.

Giving Validator an Extreme Ajax Makeover

The Validator application is one of the more immediately applicable examples in the book because intelligent data entry is a common need. The validatekit.js file offers functions for common data types that are often used in data entry forms. The first way to expand upon the Validator example is to apply the validation functions to your own user-input forms. This is as easy as importing the validatekit.js file into your pages via the following <script> tag:

<script type="text/javascript" src="validatekit.js"> </script>

Then code the HTML elements for validation by calling the appropriate validation function and passing along the IDs of the HTML elements. As an example, if you wanted to validate the age of a person to make sure it is an integer number, you might create the following text box and help field:

<input id="age" type="text" size="4"
  onblur="validateInteger(this,
  document.getElementById('age_help'))" />
<span id="age_help" class="help"></span>

You don’t have to associate a style class with the help text, but it does make the user interface look a little better. Here’s an example of a help style that you can use to dress up the help fields:

<style type="text/css">
  span.help { color:#AA0000; font-style:italic; }
</style>

Keep in mind that this style must be placed in the head of the Web page, which is also where the <script> tag you saw a moment ago must be placed. This style just makes the help text italicized and red. You can easily add on more styles or tweak these to get a different look.

The one problem with this age validator is that it doesn’t account for the user entering an age that doesn’t make sense. For example, because the validation only checks to see if the input is an integer, no help text is displayed if the user enters an age of -5 or 212. And until someone uncovers the fountain of youth, an age much over 100 can probably be considered invalid. You could account for negative ages by possibly creating a whole new validation function that only allows positive integers, but in this particular case a range validation makes more sense.

The age range validation is responsible for first checking that an input value is an integer, and then further checking to see if the integer is within an acceptable range, such as 1 to 120. Following is how you might put together a validation function to carry out this task:

function validateAge(inputControl, helpControl) {
  // First see if the input value is an integer
  if (!validateInteger(inputControl, helpControl))
    return false;
  // Then see if the input value is in the prescribed range
  if (inputControl.value < 1 || inputControl.value > 120) {
    if (helpControl != null)
      helpControl.innerHTML = "Please enter an age in the
      range 1 - 120.";
    return false;
  }
  else {
    if (helpControl != null)
      helpControl.innerHTML = "";
    return true;
  }
}

Notice that this function first calls the validateInteger() function to check and make sure the input is indeed a valid integer. There’s no sense in moving on to the range check if the input isn’t an integer. If the input is an integer, a range check is performed. The result of the range check is to simply set or clear text in the help control. The help control is checked for a null value in the event that you forget or decide not to include a help control, in which case you don’t want an error to occur.

Even if your JavaScript experience is shaky or nonexistent, you should be able to isolate the key part of the validateAge() function and modify it to fit your needs. For example, you can change the range of the validation check by simply changing this line of code:

if (inputControl.value < 1 || inputControl.value > 120) {

Let’s say you want to validate an NFL (National Football League) quarterback rating, which must be in the range 0 to 158. Here’s how you would change the range-testing line of code:

if (inputControl.value < 0 || inputControl.value > 158) {

Note

Please don’t ask why an NFL quarterback rating is in the range 0 to 158 (actually 158.33), as opposed to something more logical such as 0 to 10. It’s a tricky calculation that factors in variables such as pass completion percentage, yards per attempt, touchdowns per attempt, etc. Unless you’re a total (American) football nerd, just accept the range and move on. Otherwise, feel free to learn more at http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passer_rating.

Hopefully you can see that crafting your own validation functions out of the base set I’ve provided isn’t terribly difficult.

It’s also possible to extend the Ajax-style validation employed by the Validator application in looking up the city and state of a ZIP code. For example, a popular Ajax validation is to check a database of user names to ensure uniqueness when registering a new user. In this case, you would issue an Ajax request to your server script that passes along the user name entered, and the server script then checks the name against a database of existing user names to see if it is unique. The response could be as simple as “yes” or “no” for the uniqueness validation. So the client just needs to look at this result and display a help message accordingly.

The point to this makeover discussion is that I’ve really only exposed the tip of the iceberg in terms of data validation and Ajax. It’s up to you to take what you’ve learned and the code I’ve provided to further expand on the validation techniques as needed.

Summary

Although this book presents lots of interesting Ajax applications, some with impressive visuals and live data feeds, from a purely practical perspective few of them are as immediately handy as the Validator application. The application has a very basic user interface, but it’s the usability that shines here. The help text that appears when invalid data is entered is subtle but compelling. And consider that this help information appears immediately without you having to submit any data—this is the “Ajax” way of doing things, even when an Ajax server request isn’t explicitly being made. Of course, the Validator application does involve an Ajax server request for obtaining the city and state for a ZIP code, but the fact remains that the application promotes an “Ajax” feel throughout, even when the validation is taking place solely on the client.

Chapter 8. A Killer Interface for Image Viewing

Ajax and Early Plumbing

It’s hard to believe, but Ajax has some humble beginnings in sixteenth-century plumbing. In 1596 Sir John Harrington invented a flush toilet called “The Ajax” for Queen Elizabeth I of England. The Ajax toilet design didn’t exactly catch on in England, but it did see some success in France. The modern S-trap toilet design didn’t appear until nearly 200 years later.

We all know a picture is worth a thousand words, especially in the highly visual realm of the modern Web. However, the current technique for viewing images on the Web is a bit primitive and boring. I’m assuming that you’re using a small image thumbnail as a link to a full-size version of an image, which makes sense for most photographs given that you don’t want to hog screen space on a page with a huge photograph. The traditional way of handling thumbnail images is to simply link to the full-size image, in which case the browser opens and displays the image as if it is another Web page. This counts as a page navigation, meaning you have to click the Back button on your browser to return to the original page, including a possible reload. This is a less-than-ideal way to view images and involves far too much loading and reloading. “Ajax” offers a much better alternative.

This chapter presents you with a slick “Ajax” image viewer that eliminates the need to open the full size version of a thumbnail image in another page, thereby ditching the whole Back button reload problem. I keep quoting the word “Ajax” because you don’t technically need Ajax to carry out this image-viewing functionality. You’ll find out why as you dig into the challenge for this chapter, but understand that this chapter presents a perfect example of an Ajax-style effect that doesn’t technically involve an Ajax request.

The following files are used by the Imaginator application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap08 example code folder:

  • imaginator.html—Main Web page

  • smokiesXX.jpg—Series of Smoky Mountain example images

  • smokiesXX_thumb.jpg—Thumbnailed series of Smoky Mountain example images

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • modalimgkit.js—Modal image-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • modalimgkit.css—Style sheet for modal images

  • modalimgkit_wait.gif—Animated “loading” icon that is displayed while loading a modal image

  • modalimgkit_gray.png—Transparent gray image that is displayed against the background page while viewing a modal image in the foreground

The Challenge: Loading Images Dynamically

An inherent problem exists with how thumbnail images are typically handled on Web pages. The root of the problem is that you must initiate a full-blown HTTP request via a link in order to view the full-size image. This means the image is loaded as if it is another Web page, meaning that the browser navigates away from the page with the thumbnail to a new “page” containing the image. If you’re on a slow Internet connection or are viewing a particularly large image, the time spent waiting on the image to load can be confusing because there is no visual cue that the image is being loaded, unless you consider a frozen browser a cue.

A secondary problem is that you have to click the Back button in order to return to the original page with the thumbnail. This is important because the idea of the Forward and Back buttons on a browser is to lead you between pages that are unique from one another. In other words, leaving a page via a link should take you somewhere completely different, not just provide you with a different view of something that is already on the page. A view of a full-size image doesn’t constitute “new” content, which is why I really dislike the idea of having to browse forward to view an image, and then browse back to return to the original page.

So can Ajax help solve this problem? Not really. The problem with Ajax in this scenario is that it is designed around the notion of querying a server for text data. As you are probably well aware, images are binary, not text. If you’re curious as to that distinction, try dragging and dropping an image onto your favorite text editor. You’ll see a bunch of garbled characters that don’t make any sense, because image formats aren’t geared toward text representation. Because of this, you’d have serious problems in trying to request an image via Ajax.

We’ve established that Ajax can’t be used to dynamically load an image via a request, which is a bummer because this would definitely solve the whole “load a new page to view an image” dilemma. But this doesn’t mean we’re out of options. In fact, a mechanism in the standard JavaScript Image object makes it possible to achieve the general effect of Ajax without ever issuing an Ajax request. I’m referring to the onload event in the Image object, which notifies you when an image has finished loading. This event in many ways works like an Ajax request because it issues a notification when an image finishes loading.

Another component of the Image object that assists the onload event handler is the complete member variable, which is a Boolean (true/false) piece of data that lets you know whether an image has finished loading. When combined with the onload event handler, the complete member variable gives you everything you need to construct an “Ajax” image viewer.

Although I’m sure you would enjoy a fascinating in-depth discussion of the onload event handler and complete member variable, especially if you aren’t a JavaScript whiz, I’ll spare you the suffering. Instead, I’m happy to share that I’ve already built some JavaScript code that allows you to inject a slick image viewer into your pages with almost no work at all. Seriously, you’re going to be surprised at the utter simplicity of this chapter’s example.

The code that supports the image viewer is part of the modalimgkit.js file included as part of the Ajax Toolkit on the CD-ROM with this book, and it’s also a part of the source code files for the Imaginator sample application. As was originally mentioned back in Chapter 2, “Inside an Ajax Application,” the modalimg.js file offers a single JavaScript function you can use to automatically convert image links on an entire page to Ajax-style image viewer links. This function is called initModal(), and it must be called in the onload event handler of the body of a page containing image links.

The beauty of the initModal() function is that it automatically scans through the page finding image links and then alters them so that they use a dynamic Ajax-style image viewer. Because the function automatically doctors every image link it finds on a page, you don’t have to do anything more than call it once in order to activate the dynamic image viewer.

Note

The word modal in the modalimgkit.js file refers to the fact that the dynamic image viewer uses a modal window, which is a window that prevents you from interacting with parent windows while it is visible. Modal windows are used a great deal throughout most modern operating systems via dialog boxes. For example, the About box in most Windows applications is a modal window. The dynamic image viewer uses modal windows so that you can’t accidentally click the window in the background while viewing an image.

Note

In case you’re wondering, other Ajax-style image viewer toolkits are available that you can download and use for free. Some of them are even more powerful than the one you learn about here. However, you’ll be hard pressed to find one as compact and easy to use as this one. My goal was to simplify and minimize the code as much as possible while still providing a slick visual feature. Sometimes less really is more!

The Design: Designing a Dynamic Image Viewer

The sample application in this chapter is called Imaginator, and its job is primarily to demonstrate how ridiculously easy it is to use the modal image viewer portion of the Ajax Toolkit included with the book. All the Imaginator application really has to do is present several linked thumbnail images, and then properly call the initModal() function to turn on the image viewer. From there, everything is automatic.

You also have to include the modalimgkit.js JavaScript file in the Imaginator page, as well as its associated style sheet, modalimgkit.css. This style sheet defines the styles used by the modal image window to get a clean look when displaying an image on the screen.

The Imaginator application presents a series of thumbnail images used as the basis for dynamic image viewing, which means that clicking any one of these images results in the loading of the full-size image associated with the thumbnail and the tracking of the loading status. The Imaginator page stays in view at all times, although it is hidden behind a transparent gray overlay while an image is being displayed by the image viewer. Figure 8.1 shows how only one image in the series of thumbnail images is capable of being viewed at full size at any given time.

Figure 8.1. The Imaginator application consists of a series of linked thumbnail images that can be viewed individually at full size.

What this figure doesn’t show is how the entire Imaginator Web page is automatically disabled by the image viewer while a full-size image is being displayed. Not only that, but an animated “loading” image is displayed while the image is loading to help clue the user in on the fact that he or she is waiting for the image to load. This image is part of the image viewer, but there’s nothing stopping you from changing it to something else. Figure 8.2 shows the modalimgkit_wait.gif image used by the image viewer.

Figure 8.2. The dynamic image viewer used by the Imaginator makes use of an animated “loading” image (modalimgkit_wait.gif) to let the user know an image is being loaded.

The animated “loading” image is displayed in place of the image while the image is being loaded. Once the image finishes loading, it replaces the “loading” image. You can think of the “loading” image as a placeholder that appears in lieu of an image while it is being loaded.

Putting Together the Imaginator Application

Assembling the Imaginator application is quite trivial thanks to the image viewer in the Ajax Toolkit—the application consists of only an HTML Web page and several images. More specifically, the Web page imaginator.html includes a series of thumbnail images that link to a full-size version of each thumbnail. Figure 8.3 shows the Imaginator application with a full-size modal image in view and the series of thumbnail images visible in the background.

Figure 8.3. The Imaginator Web page consists of a series of thumbnail images that link to their full-size counterparts.

The body of the Imaginator Web page is responsible for arranging the thumbnail images on the page and establishing links to the full-size images. Following is the code for the body of the page:

<body onload="initModal()">
  <p>Following are some pictures from a recent trip to the Great
    Smoky Mountains.</p>
  <table>
    <tr>
      <td><a href="smokies01.jpg"><img src="smokies01_thumb.jpg"
        alt="The Great Smoky Mountains" style="border:none"/>
        </a></td>
      <td><a href="smokies02.jpg"><img src="smokies02_thumb.jpg"
        alt="The Great Smoky Mountains" style="border:none"/>
        </a></td>
      <td><a href="smokies03.jpg"><img src="smokies03_thumb.jpg"
        alt="The Great Smoky Mountains" style="border:none"/>
        </a></td>
      <td><a href="smokies04.jpg"><img src="smokies04_thumb.jpg"
        alt="The Great Smoky Mountains" style="border:none"/>
        </a></td>
      <td><a href="smokies05.jpg"><img src="smokies05_thumb.jpg"
        alt="The Great Smoky Mountains" style="border:none"/>
        </a></td>
    </tr>
  </table>
</body>

The most important piece of code in the entire application is the first line of code in the body, which calls the initModal() function in the Ajax Toolkit to initialize the dynamic image viewer. This function is located in the file modalimgkit.js, and its job is to take care of processing all the image links on the page so that they use the Ajax-style image viewer instead of a traditional link approach. The initModal() function also preloads the images used by modal images, including the animated “loading” icon (modalimgkit_wait.gif) and the transparent gray background image (modalimgkit_gray.png).

The remaining code in the body of the page is straight HTML code that wraps <a> tags around <img> tags to create linked thumbnail images. Notice that the thumbnail images are all named with _thumb as the filename suffix. You don’t have to use this identical naming convention, but it’s a very good idea to establish some consistency when naming related files, such as thumbnail and full-size image pairs.

Earlier I mentioned that you need to import the image viewer files from the Ajax Toolkit into the Imaginator Web page. These files include a CSS style sheet and a JavaScript file. Following is how you import the style sheet, which is named modalimgkit.css:

<link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="modalimgkit.css" />

Similarly, the modalimgkit.js JavaScript file is imported with the following line of code:

<script type="text/javascript" src="modalimgkit.js"> </script>

Both of these lines of code must be placed in the head of the page. Just to make sure you understand how the files are imported, here is the full head of the Imaginator Web page:

<head>
  <title>Imaginator</title>

  <link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css"
    href="modalimgkit.css" />
  <script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
  <script type="text/javascript" src="modalimgkit.js"> </script>
</head>

This code reveals how the Imaginator Web page also includes the domkit.js file from the Ajax Toolkit, which offers several helper functions for carrying out standard JavaScript DOM tasks such as determining the client window size, getting and replacing element text, etc.

The only other missing ingredients in the Imaginator application are the two images required by the image viewer. You’ve already seen one of them, modalimgkit_wait, which serves as an animated “loading” image that is shown while an image is being loaded. The other, modalimgkit_gray.png, is a tiny gray image with transparency that is used to gray out the background window while an image is being viewed. This image is able to gray out the entire background by being repeated across the image viewer window behind the image itself. This visual trick is accomplished by the image viewer’s CSS style sheet. All you need to worry about is making sure the two images are in the same folder as the Imaginator Web page.

When you click a link to a modal image, the tiny gray transparent image is tiled across the background to give the underlying page a “disabled” appearance, while the animated “loading” image is displayed in the center of the page during the loading of the modal image. Once the modal image finishes loading, it replaces the animated image. When you click to close the modal image, both it and the gray transparent background image go away.

Testing Imaginator

Testing the Imaginator application is pretty much as simple as putting it together, and involves clicking thumbnail images and making sure they appear properly in full size. Figure 8.4 shows the Imaginator application open initially with the thumbnail images in view and no full-size image displayed.

Figure 8.4. When the Imaginator application first begins, you see only a series of thumbnail images.

To see the dynamic image viewer in action, click one of the thumbnail images. Figure 8.5 shows the “loading” image that appears while the image is being loaded.

Figure 8.5. A “loading” image appears in front of a grayed background while a full-size image is being loaded.

It’s very important to note how the background behind the “loading” image has been grayed to prevent access to the underlying page while the image is being loaded. This visually hammers home the fact that the image viewer is modal, and helps to put total focus on the full-size image, or in this case the fact that it is being loaded.

After a few short moments, the full-size image will appear in place of the “loading” image, as shown in Figure 8.6.

Figure 8.6. Once loaded, the full-size image appears in place of the “loading” image.

Again, the background window remains grayed while the full-size image is being viewed. To close the image, just click it with the mouse or press Enter or the spacebar on the keyboard. You are immediately returned to the original page where you can view another image or otherwise resume Web surfing.

The key to the Imaginator application is that you never needed to navigate away from the original page in order to view a full-size image. Not only that, but you are presented with a very clear visual cue that an image is being loaded, as opposed to you just waiting for the browser to churn away while it opens the full-size image in a new “page.”

Giving Imaginator an Extreme Ajax Makeover

You may be wondering how there could be much room to make over the Imaginator application given that the image viewer is quite focused in solving a very specific problem. Although you could certainly consider enhancing the image viewer itself by possibly supporting captions or annotated audio clips, that would only serve to complicate things, or at the very least require considerable development effort.

A more apt extreme makeover for the Imaginator application is to experiment with your own images. In fact, the best way to experiment is to take an existing page with thumbnail images on it and just try out the image viewer with no other changes. All you have to do is add the two import lines of code to the head of the page and then add the initModal() function call to the body’s onload event handler. Then make sure you copy the source files (modalimgkit.css and modalimgkit.js) and images (modalimgkit_wait.gif and modalimgkit_gray.png) over to the folder containing your page, and you’re good to go. This is a great test because it shows how truly plug-and-play the dynamic image viewer can be.

Another fun way to make over the Imaginator application is to try out a different animated “loading” image. If you’re a crafty graphic designer, you may be able to whip one up on your own. For the rest of us, there is a very cool online tool that allows you to generate your own custom Ajax “loading” images. The Web site is called AjaxLoad.info (http://www.ajaxload.info/), and you can see it in action in Figure 8.7.

Figure 8.7. The AjaxLoad.info Web site allows you to generate custom animated “loading” images for Ajax applications.

Using this tool, you pick the type of indicator (icon) along with its background and foreground colors, and the tool generates an animated image for you. You can even look at a preview in order to fine-tune an image before you elect to download the finished result.

Once you arrive at an animated “loading” image that you like, just copy it over the existing modalimgkit_wait.gif image in the Imaginator folder (or the folder of your own Web page). Replacing the GIF file will immediately replace the image in the image viewer, although it may require a browser refresh if you’ve already opened the Imaginator page prior to copying the image.

Summary

One of the goals of this book is to build practical Ajax sample applications whose building blocks are as plug-and-play as possible. Although some components of the book’s Ajax Toolkit are certainly more plug-and-play than others, few are as easy and offer as much impact as the dynamic image viewer, which provides a slick “Web 2.0”–style image viewer in exchange for only a few lines of code in your Web page. I encourage you to take advantage of the image viewer’s ease of use by plugging it into your own pages and not looking back—it’s rare that you get such painless bang for your buck in the world of Web development.

Chapter 9. Presenting Information with Slick Popups

Ajax the Town

If you ever want to visit Ajax, you can do so by venturing into south central Ontario, Canada, where you’ll find the town of Ajax. Situated on the shores of Lake Ontario, Ajax is considered a suburb of Toronto. The town name came about as a result of the munitions plant that was there throughout World War II. Due to its role as a WWII munitions provider, the town was named after the warship that won the first major WWII British naval battle, HMS Ajax.

One of the most visible uses of Ajax across the Web is popup windows that automatically appear when you click or hover over a link. Even with the negative stigma associated with popup windows, the Ajax popup window technique can be quite handy when used intelligently. For example, such a popup can be displayed in response to hovering over a link, and it can provide a preview of a page before the user actually traverses the link via a click. Or even better, you can expand the notion of a “link” so that it shares information directly in a popup window instead of linking to an entirely new page. This can simplify the navigation of a site. The user is not taken to a new page unless a legitimate page worth of information is available—bite-size information can be displayed in popups.

This chapter explores the notion of an Ajax-powered information window, or info window, which is just a popup window that is dynamically fed with data obtained through an Ajax request. Info windows can be used to display just about anything, but the general idea is to display a nugget of information that doesn’t necessarily warrant its own complete page. By using an info window, you make the information more immediately accessible and eliminate the need for a new page load and browser navigation.

The following files are used by the Informer application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap09 example code folder:

  • snapper.html—Snapper main Web page

  • informer.html—Informer main Web page

  • skateXXX.html—Series of example Web pages related to skateboards

  • skateXXX.jpg—Series of skateboard example images

  • ajaxkit.js—Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • infowinkit.js—Information window-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • infowinkit.css—Style sheet for information windows

  • infowinkit_wait.gif—Animated “loading” icon that is displayed while loading information into an info window

The Challenge: Creating Linked Popups

Although I would certainly agree with the notion that popup windows have become one of the most negative “innovations” in the short history of the Web, sometimes a slight repurposing of a negative can lead to a positive. There’s a fine line between “helpful” and “annoying.” Unfortunately, popup windows have largely erred on the side of the latter. Ajax presents an opportunity to use popups in a more helpful and less obtrusive manner.

Remember that the killer feature of Ajax is its ability to query the server for bits and pieces of data, as opposed to complete Web pages. This book has already presented you with numerous examples of how it is much more efficient to dynamically load a chapter of an e-book, a stock quote, or a piece of validated user input without having to make a complete roundtrip full-page request on the server. The idea thus far, however, has been to load a chunk of information from the server and somehow display it on the current page. But what if it doesn’t make sense to use the current page for display purposes?

In some situations it might be a distraction to display the results of an Ajax request directly on the current page. Consider the analogy of a sidebar or note box in this book. A sidebar or note box is intended to provide some kind of supplemental information. However, it gets placed on the page smack in the middle of the main chapter text, and thus competes for your attention. It would be much better if you could somehow turn a note on and off at will, instead of having to contend with it competing visually with the main discussion.

Note

This note is admittedly a bit distracting because it eats up precious space on the printed page and interrupts the flow of the main chapter text. Sure, you could ignore it, but you obviously aren’t or you wouldn’t be reading this sentence. It would be nice if you could tap the page with your finger and make this note box go away. Unfortunately, I haven’t figured out how to inject Ajax into the printed page!

The point I’m making is that a popup window can be handy in a situation where you have some additional information to present but you don’t want to cloud the current page being viewed. Additionally, it would be extremely handy if you didn’t have to load this extra information on the front end, and instead only load it as needed. Ajax makes it possible to access such information on a need-to-know basis and then display it in a popup window that only temporarily takes up screen space.

There is obviously a lot of ill will over the abuse of popups thanks to aggressive online advertisers. For this reason, some users are averse to popups of any kind. Knowing this, it’s important to be very judicious in your usage of popups, and even when justified don’t force them on users. What users really hate is not having any control over the display of a popup, as is often the case with annoying popup ad windows. A more positive usage of popups is to only display them in response to a click or possibly a hover. In the latter case, it is important to automatically hide the popup once the hover finishes (i.e., when the mouse pointer leaves the element it was hovering over).

A popular Ajax popup technique that has been used with success involves the hover approach I just mentioned. I’m referring to a link preview popup where the contents of the page associated with a link are displayed in a small popup when you hover over the link.

A good example of this technique is the Snap Preview site (http://www.snap.com/), which displays a popup window that presents a preview of a linked page. Although you could write your own custom hover preview window in Ajax, there is no need because Snap Preview is in fact an online service that automates the process. Snap Preview allows you to turn all of the links on your Web pages to Snap Preview links.

Note

Another service similar to Snap Preview is Kontera (http://www.kontera.com/), which uses hover popup windows to display advertising associated with a link. Although Kontera links are still ad-based popups, they are much less intrusive than popup windows that appear without any warning.

To Snap-enable your own pages, you must first register your site with Snap at http://www.snap.com.

In addition to registering you with Snap, the Snap registration process also provides you with the JavaScript code necessary to add the Snap Preview feature to your pages. This Snap Preview JavaScript code snippet is contained within a standard <script> tag. Following is an example of such a snippet, which must be placed in the head of a Web page in order for it to use the Snap Preview feature:

<script defer="defer" id="snap_preview_anywhere"
  type="text/javascript"
  src="http://spa.snap.com/snap_preview_anywhere.js?
  ap=1&amp;key=c6a9f...&amp;
  sb=0&amp;domain=www.stalefishlabs.com"></script>

This code is actually included as part of a sample Web page I put together (snapper.html) to demonstrate how easy it is to use Snap Preview. Here’s the code for the body of the page:

<body>
  <p>Here are some links:</p>
  <p><a href="http://www.usatoday.com/">USA Today</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.amazon.com/">Amazon.com</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.nhl.com/">National Hockey League</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.snopes.com/">Snopes.com</a></p>
  <p><a href="http://www.michaelmorrison.com/">Michael's Web
site</a></p>
</body>

As you can see, nothing specific about this code ties it to the Snap Preview functionality—the Snap Preview script automatically alters every link on a page so that it uses the feature. Figure 9.1 shows what the resulting page looks like with a preview popup window in view.

Figure 9.1. The Snap Preview popup window appears any time you hover over a link on the page, providing a small-scale preview of the page.

It’s very important to note that Snap Preview popups appear and disappear in response to mouse hovers and exits, not mouse clicks. When you hover over a link, a popup automatically appears, and when you hover off of (exit) the link, the popup disappears. Some people feel that even this approach to popups is a bit intrusive because it is easy to inadvertently trigger a preview when moving the mouse around on the page. An even less aggressive approach is to tie information popups to mouse clicks.

And finally we arrive at the challenge for this chapter—information popup windows that are initiated by mouse clicks. I think the Snap Preview approach to popups, which is also employed in a slightly different manner in sites such as NetFlix, is very cool, but I agree with the complaint that it can be annoying when you accidentally trigger a popup by hovering (dragging) over a link. In my opinion, the safer route is to rely on mouse clicks for popup windows. Of course, the click approach doesn’t work in some cases, such as Snap Preview, because there truly is a link that must be followed when a click occurs.

The focus of this chapter is on information popup windows that don’t apply to traditional links to other pages. The idea here is to essentially create a special “popup link” or “info link” that is only used when you want to display a nugget of information. This makes it possible to trigger the popup on a mouse click as opposed to a hover, and thus minimize any potential negative impact on the usability of a page. The good news is that I’ve already written the bulk of the code that makes information popup windows possible.

The code that supports information popups is part of the infowinkit.js file included as part of the Ajax Toolkit on the CD-ROM with this book, and it’s also a part of the source code files for the Informer sample application. As was originally mentioned back in Chapter 2, “Inside an Ajax Application,” the infowinkit.js file offers two JavaScript functions that are used to support dynamic information popup windows:

  • initInfoWindow()—Initialize the information popup window for the entire page

  • showInfoWindow()—Show a specific piece of dynamically loaded information in the popup window

The initInfoWindow() function is used to initialize the information popup window, and must be called in the onload event handler of the body of a page containing info links. The showInfoWindow() function is then called for each info link in the link’s onclick event handler; this is what actually causes the popup window to get displayed. You learn more about the specifics of these two functions as you get into the nuts and bolts of the Informer application later in the chapter.

Getting back to the challenge associated with the Informer application, you know that information popups are used to share information. In this particular example you create a Web page about the sport of skateboarding, using information popups to describe the primary equipment involved. The idea is that the main content on the page simply mentions each piece of equipment, whereas the specifics of each piece are delegated to info links (popups). So, you’ll be wrapping info links around several pieces of text on the page so that the text is linked to information popups.

The Design: Designing an Information Popup Application

The Informer application is really just a normal Web page with a few specialty links that are made interesting with the assistance of information popup windows. Because an info link is triggered via a mouse click, the associated popup window appears over the clicked link. Figure 9.2 shows the conceptual user interface for the Informer application, complete with a popup window that appears over an info link.

Figure 9.2. The user interface for the Informer application consists of a standard Web page with special info links capable of triggering a popup window.

The figure reveals how a link on the page results in the display of a popup window that is overlaid on the page where the link is located. Although the code that powers the popups is contained in the Ajax Toolkit, the information that gets displayed in the popups is HTML code that is ultimately your responsibility. This HTML code is stored in separate files that map one-to-one with information popup windows. This allows the Informer application to make an Ajax request for a specific file in response to each popup window. So you can think of the contents of each popup window as a mini Web page.

The design of the application therefore isn’t just about importing the appropriate JavaScript code but also involves the creation of content for the popup windows. The good news is that this content is pure HTML code with no special scripting required.

Although the Ajax request within the Informer application takes place inside the infowinkit.js file, it’s worth evaluating what is involved in making a request on behalf of an information popup. The next few sections quickly explore the Ajax request and response associated with an information popup.

The Client Request

The information displayed in popup windows within the Informer application is loaded from discrete files that act as mini Web pages. For example, clicking a link for skateboard wheels will display a popup that shows a picture of wheels and a description of them. This picture and description are stored in an HTML file such as skatewheels.html, which is dynamically loaded via an Ajax request when the popup window is displayed. The client request for an information popup window therefore consists solely of the URL for an information Web page.

Keep in mind that the Web page URL sent along in a client request doesn’t actually reference a complete Web page. This is because the resulting HTML content will be injected into an existing popup window, as opposed to being opened in a browser as a true Web page. You can think of the HTML content for an information window as an HTML document “fragment.”

The Server Response

The document “fragment” notion for information popup windows should clue you in to what the server returns in response to a request made by the Informer application. Indeed, the server simply returns a document fragment as stored in the HTML file referenced by the request URL. So, in the skateboard wheel example, the client request includes the URL of the file (skatewheels.html), whereas the server response is the actual HTML code for the mini page.

Because the server is responding with a complete file (even if its contents represent a fragment of HTML code), it isn’t necessary to do any special processing on the server. Therefore, you don’t need a PHP script of any kind for the Informer application.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

The Informer application uses very straightforward Ajax communication thanks to the Ajax Toolkit. Figure 9.3 reveals how the process of dynamically loading content into an information popup window involves sending a URL and getting HTML code (content) in response.

Figure 9.3. The Informer application uses a URL as the basis for a client request that results in information content being delivered by the server.

A client request in the Informer application consists solely of the URL for the HTML file representing the information content. No parameters are necessary since you’re only interested in the HTML file, so the Ajax request results in the loading of an entire file, which in this case contains HTML code for an information “page.”
