Or save yourself some time and jump straight to the ZIP code field on the page. First try entering a ZIP code that doesn’t even exist in the real world (yet), such as 20080. Figure 11.7 shows how the application displays an error/helper message to indicate that shipping rate information for the ZIP code doesn’t exist.

Figure 11.7. Entering an invalid ZIP code results in an error/helper message beside the ZIP code field.

In reality, any ZIP code you enter other than 20008 (Washington, DC) is considered invalid for testing purposes, so you can enter just about anything and get the error message. To see a successful shipping rate calculation, enter 20008 as the ZIP code and navigate off the ZIP code field. Figure 11.8 shows the dynamically retrieved shipping charge and the newly calculated order total.

Figure 11.8. Entering a valid ZIP code results in a successful Ajax request of the shipping rate, which also results in the calculation of the order total.

Unless you were paying careful attention, you might have missed the “loading” image that appeared in place of the shipping charge while the request was taking place. Try again by selecting a different shipping method and watching the shipping rate closely. Figure 11.9 shows the “loading” image in action.

Figure 11.9. The small “loading” image appears in place of the shipping charge while an Ajax request is being carried out.

This figure shows how the “loading” image provides a subtle but effective indication that something is taking place in the background related to the shipping rate. When the new rate is obtained, the image disappears and the new rate is displayed (see Figure 11.10).

Figure 11.10. Changing shipping methods also results in a request, and therefore a new shipping rate and order total.

Although I’d like to tell you to experiment with all kinds of different ZIP codes to see how the application responds to shipping an item all over the country, this isn’t an option with the USPS test server. You’ll need to take an additional step to enable the application so that it can access the full range of shipping rates. The next section tells you what to do.

Giving Shipper an Extreme Ajax Makeover

Because the Shipper application bases its shipping options on U.S. Postal Service shipping methods, it is highly dependent upon the U.S. Postal Service Domestic Rates calculator Web service, which is part of the USPS Web Tools (http://www.usps.com/webtools/). Unlike many of the other third-party Web services used throughout the book, the USPS Web Tools are tightly controlled and can only be used by carefully coordinating your development efforts with the USPS.

More specifically, you must go through a validation process in order to move beyond the limited test data on the USPS test server and try out your application on the USPS production server. This validation is carried out by the USPS Internet Customer Care Center (ICCC), which is available at icustomercare@usps.com or 1-800-344-7779 (7:00 a.m. to 11:00 p.m. EST). You must already be a registered USPS Web Tools user (with an ID) and have tested the application using the Domestic Rates Web Tool server before contacting the ICCC for access to the production server. The first part of the Shipper extreme makeover, therefore, is simply to gain access to the USPS production server so that you can try out a full range of test ZIP codes.

Note

You will have to contact the ICCC in order to use the Shipper application in any real capacity because it is limited to using only USPS-specified test data when running on the USPS test server.

After you’ve been validated by the ICCC and obtained the URL of the Domestic Rates Web Tool production server, be sure to change the Shipper application to use the production server by changing the following line of code near the beginning of the shipper.php file:

$uspsRequest =
  "http://testing.shippingapis.com/ShippingAPITest.dll?
  API=RateV2&";

The first part of the URL in this code, http://testing.shippingapis.com/Shipping-APITest.dll, is the URL of the USPS test server and is the part of the code you’ll need to change to the production URL. This should be the only change required to move the Shipper application from the USPS test server to the production server, and thus open you up to trying out any ZIP codes to calculate shipping charges.

Once you get the Shipper application successfully running on the USPS production server, you’ll want to seriously consider incorporating the Ajax code into your own Web pages. Unfortunately, this facet of the extreme makeover is entirely dependent upon your specific site design, so it’s hard to give specifics about how to carry out the design and implementation. What I can do, however, is provide a general strategy for incorporating the Ajax shipping calculator into an existing shopping cart.

First off, you’ll need to import the two relevant Ajax Toolkit files, like this:

<script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>

You then need to make sure you have an input text element for the ZIP code input field, as well as a span element for the ZIP code error message. If you already have a ZIP code input element, make sure to add an onchange event handler similar to the one in the Shipper application. Here’s the relevant code from the Shipper Web page:

<td class="value"><input type="text" id="zipCode" size="10"
  onchange="getShipRate()" /></td>
<td class="label" width="50%"><span id="zipCodeErr"
  style="font-size:80%; color:#BB0000;"></span></td>

Notice that the ZIP code input element calls the getShipRate() function to dynamically grab the shipping charge from the USPS server. You saw this function earlier, so I won’t repeat it here. However, you’ll need to copy and paste its code into the head of your page, along with the response handler function, handleRequest().

You may already have a select element for choosing the shipping method, but if you don’t you’ll need to add that to your page. Following is the selection list used in the Shipper application to offer a choice of USPS shipping methods:

<select id="mailService" onchange="getShipRate()">
  <option value="Parcel Post">Parcel Post</option>
  <option value="Priority Mail" selected="selected">
    Priority Mail</option>
  <option value="Express Mail to PO Addressee">
    Express Mail</option>
</select>

It is extremely important to notice how the value of each option is set to exactly match the shipping method as specified in the Domestic Rates documentation for the USPS Web Tools. This is important because the Shipper application uses this value as the basis of the rate request, which means it must exactly match a shipping method in order for the lookup to work. In other words, value="US Priority Mail" won’t work because the shipping method is "Priority Mail", not "US Priority Mail".

Note

It is possible to support other USPS shipping methods beyond the three shown in the Shipper application. To find out about all of the available shipping methods, take a look at the USPS Web Tools documentation at http://www.usps.com/webtools/technical.htm. If you’d like to support a different shipping provider such as FedEx or UPS, you’ll need to dig into its documentation and do some work to support its request/response formats.

Finally, there are several “shopping cart” variables in the Shipper application that you’ll need to provide or wire to your existing shopping cart fields:

var origZip = "10022"; // Origination ZIP code
var itemDesc = "Popcorn Holiday Feast";
var itemPrice = 29.99;
var weightLb = "10";
var weightOz = "5";
var container = "Flat Rate Box";
var size = "Large";

Keep in mind that most of these variables will be somehow tied to real data that has either already been entered or selected in your shopping cart. I opted to make them static in the Shipper application just to keep things simple; my goal was showing how to calculate a shipping charge with Ajax, not how to build an entire shopping cart.

If you follow these general design guidelines, you should be able to tie the Ajax dynamic shipping charge lookup into your own Web site without too much trouble.

Summary

This chapter explored the dynamic lookup of shipping charges, which falls clearly within the realm of a problem needing an Ajax solution. It simply shouldn’t be necessary to navigate from one page to the next in a shopping cart just to find out shipping charges for an online purchase. Not only that, but all you really need to provide in order to determine the charges is your ZIP code. The Shipper application in this chapter demonstrated how the clever use of Ajax can make a subtle but substantial improvement to a shopping cart or any other application that needs to calculate a shipping charge on the fly and present it to the user.

Chapter 12. Adding an Ajax-Powered Rating System to Your Site

Ajax Yet Again in Cartoons

MGM’s Ajax Corporation isn’t the only time Ajax has been used in cartoons. Duckman was a regular show on the USA Network in the mid 1990s, and featured a character named Ajax Duckman, who was the son of the title character, Eric Duckman. Ajax Duckman was voiced by musician Dweezil Zappa, and was somewhat of a slacker teenager on the show.

An increasingly popular component of many popular Web sites is the ability to rate things. Amazon probably started the trend when it provided the ability to rate products and write your own reviews. Since then, all kinds of different Web sites offer a rating system of some sort, be it for rating movies, music, photographs, blog posts, whatever. If it can be posted online, it can be rated. I suppose deep down most of us are critics at heart, so the idea of being able to rate anything and everything somehow taps into that need to share our criticism and in many cases our praise. Admittedly, it probably isn’t fair to simplify everything in the world to a five-star rating, but it can give you a quick glimpse at how people perceive something.

This chapter is without a doubt the most ambitious chapter in the book in terms of how it utilizes Ajax to add five-star ratings to any content on a Web page. By ambitious, I simply mean that the Rater sample application is considerably more advanced in its usage of Ajax when you consider that it has to be able to read and write multiple ratings to a file or database on the server while preventing duplicate votes by the same user. The good news is that the vast majority of this complexity is hidden within the Ajax Toolkit, meaning that your job primarily boils down to properly using the Toolkit, as opposed to constructing your own rating code.

The following files are used by the Rater application in this chapter, and are located on the live Linux CD-ROM with the book in the chap12 example code folder:

  • rater.html—Rater main Web page

  • cookiereset.html—Cookie reset Web page for simulating unique users

  • ajaxkit.js— Core Ajax functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • domkit.js—DOM-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • ratingkit.js—Rating-related functions for the Ajax Toolkit

  • ratingkit.php—Server script for saving and loading ratings

  • ratingkit.css—Style sheet for five-star ratings

  • star.gif—Star images used to represent a five-star rating

  • star_sm.gif—Smaller star images used to represent a smaller five-star rating

The Challenge: Adding Ratings to a Web Site

A handy way of determining the importance of an item is to consider whether it would make the cut if you were to be stranded on a deserted island and could only bring a selected number of items. For example, what if you could only take a single music CD to listen to for the many years you might be stranded with no companionship (but hopefully plenty of batteries to power your CD player!)? What CD would you choose?

Hopefully you never end up having to actually make the deserted island CD selection. Short of that, you might find it useful to rate your favorite CDs on Amazon.com so that other people can make more educated music buying decisions. Amazon.com allows you to rate music CDs and pretty much everything else it has for sale using a five-star rating system. If you’re near a computer right now, take a moment to visit Amazon.com and view a music CD. This will clue you in as to how an online rating system works.

If you look at the five stars below the name of the artist and CD title, you’ll notice that out beside the stars is a count of how many customers issued ratings. This is very important because it gives you an idea as to how accurate the rating might be—if only a handful of people have rated the item, the rating might not mean so much.

The real reason for mentioning this rating example is to point out how the Amazon.com rating approach involves a fairly unimpressive user interface for choosing the rating. I’m referring to the selection list used to select a rating, which allows you to choose a text description of the star rating, such as “five stars” or “four stars.” Aside from the fact that Amazon.com also supports a text review to go with the rating, you must also preview and accept your rating before it goes into effect. It would be much more intuitive and efficient if you could just click directly on the five-star rating to rate the item right there on the main item page. Granted, this wouldn’t address the need for writing a review, but not everyone is interested in writing a review—sometimes a quick “hit-and-run” rating is all you need.

The challenge for this chapter is to use Ajax to incorporate an interactive five-star rating component into a Web page so that users can vote on items without leaving the page. This is in keeping with much of what you’ve already learned about Ajax in terms of minimizing page reloads when all you’re doing is tweaking a small amount of data. In this case, all you want to do is submit a single piece of rating data to the server and then see the total rating tally after the vote is processed. This is definitely within the realm of something Ajax can handle.

One thorny part of this challenge that you may already be curious about is the persistent storage of the rating votes. If you think back on all the sample Ajax applications you’ve seen thus far, this is the first one that has to remember something the user submits. More specifically, the Rater application must somehow store on the server each vote for each item after it is issued. Fortunately, I provide you with a prebuilt rating component that handles all the details of storing rating data on the server in an XML document. I also provide you with some ideas about possibly using a database instead of an XML file if you’re so inclined.

This rating component I’m talking about is included as part of the Ajax Toolkit included on the CD-ROM with this book. The component involves both client- and server-side code, as well as a few images and a style sheet. Behind the scenes, an Ajax-powered five-star rating system is a bit trickier than you might imagine, which is why so many files are necessary in order to work the magic.

Within the client-side JavaScript code for the rating component (contained in ratingkit.js), the following two functions are used to control the rating system:

  • initRatings()—Initializes the ratings for all of the items on a page

  • setRating()—Issues a rating vote for a single item

With a little help from the PHP server script (ratingkit.php), these functions make it possible to assign a rating to as many individual items on a page as you want. Not only that, but the rating component is smart enough to limit the number of votes on a given computer to a single vote per user. It does this through the clever use of cookies, which provide a perfect mechanism for remembering whether or not the user has taken a certain action (such as rating a particular item).

Hopefully I’ve sold you on the fact that I’ve already done the lion’s share of the ratings work by handing you a prebuilt component. What does that leave for the Rater application challenge? The challenge for the Rater application is to properly set up the HTML code for ratable items so that they tie into the rating component and issue their votes correctly. Also, several CSS rating classes are part of the rating component and must be carefully assigned to elements on the Rater page in order for the rating to work.

To keep the HTML code for the Rater application fairly simple and not involve a bunch of extra images or other resources, the items being rated are famous quotations. The Rater Web page consists of five famous quotations, and therefore five different ratings—one per quotation. The Rater Web page needs to include a unique rating component for each quotation and properly identify it so that votes on that quotation are stored on the server for future retrieval. When all is said and done, the Rater application will allow countless people all over the Web to vote on their favorite quotations, with all the rating votes tallied on the server and the total number of votes displayed on the page.

What you’ll take with you after developing the Rater application is the ability to reuse the rating component to rate any kind of Web content.

The Design: Designing a Rater Application

The design of the Rater application begins with the quotations themselves because they are driving the rating process in this particular example. Because the quotations are just text, it is sufficient to wrap them up in div elements without any special formatting or other code. Although a rating appears immediately below a quotation, there is nothing directly tying a rating and a quotation together in terms of HTML code.

The connection between a rating and a quotation has everything to do with the “item ID” of the rating, which is a piece of text used to uniquely identify a rating among other ratings. If you think of all the ratings on your Web site as one big collection, which they are, then each of them has a unique ID that allows you to tell them apart. The item ID should somehow give you a clue as to what quotation the rating is connected to. For example, you could use the last name of the quotation author as the ID for a rating, which is in fact what the Rater application uses.

The item ID is critical because it gets stored along with the rating votes, and is therefore what is used to associate a stored rating with a quotation on the page. Speaking of quotations on the page, Figure 12.1 shows the basic layout of the Rater user interface, including the quotations and their respective ratings.

Figure 12.1. The user interface for the Rater application consists of several text quotations with a rating component for each.

The figure reveals how each quotation is immediately followed by a rating and its associated helper text. The helper text initially just instructs you to click the rating in order to submit a vote. Once you’ve voted on a rating, the helper text changes to show you how many votes have been cast for the rating. So the helper text serves double duty—first as an instruction and then as a means of displaying rating information.

The rating component provided in the Ajax Toolkit handles all the details of the Ajax requests in the Rater application. The goal in this chapter is not to dissect the inner workings of the rater component but instead to show you how to use it to incorporate ratings into Web pages. Even so, it’s worth taking a quick peek at how Ajax is at work in the component in the form of client requests and server responses.

The Client Request

Two different client requests are involved with the rating component: one for initially obtaining rating information for all of the ratings on a page, and another for casting a vote for a particular rating. The first request occurs exactly one time when a page is initially loaded, whereas the second request can occur multiple times if you have multiple ratings on a page. Of course, the rating component does use cookies to prevent you from casting more than one vote on a given rating. But the fact remains that each vote on each rating results in an Ajax request.

When an Ajax request is made for all of the ratings, the client isn’t required to pass along any information. In other words, the URL for the Ajax request consists of nothing more than the PHP server script that reads the ratings from an XML file and returns the results.

Unlike the rating retrieval request, the Ajax request for casting a rating vote does require parameters. The parameters required of this request are the item ID and the rating itself (one to five stars). These two parameters are passed along to the server where they are properly integrated into the rating data, meaning that they are used to increment the number of votes and recalculate the average rating.

The Server Response

The server responses for the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit vary considerably depending on the request. When retrieving all of the ratings for the purposes of initializing the ratings on a page, the server returns all of the ratings that have been stored as an XML document. It is then up to the code on the client to determine which ratings apply to the page, based upon the rating IDs.

The server response for the setting of a rating involves the newly calculated rating average and the total number of votes that have been cast for the specified item ID. These two pieces of information are returned to the client script, which then updates the rating on the page appropriately.

Making Sense of the Client-to-Server Conversation

To help get a feel for the flow of data that takes place between the client and server in the Rater application (rater component), take a look at Figure 12.2.

Figure 12.2. Behind the scenes in the Rater application are two Ajax requests for initializing the ratings and submitting new rating votes.

The figure reveals how the initial rating request involves no flow of data from the client but returns a full-blown XML document containing all of the ratings. Because no parameters are required when the Ajax request is submitted, the URL for the request is simply the name of the PHP server script:

ratingkit.php

Although the data flowing from the client to the server is quite minimal, the data returned in the other direction is much more interesting. Following is an example of what the response XML document looks like:

<?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>
<ratings>
  <rating>
    <id>twain</id>
    <avg>4.5</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>ali</id>
    <avg>3.33333333333</avg>
    <votes>3</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>einstein</id>
    <avg>3</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>shaw</id>
    <avg>4</avg>
    <votes>2</votes>
  </rating>
  <rating>
    <id>paine</id>
    <avg>4</avg>
    <votes>1</votes>
  </rating>
 </ratings>

The structure of this XML document is pretty simple in that it consists of a list of ratings, each of which has three distinct pieces of data:

  • Item ID (<id>)

  • Rating average (<avg>)

  • Number of votes (<votes>)

This information is sufficient to describe a rating for a single item. Each <rating> tag in the XML document corresponds to a rating for a single item. The document as a whole serves as the data format for the ratings, meaning that if you open the ratings.xml file generated on the server, you’ll see the same XML code. It is important to note that this file serves as the main database for all ratings on your site, potentially across multiple pages. The key is the item ID, which allows you to use ratings anywhere you want on your site as long as they are properly ID’d.

Note

As you find out later in the chapter in the “Giving Rater an Extreme Ajax Makeover” section, item IDs can be any text you want. Although the Rater application uses names as IDs, you can just as easily use numbers or a combination of letters and numbers. The important thing is to use item IDs that are unique and easy to associate with an item. For example, an item ID for a rating of a book might use the book’s International Standard Book Number (ISBN) so that the ID is guaranteed unique and also has an obvious association with the book.

If you ever want to view the XML data stored for the ratings on your site, just enter the rating component’s PHP server script (ratingkit.php) in your browser with no parameters or anything. Figure 12.3 shows the rating XML code as it appears in a browser by directly opening the server script.

Figure 12.3. Directly entering the URL of the PHP server script for the rating component results in the display of the rating XML document.

The other Ajax request used in the Rater application involves storing a new rating. The underlying code in the server script used to process this request is a bit messier because it has to be smart enough to create a new XML file (ratings.xml) if it doesn’t already exist (i.e., this is the very first vote on any item). Assuming the XML file does in fact exist, the server script has to search through the XML code and find the rating based upon the item ID. It then has to update the rating by calculating a new average and incrementing the number of votes.

The URL used to issue a new rating involves passing along the item ID and rating as parameters to the server script. However, unlike other Ajax requests you’ve seen throughout the book, this request is issued as a POST instead of a GET because the intent of the request is to alter information on the server. In a POST request, the URL is simply the name of the server script, while the parameters (item ID and rating) are passed as a separate parameter to the ajaxSendRequest() function in the Ajax Toolkit in the file ajaxkit.js. I realize I’m drifting into some details that you may not be all that interested in knowing, given that the focus here is code reuse, but I want you to understand enough about the inner workings of the rater component to potentially be able to modify it if need be.

Getting back to the rating submission request, the response from the server includes a newly calculated rating average and the total number of votes that have been cast. Although XML could certainly be used to format this return data, it just isn’t necessary. So the data is returned as a colon-delimited pair, like this:

4.5:2

This is the data returned after issuing the last vote made on the first quote in the XML data you just saw. If you look back at that data, you’ll see the following code:

<rating>
  <id>twain</id>
  <avg>4.5</avg>
  <votes>2</votes>
</rating>

So, the server response to a rating vote that has been cast is just a matter of returning a couple pieces of data that have just been written to the XML file: the <avg> and <votes> content.

Putting Together the Rater Application

In addition to several files that are part of the Ajax Toolkit included on the CD-ROM accompanying the book, the Rater application consists of a single Web page and a helper Web page that isn’t strictly required:

  • HTML Web page (rater.html)

  • Cookie Reset Web page (cookiereset.html)

The Rater application is contained within the Rater Web page (rater.html), whereas the Cookie Reset Web page (cookiereset.html) is a separate helper page for clearing the rating cookies for testing purposes. The next couple sections examine these files in more detail.

The Rater Web Page

The Rater Web page makes use of five individual ratings that are each applied to a text quotation that appears on the page just before each rating. The idea is that you are able to read a quotation and then immediately rate it. Figure 12.4 shows the Rater Web page with several rating votes already made but a few items remaining in an unrated state.

Figure 12.4. The Rater Web page displays several quotations along with a five-star rating component for each one.

If you pay close attention to the figure, you’ll see that the rating for the Muhammad Ali quotation appears with larger stars and text. This demonstrates how the rating component supports two different sizes. In practical terms, the difference between the small and large versions of the rating component simply involves the usage of the different CSS style classes for the ratings.

The actual code for the Rater Web page is admittedly on the long side, so brace yourself. Just keep in mind that the main reason for the long code listing is because the page is using five different ratings, each of which requires several lines of code. Here’s abbreviated code for the body of the Rater page, which is where all of the interesting stuff takes place:

<body onload="initRatings()">
  <div id="ajaxState" style="display:none"></div>
  <br />
  <div>A banker is a fellow who lends you his umbrella when the
    sun is shining, but wants it back the minute it begins to
    rain.<br />
    - Mark Twain</div>
  <div>
    <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
    <span class="inline-rating">
      <ul class="star-rating small-star">
        <li id="twain-rating" class="current-rating"
style="width:0%">Rating</li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 1);"
          class="one-star">1</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 2);"
          class="two-stars">2</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 3);"
          class="three-stars">3</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 4);"
          class="four-stars">4</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 5);"
          class="five-stars">5</a></li>
     </ul>
   </span>
   <span id="twain-info" class="rating-info-small">
     (click to vote)</span>
  </div>
  <hr />
  <div>Learn from yesterday, live for today, hope for tomorrow.
   The important thing is not to stop questioning.<br />
   - Albert Einstein</div>
  <div>
    <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
    <span class="inline-rating">
      <ul class="star-rating small-star">
        <li id="einstein-rating" class="current-rating"
          style="width:0%">Rating</li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 1);"
          class="one-star">1</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 2);"
          class="two-stars">2</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 3);"
          class="three-stars">3</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 4);"
          class="four-stars">4</a></li>
        <li><a href="javascript:setRating('einstein', 5);"
          class="five-stars">5</a></li>
      </ul>
    </span>
    <span id="einstein-info" class="rating-info-small">
      (click to vote)</span>
  </div>
  ...
</body>

The key to this code is the careful usage of CSS style classes for the ratings. These classes are defined in the ratingkit.css style sheet and are responsible for the animated hover effect when you drag the mouse over the rating stars. This CSS approach to implementing a five-star rating was originally created by Rogie King of Komodo Media, which is located on the Web at http://www.komodomedia.com/. The code works so well that I used it as the basis for the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit.

Each rating in the Rater Web page is created as an unordered list where each star in the rating is an item in the list. The twist is that the list items are displayed horizontally instead of vertically, which is what causes the stars to appear horizontally. This may seem like a strange way to use a list, and it is, but it ends up working quite well for ratings. The trick is then to apply the appropriate styles to the list so that the hover effect works as intended.

The first step is to create a span element that houses the label for the rating, which is just static text:

<span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>

Of course, even the static text must have the appropriate CSS style class applied to it (rating-label-small). If you want a larger label for the larger-sized rating component, use rating-label instead of rating-label-small.

The next step is to create another span element that serves as the outer shell for the rating component:

<span class="inline-rating">

Within this span element you then add the unordered list container element, ul, like this:

<ul class="star-rating small-star">

This ul element is styled for a small rating component, meaning that it will use the smaller star images found in the star_sm.gif image file. If you want to use the larger rating component instead, just leave off the small-star class so that the code looks like this:

<ul class="star-rating">

Up to now the rating component is entirely generic with no code that is specific to an item. That’s about to change with the addition of the first list item, which is a special item that controls how many of the stars are visible:

    <li id="twain-rating" class="current-rating"
style="width:0%">Rating</li>

Now you see the first evidence of an item-specific rating, as the item ID twain is used to identify the rating via its id attribute (twain-rating).

Next up are the stars themselves, which are all coded as li list elements:

   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 1);"
     class="one-star">1</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 2);"
     class="two-stars">2</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 3);"
     class="three-stars">3</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 4);"
     class="four-stars">4</a></li>
   <li><a href="javascript:setRating('twain', 5);"
     class="five-stars">5</a></li>

Each line of code here represents one of the five stars in a five-star rating. Not only does each star serve as a visual indication of a rating, but it also acts as a trigger for issuing a rating vote. This is evident in the fact that the setRating() function is called in response to each link within the list items. The setRating() function accepts as its first parameter the item ID for the rating, which in this case is twain, followed by the rating to be set. So the second parameter to the setRating() function is the number of stars to be set for the rating.

You’re now ready to close up the main part of the rating component, like this:

  </ul>
</span>

The only remaining code is one last span element for the helper text that appears to the right of the rating:

<span id="twain-info" class="rating-info-small">
  (click to vote)</span>

Notice that this code uses the rating-info-small CSS style class, which corresponds to the small rating component. If you’re creating a large rating component, change this style to rating-info.

This code also uses the item ID (twain) in the formation of the id attribute for the element (twain-info). Keep in mind that the purpose of this element is to instruct the user on how to cast a vote (prior to voting) and to display the total number of votes (after voting). A third role that the helper text plays is to display a status message while a vote is being stored on the server. This last role is very similar to the “loading” images you’ve seen in other examples, except in this case “loading” text is used instead.

That completes the breakdown of an individual rating component within the Rater Web page. If you look back at the rating code used throughout the page, you’ll see five distinct chunks of code that exactly follow the structure you just examined.

The only loose end remaining in the Rater application is the call to the initRatings() function, which must be made in the onload event handler for the page’s body. This is the function that initially sets the values for all of the ratings on the page. Without this function, the benefit of writing the ratings to a file on the server would be lost.

The Cookie Reset Web Page

The Cookie Reset Web page (cookiereset.html) is a helper tool for the Rater application that is used to reset the cookies and allow you to vote again on any of the quotations on the Rater page. This is helpful for testing out the application and experimenting with different ratings without having to get access to a bunch of unique computers. Of course, you would never publish the Cookie Reset Web page so that it is accessible to other users; it is purely an internal tool to use for your own testing.

Figure 12.5 shows the Cookie Reset Web page in action, which admittedly isn’t very exciting at all.

Figure 12.5. The Cookie Reset Web page displays a quick message to let you know that the Rater cookies have been reset.

The lack of excitement in the Cookie Reset Web page stems from the fact that it doesn’t do anything beyond clearing the value of the five cookies associated with the five quotation ratings. Following is the complete HTML code for the page, cookiereset.html, which should illuminate the cookie-clearing process:

<html>
  <head>
    <title>Rater - Cookie Reset</title>
    <script type="text/javascript">
      document.cookie = "twain-rating=";
      document.cookie = "einstein-rating=";
      document.cookie = "ali-rating=";
      document.cookie = "paine-rating=";
      document.cookie = "shaw-rating=";
    </script>
  </head>

 <body>
   <p>The cookies are reset.</p>
 </body>
</html>

As you can see, the five cookies are cleared in the head of the page. Clearing a cookie simply involves setting the cookie to nothing. It’s extremely important to get the name of each cookie correct, and in this case I stuck with the naming convention established in the Rater application where the quotation ID is placed in front of the text–rating to arrive at the cookie name.

Keep in mind that the Cookie Reset Web page is really just a time-saving tool. Your Web browser likely includes an option for clearing all of the cookies it has amassed. However, some browsers only offer an option to clear all cookies. Even when there is an option to clear individual cookies, it can be extremely tedious hunting down the rating cookies when you consider that you likely have hundreds of cookies stored in your browser at any given time.

Note

You will likely end up using different item IDs when reusing the rating component in your own Web pages, in which case you’ll want to modify the Cookie Reset page so that it clears your new cookies.

Testing Rater

The Rater application makes for a fairly interesting application to test because it involves data that is stored away on the server, meaning that the application maintains a certain state. This is another way of saying that it remembers the votes that have been made. Also keep in mind that the application is designed to prevent more than one vote on a particular item from a given computer. So to test the application thoroughly on one computer, you’ll need to use the cookiereset.html Web page to reset the rating cookies created.

Figure 12.6 shows the Rater application with a clean slate (i.e., no votes have been cast for any of the ratings).

Figure 12.6. When you first launch the Rater application, all of the quotations are ready to be rated.

Because no rating votes have been cast at this point, no cookies have been set, and therefore all of the ratings are available for voting. Hovering the mouse over one of the ratings allows you to see the potential rating by highlighting the light stars with darker stars (see Figure 12.7).

Figure 12.7. Hovering over a rating with the mouse allows you to preview the rating you’re about to make.

To finalize a rating for a particular item, just click the number of stars for the rating. This will result in the rating vote being cast, in which case the helper text beside the stars changes to indicate that the vote is being cast. Figure 12.8 shows this effect on the first rating component on the page, which corresponds to a quotation by Mark Twain.

Figure 12.8. Upon a rating vote being issued, the helper text next to the five-star rating changes to indicate that the vote is being cast.

Once the vote is cast, the average rating for the item appears in the form of gold stars. This is significant because you aren’t allowed to see the average rating until after casting your own vote. The total number of votes received for the item is also displayed in the helper text next to the stars. Figure 12.9 shows the Mark Twain quotation after receiving its first vote of four stars.

Figure 12.9. Upon a rating vote being successfully submitted, the rating component changes to reveal the average rating and the total number of votes.

In addition to demonstrating the smaller five-star rating component, the Rater application also shows how to use a larger version with larger star images and related text. The Muhammad Ali quotation uses the larger version of the component—Figure 12.10 shows a vote being cast using this component.

Figure 12.10. The rating component also supports a larger version that uses both a larger star image and larger text.

The real power of the Rater application and the rating component is many different people being able to rate items. Although the component is designed to prevent duplicate voting on the same machine through the use of a cookie, you can circumvent this by loading a special Web page that clears the rating cookies. If you open the cookiereset.html Web page in your browser, it will clear out all of the cookies for the Rater Web page, thereby allowing you to view the page as someone else would see it on his or her own computer.

Note

Make sure to refresh the Cookie Reset Web page if you end up using it more than once, and also refresh the Rater Web page after resetting the cookies. Otherwise, the cookie changes won’t be noticeable.

Figure 12.11 shows the Rater application with several votes made on the quotations. In fact, the figure shows all of the rating averages, which means that a vote has been cast on every quotation.

Figure 12.11. By resetting the cookies, you can vote again on items and bypass the limitation of one vote per computer.

The cool thing about the rating component is that it stores the ratings in a manner such that the XML file on the server doesn’t grow when additional votes are cast. So as many people as you want can vote on items without you having to worry about a rating data file growing out of control.

Giving Rater an Extreme Ajax Makeover

There are a variety of different ways to expand on the Rater application, but the most likely thing you’ll want to do first is apply the rating component to your own Web pages. I’m assuming you have some items in mind that you’d like to add the capability of rating on a five-star scale. These items can be images, music clips, videos, text, or anything else you dream up. The main thing is that you need to be able to add the rating component HTML code somewhere near the item on the page so that it can be easily accessed.

The first step in adding the rating component to a page of your own is to import the appropriate Ajax Toolkit files. The following code needs to be added to the head of the page that will contain the ratings:

<link rel="stylesheet" type="text/css" href="ratingkit.css" />
<script type="text/javascript" src="ajaxkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="domkit.js"> </script>
<script type="text/javascript" src="ratingkit.js"> </script>

This code imports the rating CSS style sheet along with several JavaScript files that are all required in order for the rating component to work. The next thing to do is to call the initRatings() function from the onload event handler in the <body> tag, like this:

<body onload="initRatings()">

This makes sure that all of the ratings on the page get initialized properly.

From here on, the code largely depends on how you want to structure the ratings. Let’s say you have lots of products in an online store that you’d like to rate. In this case, you’ll want to use an item ID for the ratings that somehow ties into an existing product ID. This is likely a number of some sort, along the lines of a retail UPC code or Amazon.com’s ASIN numbers, which the company uses to uniquely identify products. Let’s assume for simplicity that your products are simple integers that start at 1 and count up. So your products are numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, and so on.

You now have an item ID to uniquely identify the rating for each item, which is all you need to add the ratings to your page. Here’s the code for a small rating for the first product (item ID of 1):

<div>
  <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
  <span class="inline-rating">
    <ul class="star-rating small-star">
      <li id="1-rating" class="current-rating"
        style="width:0%">Rating</li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 1);"
        class="one-star">1</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 2);"
        class="two-stars">2</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 3);"
        class="three-stars">3</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 4);"
        class="four-stars">4</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('1', 5);"
        class="five-stars">5</a></li>
    </ul>
  </span>
  <span id="1-info" class="rating-info-small">
    (click to vote)</span>
</div>

Do you see the pattern in the code? The item ID appears in exactly seven places, and all of them are important. The first place the item ID appears is in the id attribute of the <li> list item tag, which is set to 1-rating. The next five appearances are in the calls to the setRating() function for each of the possible votes (one star through five stars). In this case, the value ‘1’ is passed into the function as its first parameter; this is the item ID. Finally, the item ID is referenced once more as 1-info in the id attribute of the <span> element near the end of the code. This is what ties the helper text for the rating component to the particular item.

All of these item ID references must be set properly for each individual rating in a page. Just to make sure you understand how this works, let’s fast-forward through the product catalog a bit until we arrive at the product with an item ID of 379. Here’s the code to add a rating component for this item:

<div>
  <span class="rating-label-small">Rating:</span>
  <span class="inline-rating">
    <ul class="star-rating small-star">
      <li id="379-rating" class="current-rating"
        style="width:0%">Rating</li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 1);"
        class="one-star">1</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 2);"
        class="two-stars">2</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 3);"
        class="three-stars">3</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 4);"
        class="four-stars">4</a></li>
      <li><a href="javascript:setRating('379', 5);"
        class="five-stars">5</a></li>
    </ul>
  </span>
  <span id="379-info" class="rating-info-small">
    (click to vote)</span>
</div>

Hopefully this code hammers home the placement of the item ID in the code for a rating. You can paste this chunk of code as many times as you want to create different ratings, as long as you are careful to change the item ID each time.

Note

One other quick rating makeover that is extremely easy to apply is changing out the star images with images of your own. The large and small star images are stored in the files star.gif and star_sm.gif, respectively. Each of these files contains three different stars that are required for the hover effect. The stars are arranged vertically in the GIF image, and are all the same size (25×25 for large and 10×10 for small). If you use images that are the same size and arrange them vertically in the same manner, you can easily change the entire look of the rating component—just copy your own star image file over star.gif and/or star_sm.gif.

Reusing the rating component in your own pages isn’t the only extreme makeover worth considering. Although the rater component works great as is, you may not be fully satisfied with the usage of an XML file for storing the rating data on the server. I opted for this approach primarily because I didn’t want to make the application any more complex than necessary, and adding a database into the mix is a fair amount of additional complexity. But it’s not a bad idea.

If you have some experience with MySQL or some other database, you might consider reworking the ratingkit.php server script so that it reads and writes rating data using a database instead of an XML file. Rather than get into the actual coding details of an implementation in MySQL, I’ll just paint you a general picture of how this can be done.

In short, the following steps are required to change the rating component in the Ajax Toolkit so that it uses a MySQL database for rating storage and retrieval instead of an XML file:

  1. Create a database table named rating with the following fields: id, avg, and votes. These fields map one-to-one with the tags of the same name in the ratings.xml file.

  2. Change ratingkit.php so that it establishes a database connection instead of opening an XML file.

  3. Change ratingkit.php so that it reads all of the rows from the rating table via a database query instead of reading in the ratings.xml file.

  4. Change ratingkit.php so that all of the code that iterates through the ratings in the XML data structure instead iterates through the rows returned from the database query.

  5. Change ratingkit.php so that it modifies the appropriate row in the rating table to store a rating instead of writing out the XML file.

I realize this list of steps is incredibly generalized and requires a fair amount of MySQL experience to carry out. But in all honesty, that’s kind of the point—I’d rather see you stick with the XML file solution if you don’t have the MySQL skills to pull this off. Trust me, this is a very basic database challenge to anyone with database skills. If you’re short on database skills, as many of us are, just roll with the XML version knowing that it should meet your needs just fine.

Summary

Although you may not always like the feedback you get, it’s hard to argue the benefits of allowing users the freedom to rate content on your site. Ratings provide one of the key features of any interactive online community, and help people feel more involved and more informed. The Ajax rating component you learned how to use in this chapter is flexible enough to support any number of individually rated items, as well as sports a highly intuitive user interface for submitting a rating in the range of one to five stars. And you don’t even have to be a database guru to start using ratings thanks to the fact that a simple XML file is used for storing ratings on the server. If you do happen to be a database guru, however, you may want to consider applying the extreme Ajax makeover ideas to incorporate the rating storage into your own database.

Part III. Appendices

Appendix A. The Past, Present, and Future of Ajax

Maybe you couldn’t care less where Ajax came from, but just in case you do, I thought a little history lesson might be worthwhile. By understanding a bit more about the origins of Ajax, you’ll hopefully be more capable of assessing the role it will play in the future of Web development. It’s a very exciting technology, but one that is in many ways still in an exploratory state. So where exactly did Ajax come from and where is it headed? Let’s find out.

The Road to Ajax

When you get right down to it, Ajax is really just a term used to describe a particular application of existing technologies. This particular application has been around for a few years, but not until February of 2005 was given a catchy name. The man responsible for the name is Jesse James Garrett, who needed a more efficient way to describe a Web solution to a client of his employer, Adaptive Path. The name Ajax hit Garrett one day in the shower, and it stuck. People began thinking that Ajax was actually an acronym (AJAX) that stood for Asynchronous JavaScript and XML, but this isn’t entirely the case. Although the name Ajax does in fact refer to a development approach involving asynchronous JavaScript and XML, the word “Ajax” isn’t strictly an acronym.

Surprisingly enough, the road leading up to Garrett’s catchy name can be traced back to Microsoft’s Remote Scripting technology, which was officially introduced in 1998 in Internet Explorer 4 and Netscape Navigator 4. A functional application that utilized Microsoft’s Remote Scripting technology was Outlook Web Access, which was certainly pioneering for its time. Taking a step back a few years earlier, the iframe (Internet Explorer) and layer (Netscape Navigator) elements supported the asynchronous loading of Web content as far back as 1996. The iframe and layer elements in combination with crafty JavaScript coding ushered in the notion of Dynamic HTML, or DHTML, which was popular for several years prior to the advent of Ajax techniques.

The true birth of Ajax took place in 2000 when Microsoft added the XMLHTTPRequest object to Internet Explorer 5. It still took a few years for the technology to mature to a point where it could be used consistently, and even then it took a groundbreaking application for everyone to truly see the power of Ajax.

The Ajax Boom

Major technological shifts rarely happen in the abstract. In the case of Ajax, it took a real-world example to demonstrate how compelling the technology could be. That example was and still is Google Maps. Released to the world in beta form in February of 2005, Google Maps boggled the minds of many Web developers who marveled at how the map could be dragged in real time. How could it be possible for that much data to be transferred so fast? As it turned out, it was a very clever usage of Ajax that allowed Google Maps to appear so responsive. In reality, the application attempts to anticipate your next move and preloads map data asynchronously.

Note

It’s worth noting that the social networking site Orkut was technically the first mainstream application of Ajax, but it had nowhere near the shock and awe of Google Maps. Orkut used (and continues to use) Ajax to allow users to rate their friends with a star rating system.

In my mind at least, Google Maps formally ushered in the Ajax era of Web applications. The stampede began, and now numerous mainstream applications have an Ajax feel that is unmistakable. Gmail, Flickr, and Netflix are but a few of the other mainstream applications that are now heavily injected with Ajax, never to return to the land of click, wait, reload. With the huge momentum behind the technology, we will no doubt see many more applications become Ajax-powered as time goes by.

What’s Next for Ajax?

In lieu of the rapid ascent of Ajax, it is worth putting things into perspective as we chart a path into the future. One thing for certain is that many of us in the tech industry still have not learned our lesson from the dot-com bubble bursting of the late 90s. The term “irrational exuberance” has been used to describe the mentality that lead to unchecked rises in tech stocks during that period. A big part of the problem had to do with people latching on to the next “big idea” as if it was going to change the world as we know it. Of course the Internet as a whole has changed the world a fair amount, but many of us have a tendency to hype each new advance as if it alone holds the promise of unimaginable progress, efficiency, riches, etc. Ajax is one of these new advances.

When observed with a bit more perspective, it becomes apparent that Ajax is but one advance in the evolution of the Web. I’m not attempting to understate the significance of the advance, but I am cautioning that we all shouldn’t get carried away in the excitement of the newness. Ajax indeed offers the promise of a more responsive and ultimately more usable Web, and that is worthy of some excitement. But at the same time, let’s not forget that it solves a very specific problem that isn’t necessarily an issue with every Web application. Ajax is not a magic bullet that you fire at every Web page you develop.

As we venture into the uncharted future of the Web plus Ajax, the degree to which we restrain the usage of Ajax may ultimately play a critical role in Ajax’s continued success. This has to do with a variety of reasons, one of which is the fact that Ajax can actually put more of a workload on servers despite the fact that less information is typically being transferred. Sure, you’ve eliminated an entire page of data being sent with each request, but you’ve opted to fire off a couple dozen requests instead of just one. Depending on the specific server configuration, multiple small requests can potentially present more of a bottleneck than a single large request.

All this is to say that you should approach future Web projects knowing that Ajax is an incredibly handy tool, but it’s ultimately still just a tool. The old saying that to a hammer every problem looks like a nail certainly applies here. Just because you have the knowledge and code to inject Ajax into everything you do doesn’t necessarily make it right. At the same time, even the smallest properly structured Ajax application can be incredibly compelling and dramatically improve the usability of the app. If we all use Ajax with a tiny bit of creative restraint, the Web of the future will certainly be a more enjoyable place to live and work.

Appendix B. A Quick and Dirty XMLHttpRequest Reference

Although the major premise of this book is to empower you to use Ajax without having to immerse yourself in the nitty-gritty details of asynchronous JavaScript coding, you may get motivated enough by the examples to dive into Ajax programming. If so, you will need to understand the XMLHttpRequest object inside and out—this object houses the primary functionality responsible for making Ajax work. This appendix provides you with a concise reference to the XMLHttpRequest object, along with a few helpful hints about how to best use its methods and properties. For a complete reference on all of the objects supported by the core JavaScript language, please refer to http://developer.mozilla.org/en/docs/Core_JavaScript_1.5_Reference.

Methods

The XMLHttpRequest object contains several methods used to establish a connection with a Web server and send and receive data. Although the modern usage of the object typically involves asynchronous communication, the object is perfectly capable of performing synchronous operations as well. It’s how you use the methods of the object that determines the nature of a particular request.

Following are the main methods supported by the XMLHttpRequest object:

  • open()

  • send()

  • abort()

  • overrideMimeType()

  • setRequestHeader()

  • getAllResponseHeaders()

  • getResponseHeader()

Of these methods, the first two are by far the most important and most commonly used. The next few sections explore these methods in more detail.

open()

open(method, url[, async[, userName[, password]]])

The open() method opens a server request and prepares it for impending communication. The two required parameters (method and url) specify the request method and URL of the server connection, respectively. The request method parameter can be either "GET" or "POST", depending on whether you are making a fairly simple data retrieval request ("GET") or needing to send a fair amount of data to the server as part of the request ("POST"). You can still use the GET method if you’re just sending along a few pieces of information, but for larger data-submission requests (greater than 512 bytes), POST is your best bet.

Tip

Another handy way to know when to use GET or POST as the request method for an Ajax request is to assess whether or not data will change on the server based upon the request. If the state of the server changes due to the request, then POST is the answer; otherwise, go with GET.

The url parameter may be specified as an absolute (full) or relative URL, but keep in mind that in either case the URL must match that of the JavaScript code generating the request. In other words, an Ajax connection can only make a request on the same server as the Ajax script code itself. This is a security limitation of the XMLHttpRequest object, but one that is designed to protect users from malicious Ajax coders.

The third parameter to the open() method, async, is an optional Boolean value that determines whether or not the request is handled asynchronously. The default value of true indicates an asynchronous (Ajax) request, whereas false indicates a synchronous request. In practice, this means that a synchronous request makes your script wait for a response before doing anything else, whereas an asynchronous request takes place in the background, only reporting back when it’s finished. In a few rare situations, a synchronous request might make sense, such as when waiting for absolutely critical data before allowing the user to proceed with a task, but in the vast majority of cases, you’ll want to go with an asynchronous request. Besides, asynchronicity is what makes Ajax so cool.

Note

Most browsers that support Ajax limit Ajax requests to HTTP requests (http: protocol), which means you won’t be able to test an Ajax script from your local hard drive using the file: protocol. Of course, you can always install a Web server locally, in which case you can use the http: protocol to open Ajax pages.

The last two parameters to open() are entirely optional, and are ultimately dependent upon whether your server requires a username and password in order to carry out the request.

Following is a simple example of creating an XMLHttpRequest object and calling the open() method to establish an asynchronous request:

var request = new XMLHttpRequest;
request.open("GET", "processSomeStuff.php", true);

Two aspects of the XMLHttpRequest object are closely tied to the open() method: the send() method and the onreadystatechange event handler. The send() method must be called after the open() method in order to actually carry out an Ajax request. The onreadystatechange event is assigned a callback method of your own that gets automatically called when the Ajax request completes. In other words, you must write your own function that will get called whenever an Ajax request finishes doing its job. This function is where you process the data returned from the completed Ajax request.

send()

send(content)

The send() method is responsible for actually sending a request to a server for processing. The connection must have already been prepared with a call to open() prior to send() being called. The single parameter to the send() method, content, is data to be sent to the server as part of the request. In many cases you will be strictly making a server request without sending any data, in which case it is perfectly acceptable to pass null into the send() method, like this:

request.send(null);

Passing null to the send() method is often done when using the "GET" request method. The "POST" method, on the other hand, is designed specifically for passing along data to the server, in which case you’ll want to include meaningful data in the content parameter.

Content passed into the send() method via the content parameter can be a string, an array of unsigned bytes, or an XML DOM object. In many cases you’ll just pass a string into the method, in which case it usually takes the familiar POST-style name/value pair, like in this example:

request.send("artist=phish&album=rift&track=weigh");

It’s important to note that for asynchronous requests you’ll need to set the onreadystatechange event handler before calling the send() method. Otherwise, your event handler callback method will never get called and the Ajax effect will be entirely lost. Following is an example of how this handler is set, which must take place prior to calling the send() method:

request.onreadystatechange = yourHandler;

Keep in mind that when the Ajax request finishes, a call is automatically made to the yourHandler() function so that you can do something useful with the data returned by the request.

abort()

abort()

The abort() method stops the server request. This method can be handy in a timeout situation when you elect to give up on a lengthy request. You can also call the abort() method to make sure that the request object is reset before reusing it to carry out another request. Following is an example of calling the abort() method to terminate an Ajax request:

request.abort();

overrideMimeType()

overrideMimeType(type)

The overrideMimeType() method provides you with a means of overruling the MIME type of the data returned from the server in response to a request. This can be handy in situations where you are expecting XML but for some reason can’t get the server to cooperate. For example, the server may be rejecting XML data because it isn’t perfectly well-formed, in which case the server is returning the data as raw text via the responseText property. If you call the overrideMimeType() method and specify "text/xml" as the MIME type, the server will attempt to return XML data in the responseXML property:

request.overrideMimeType("text/xml");

Note

The overrideMimeType() method should be called after you call the open() method but before calling the send() method.

setRequestHeader()

setRequestHeader(label, value)

The setRequestHeader() method is used to add a label/value pair to the request header. This method is not necessary for normal GET requests but is essential when making POST requests. For example, the following request header setting lets the server know that the data in a POST request is going to be in the form of an encoded URL:

request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "application/x-www-form-urlencoded");

XML is another popular data format for issuing a POST request. If you plan on passing XML data to the server in a POST request, then the following request header setting is necessary:

request.setRequestHeader("Content-Type",
  "text/xml");

The need to set the request header ultimately depends upon your specific request. If your request is a POST, you should always set it. Otherwise, in the case of a GET, you should be just fine without setting anything on the request header.

getAllResponseHeaders()

getAllResponseHeaders()

The getAllResponseHeaders() method returns all of the response headers associated with the response as a string containing a series of newline-separated label/value pairs. Of perhaps a bit more use is the getResponseHeader() method, which returns a single value for a particular header label.

getResponseHeader()

getResponseHeader(headerLabel)

The getResponseHeader() method accepts the label of a specific header and returns the string value of the header for that label. As an example, you can determine the content type of a request by getting the response header for the "Content-Type" label, like this:

var header = request.getResponseHeader("Content-Type");

In this example, the return value stored in the header variable might be something along the lines of text/xml or text/plain, depending upon the type of data returned by the request.

Properties

Methods are only half of the XMLHttpRequest equation. Several properties also come into play and are in fact quite important in successfully carrying out Ajax requests. Following are the most important properties supported by the XMLHttpRequest object:

  • readyState

  • status

  • statusText

  • responseText

  • responseXML

These properties are important because they tell you critical information about the status and state of Ajax requests, such as if a request is finished and successful, along with storing the data returned by Ajax requests. All of these properties are read-only, meaning that you can’t set their values. The first three properties relate to the state and status of a request, whereas the last two properties are used to access the actual data returned from a request. The next few sections dig deeper into how these properties work.

readyState

The readyState property reflects the current state of a request and is your primary means of keeping track of the progress of the request. The property steps through a series of values as a request is carried out, with the last of these values indicating that the request has completed. Following are the possible values for the readyState property, in order of occurrence:

  • 0—Uninitialized

  • 1—Loading

  • 2—Loaded

  • 3—Interactive

  • 4—Complete

Of these property values, the last one is the only one you will typically be concerned with because it indicates the completion of a request. However, a value of 4 for the readyState property isn’t the end of the story when it comes to detecting a successful Ajax request. You also need to consider the status property.

Note

The custom callback method assigned to the onreadystatechange event handler is called every time the value of the readyState property changes. This is why it is important to check for the readyState property equaling 4 in the callback method before taking action in response to a request—you need to confirm that the request has completed before doing anything.

status

The status property keeps up with the current HTTP server status, which can be one of many different values, including 404 for “Not Found” or 200 for “OK.” Practically speaking, all you’re really interested in from an Ajax perspective is a status setting of 200, which indicates that a request was carried out “OK.” Plenty of other codes could feasibly pop up, but you’re primarily interested in 200 because anything else means there was a problem.

You will typically check for the status property equaling 200 (OK) while also checking for the readyState property equaling 4 (Complete). If both properties check out, you know the request has completed successfully and you can begin processing the request data.

statusText

The statusText property contains a string message associated with the current status code. For example, the status text for the successful status code of 200 is "OK". You might consider displaying the status text for diagnostic or error-handling purposes should a request fail with a status code other than 200.

responseText

The responseText property is half of the property pair used to store the data returned from an Ajax request. I say it’s half of the property pair because another property, called responseXML, is capable of returning data in a format different from responseText. If you plan on receiving request data as raw text, responseText is the property you’ll want to use because it simply provides you with a string of text containing the data.

responseXML

The other half of the response data property pair is the responseXML property, which formats the return data from a request as a DOM-compatible XML document node object. Because the responseXML property provides you with a DOM node object, you can use all of the incredibly powerful and flexible DOM methods and properties to explore and manipulate the data. Although it is entirely possible and often useful to work with Ajax request data as raw text via the responseText property, it’s the structure of XML request data supplied by responseXML that gives Ajax much of its power.

It’s important to note that request data is only stored in the responseXML property when the response’s content type is set to the XML MIME type, text/xml. In many cases the XML MIME type is automatically set correctly based upon the recognition of XML data in the response. However, this is not always the case, and you may run into a situation where the responseXML property is coming up empty even though you know the response is receiving XML data. The solution is to explicitly set the MIME type of the request to "text/xml" via the overrideMimeType() method.

Events

The event that works all the magic behind the scenes in any Ajax application is onreadystatechange, which is triggered any time the ready state of a request changes. This event is typically assigned a custom event-handler method that takes action based upon a change in the ready state. It’s your job to check and make sure within this method that the request completed (readyState == 4) and that it proceeded without error (status == 200).

Appendix C. Using the Live Linux CD

A live CD is an entire computer operating system on a removable disc. The CD can be booted directly on most standard PCs, ignoring the contents of your hard drive, and run as a desktop, server, or other specialty kind of system. The Ajax Construction Kit live CD included with this book contains elements of all three. Using this live CD, you can run all the book’s examples live, follow along with the code as you read the book, and modify the code to reuse later.

Inside the Live CD

The live CD is a remastered Ubuntu Linux live CD that has been adapted to include the following components:

  • Ajax Construction Kit examples—. All the example applications described in this book are included on the live CD.

  • Web server—. The XAMPP server is included on the live CD. With one click, you can start the server to try out the Ajax examples on your local computer or from another browser over the network.

  • Tools—. The live CD also includes the Firefox Web browser (for displaying the examples) and Bluefish editor (for modifying the code). Because the CD is a full-featured, desktop live CD, other command-line tools and applications are also available to you.

  • Documentation—. Links in the top panel let you access useful Ajax, PHP, and XAMPP documentation, in case you need reference material as you work with the code.

Using the Ajax Construction Kit CD as a live CD means that, after rebooting your PC, all the software you need to display, use, and modify the Ajax projects is available to you without any special configuration. However, in cases where you don’t want to (or are not allowed to) reboot the PC you are working on, the live CD also offers other ways to work with the code.

On the CD we have included a version of the XAMPP Web server package that will easily install on Windows systems. Following the instructions for using that software and adding the code examples from the CD, you can run the Ajax projects from your Windows desktop. Everything you need is included on the CD. (You just need to do a bit more setup.)

Running the Live CD

With an average PC, containing at least 256MB of RAM and a Pentium III, 650MHz (or better) processor, the live CD should perform quite well. Here’s what you do:

  1. Insert the CD into a standard PC and reboot.

  2. Press Enter at the boot screen to select Start or Install.

  3. When the desktop appears after a few minutes (be patient), open the Start LAMPP icon to start the server.

  4. After the server is running, open the Start Projects icon to view and work with projects.

At this point you should see the Firefox Web browser open to the index page, from which you can run all the code examples in the book. The live CD’s Ubuntu desktop, along with the Ajax Construction Kit components, appears in Figure C.1.

Figure C.1. The live CD provides easy access to launching the XAMPP Web server and exploring the Ajax example applications.

If you want to start modifying the code examples, select the Bluefish icon in the top panel. The Bluefish editor (shown in the upper-right corner of Figure C.1) displays the Ajax code example files in the left column. You can change any of those files as you like. Then see the results live in your Firefox browser by reloading the page.

Besides the icon for the Bluefish editor, there are other useful icons in the top panel. Each of the small book icons in the panel represents a link to different support information. They include links to Ajax and related documentation and links to information on the XAMPP server.

The entire XAMPP server files are stored in the /opt/lampp folder. The code examples are stored in the /opt/lampp/htdocs folder. Any changes you make to the code or server settings that you want to keep must be saved before you shut down (which I’ll discuss later). Without saving those files, the next time you reboot, the entire contents of your live CD system are returned to their original state.

Setting Up Networking

Many of the Ajax projects require Internet connectivity to work properly. Most wired Ethernet cards in Linux will be automatically detected and configured. If a DHCP server is available on the line, you will most likely be connected automatically and have immediate access to the Internet.

Wireless connectivity can be more challenging in Linux. Because many wireless cards require proprietary firmware from the manufacturer that cannot be legally redistributed with Linux, those wireless cards may require some extra work to get going in Linux. Other wireless cards won’t work at all (although the number of completely unsupported cards has shrunk greatly in the past few years).

Whether wired or wireless, the first place to try setting up networking on the live CD is the Network Setup window. From the live CD desktop, select System, Administration, Networking. That window shows you immediately if your network interface hardware was detected and configured to run. You can go ahead and do any manual configuration of those interfaces (such as to set up a static IP address, if needed) that are shown as available.

If the Network Setup window tells you that your wireless card is not configured, open a Terminal window (from the Terminal icon in the top panel) to do a bit of detective work. Type the following command to try to determine the type of wireless card you are using:

$ lspci -v |grep -i wireless

You will likely see something like this after issuing the command:

02:02.0 Network controller: Intel Corporation PRO/Wireless
2200BG Network Connection (rev 05)

In this example, the wireless card is an Intel PRO/Wireless 2200BG card. With that information, go to your favorite search engine (presuming you have some machine to get you on the network or can temporarily hook up to a wired network) and search for the wireless card model and the word Linux (as in “Linux 2200BG”). This example turned up the ipw2200 driver project. I’ll continue with that example. First I check to see if the driver had been loaded:

$ lsmod |grep ipw2200

A match for this command reveals that the driver has indeed been loaded:

ipw2200     115652   0

If the driver had not been installed, I would have to download (or otherwise get the driver on the machine) then load it using the modprobe command (such as sudo modprobe ipw2200). Next, I have to get the firmware needed by the card. Drivers for many wireless cards are in the /lib/firmware folder. If the firmware you need is not there, download the firmware and add it to the /lib/firmware folder.

At this point, you should be able to configure your wireless card. Select your wireless card from the Network Setup window and select the Properties window. Set up your wireless settings (ESSID, password, and configuration type, such as static or DHCP). Restart your network interfaces by typing sudo /etc/init.d/networking restart from a Terminal window. You should now be able to select the Network Monitor icon in the top panel to check the activity of your wireless card.

Backing Up Your Data

As I mentioned before, any changes you make during a live CD session will just disappear when you reboot, unless you back up your data in some way. You can back up your Ajax Construction Kit data to a local hard disk, to USB flash drive, or over the network. Then, the next time you start the live CD, you can restore that data. Here’s an example of backing up data with a USB flash drive.
